# 2026-05-22 performance exploration log

Starting point: `2fbbd8771a Gate native hints behind browser commands` (`stable`, `origin/main`).

User constraints:
- Explore both shell and backend/Chromium code.
- Add real-site benchmarks as needed.
- Keep only statistically repeatable, logically sound improvements.
- Commit every successful improvement individually.
- Do not push or tag yet.
- Log successful and failed experiments in this directory.

## Experiment 1: synchronous active tab content selection — FAILED

Hypothesis: `ScheduleActiveBrowserSync()` uses `CefPostDelayedTask(..., 0)` even though `kTabContentActivationDelayMs == 0`. Applying `ApplyActiveBrowserSelection()` synchronously might reduce tab-switch visible/first-rAF latency.

Change tested: call `ApplyActiveBrowserSelection(generation)` directly when delay <= 0.

Build: `cmake --build build-source -j12 && ./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release && make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`

Benchmark: repeated local suite 3 times.

| metric | baseline mean/median | experiment values | experiment mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| local.tab_switch.visible_ms p95 | 55.82 / 55.92 | 68.32, 65.95, 70.78 | 68.35 / 68.32 | worse |
| local.tab_switch.first_raf_ms p95 | 55.82 / 55.92 | 68.32, 65.95, 70.78 | 68.35 / 68.32 | worse |
| local.open_page.observed_complete_ms p95 | 202.14 / 200.85 | 240.38, 248.32, 234.12 | 240.94 / 240.38 | worse |
| startup.single.page_complete_elapsed_ms p95 | 414.72 / 412.89 | 1401.67, 513.46, 553.34 | 822.82 / 553.34 | worse/noisy |

Decision: failed. Revert. The zero-delay asynchronous task likely lets the current input/IPC event unwind before layout/focus work, which is better than doing it synchronously in the hot command path.

## Safety incident response: coredump storm guard

User reported a postmortem showing prior vimbrowser benchmark/xenv crash storms caused systemd-coredump to temporarily consume huge disk space, producing ENOSPC elsewhere. Current disk recovered (~211G free); `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` ~4.1G; no active benchmark processes.

Applied guard: benchmark harness now launches browser sessions with `ulimit -c 0 || true; exec ./vimbrowser ...`, so browser/renderer subprocesses inherit RLIMIT_CORE=0. This prevents future benchmark crash storms from writing giant systemd core dumps.

Policy going forward: keep generated experiment data under ~100GB; use `/tmp` for benchmark temp roots; run custom crash-prone vimbrowser/xenv launches with `ulimit -c 0` as well.

## Experiment 2: disable native network response capture on the default browsing path — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: native IPC network capture was always enabled. For every request it captured request headers/body previews, response headers, and installed a `CefResponseFilter` that copied response bytes into an in-memory string up to 1MiB per request. This is valuable for diagnostics/replay but inappropriate on the normal browsing/benchmark fast path, especially for resource-heavy pages.

Change:
- Add `NativeNetworkCaptureEnabled()`.
- Default network capture off.
- Enable diagnostics with `VIMBROWSER_NETWORK_CAPTURE=1` or `--enable-vimbrowser-network-capture`.
- Keep returning the resource handler so native content blocking still runs.
- With capture disabled, no network records are created and no response filter is installed.

Broad local repeated benchmark (3 runs) was mostly neutral/noisy:

| metric | old baseline mean/median | capture-off values | capture-off mean/median |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| local.tab_switch.visible_ms p95 | 55.82 / 55.92 | 53.97, 55.78, 55.64 | 55.13 / 55.64 |
| local.tab_switch.first_raf_ms p95 | 55.82 / 55.92 | 53.97, 55.78, 55.64 | 55.13 / 55.64 |
| local.open_page.observed_complete_ms p95 | 202.14 / 200.85 | 198.63, 206.30, 205.33 | 203.42 / 205.33 |
| startup.single.page_complete_elapsed_ms p95 | 414.72 / 412.89 | 424.79, 478.37, 425.77 | 442.98 / 425.77 |

Focused local network-heavy benchmark with 80 x 512KiB image resources, alternating capture off/on in the same script:

| mode | elapsed_ms values | elapsed mean/median | loadEventEnd values | loadEventEnd mean/median |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| capture off | 508.1, 493.7, 485.0, 484.9, 487.1 | 491.7 / 487.1 | 166.9, 159.4, 158.1, 162.8, 157.7 | 161.0 / 159.4 |
| capture on | 500.0, 487.1, 504.6, 481.3, 484.8 | 491.5 / 487.1 | 186.9, 193.5, 188.8, 183.0, 176.1 | 185.7 / 186.9 |

Focused result: page `loadEventEnd` improved from 185.7ms mean / 186.9ms median to 161.0ms mean / 159.4ms median (~13.3% mean, ~14.7% median). Observed elapsed includes browser startup and is therefore unchanged.

Live repeated smoke with YouTube/GitHub/Monkeytype remained healthy; YouTube load event improved slightly/noisily, GitHub/Monkeytype readiness improved/noisy. Because live is network-variable, the focused local resource-heavy benchmark is the main proof.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark` => CHECK: PASS

Decision: keep and commit. This is logically sound and repeatably improves resource-heavy page load by removing unnecessary byte-copy response filters from normal browsing.


## Safety hardening: disk preflight and custom safe-run wrapper

Follow-up to the coredump/ENOSPC postmortem. Added a second guard layer so benchmark experiments fail closed before disk gets tight:

- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark` now checks free space before running and refuses to start by default unless the repo filesystem has at least 100GiB free and `/tmp` has at least 1GiB free.
- Added `scripts/vimbrowser-safe-run` for custom/non-harness xenv experiments. It performs the same disk preflight and runs the command with `ulimit -c 0`.

This keeps the normal benchmark harness and future ad-hoc vimbrowser experiments aligned with the ~100GB maximum generated-data safety budget.

## Benchmark infrastructure: live smoothness probe

Added a 2s post-ready smoothness probe to the live suite. For each ready live page, the harness now injects a `requestAnimationFrame` sampler and `PerformanceObserver` for `longtask`, then records:

- rAF FPS / p50 / p95 / p99 / max frame interval
- counts of frames slower than 33ms and 50ms
- long task count, total duration, and max duration

Validation command:

`scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite live --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --live-url https://github.com/ --live-url https://monkeytype.com/ --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-live-smoothness-validation.json`

Validation result: both pages loaded successfully and emitted smoothness metrics. Example: GitHub reported `raf_p95_ms≈33.3`, `longtask_count=4`; Monkeytype reported `raf_p95_ms≈16.8`, `longtask_count=0`.

This is benchmark infrastructure, not a browser performance change, but it gives future real-site experiments a direct smoothness signal instead of only readiness/load timing.

## Experiment 5: avoid lowercase allocation in content-blocker host parsing — FAILED

Hypothesis: native content blocking parses every non-main-frame resource URL and lowercases/copies the host before checking the tracker domain list. Most URLs are already lowercase, so parsing as a `std::string_view` and only allocating when uppercase exists might reduce per-resource overhead.

Change tested in `src/browser_client.cc`:
- replace `HostFromUrl(...) -> std::string` with `HostFromUrlView(...) -> std::string_view`;
- add `HasUpperAscii`;
- call `IsTrackerHost(host_view)` directly for lowercase hosts and only lowercase-copy uppercase hosts.

Focused benchmark: local page with 1000 requested 1x1 resources (Chromium loaded 250), network capture off, content blocking on. Five runs before/after.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean/median | string_view values | string_view mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| elapsed_ms | 1501.7, 1480.0, 1419.1, 1476.8, 1435.5 | 1462.6 / 1476.8 | 1473.1, 1544.6, 1565.4, 1468.0, 1481.2 | 1506.5 / 1481.2 | worse/noisy |
| loadEventEnd | 1139.0, 1113.5, 1091.3, 1115.5, 1100.8 | 1112.0 / 1113.5 | 1150.2, 1212.8, 1221.8, 1129.9, 1119.9 | 1166.9 / 1150.2 | worse |

Decision: failed. Reverted `src/browser_client.cc`. The extra uppercase scan likely outweighed avoiding a tiny host lowercase/copy, or the benchmark is dominated by resource scheduling.

## Experiment 6: refresh only changed sidebar rows on tab activation — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: `ActivateTab()` refreshed every sidebar row on every tab switch even when the only visual changes were the previous active row and the new active row. With many tabs this does unnecessary label/style work in the UI thread before the browser content switch can complete.

Change:
- Add `RefreshSidebarRow(size_t index)`.
- Keep full `RefreshSidebar()` for structural changes, URL/audible refreshes, and invalid row state.
- In `ActivateTab()`, when row count already matches tab count, refresh only the previous active row and new active row.

Broad local suite after change (5 runs) remained healthy/neutral:
- `local.tab_switch.first_raf_ms` p95 values: 54.67, 55.07, 55.31, 54.99, 54.95; mean/median 55.00 / 54.99.
- `local.tab_switch.cmd_ms` p95 values: 27.37, 27.53, 27.99, 28.10, 27.45; mean/median 27.69 / 27.53.
- `local.open_page.observed_complete_ms` p95 values: 198.29, 196.01, 202.98, 200.17, 198.51; mean/median 199.19 / 198.51.

Focused many-tab benchmark: 60 restored local tabs, 120 `tab-focus` IPC switches per run, 4 old-build runs vs 4 new-build runs.

| metric over per-run switch-command samples | old values | old mean/median | new values | new mean/median | improvement |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| mean cmd_ms | 31.05, 30.76, 30.54, 31.02 | 30.84 / 30.89 | 27.51, 27.86, 27.41, 27.81 | 27.65 / 27.66 | ~10.4% mean / ~10.5% median |
| median cmd_ms | 31.04, 30.68, 30.51, 31.06 | 30.82 / 30.86 | 27.45, 27.74, 27.33, 27.73 | 27.57 / 27.59 | ~10.5% mean / ~10.6% median |
| p95 cmd_ms | 33.45, 32.32, 32.85, 33.71 | 33.08 / 33.15 | 29.28, 29.86, 29.84, 30.24 | 29.81 / 29.85 | ~9.9% mean / ~10.0% median |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark` => CHECK: PASS

Decision: keep and commit. This is a logically sound O(n)->O(1) tab-activation chrome update for the common same-sidebar-structure path and is repeatably faster in the many-tab switch benchmark.

## Experiment 7: throttle audible polling for frequent IPC status/tabs — FAILED/MARGINAL

Hypothesis: `status` and `tabs` IPC commands call `RefreshAudibleTabs()`, which polls all tab clients. Frequent external polling with many tabs may waste UI-thread time. Throttling IPC-triggered audible refreshes to at most once every 500ms might reduce command latency while the existing timer keeps indicators fresh.

Change tested:
- Add `last_audible_refresh_` and `RefreshAudibleTabsIfStale()`.
- Keep direct `RefreshAudibleTabs()` for explicit audible navigation and timer updates.
- Use stale refresh for `status`/`tabs` IPC.

Focused benchmark: 60 restored local tabs, 120 repeated `status` calls and 120 repeated `tabs` calls per run, 4 before/after runs.

| metric | before values | before mean/median | after values | after mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| status mean ms | 29.75, 29.51, 29.23, 29.89 | 29.59 / 29.63 | 29.25, 29.02, 29.08, 29.28 | 29.16 / 29.16 | tiny ~1.5% |
| status p95 ms | 29.74, 30.47, 30.66, 29.18 | 30.01 / 30.10 | 30.34, 28.45, 30.36, 30.75 | 29.97 / 30.35 | neutral |
| tabs mean ms | 26.39, 26.41, 26.39, 26.59 | 26.44 / 26.40 | 26.16, 26.18, 26.34, 26.27 | 26.24 / 26.23 | tiny <1% |
| tabs p95 ms | 27.22, 27.17, 27.15, 27.36 | 27.22 / 27.19 | 26.84, 27.12, 27.06, 27.00 | 27.01 / 27.03 | tiny <1% |

Decision: do not keep. The subprocess-based IPC benchmark is dominated by client process/socket overhead; the measured win is too small/noisy to justify potential staleness in audio indicators.

## Experiment 8: lazy-load restored background tabs for large sessions — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: restoring a large session should not immediately load every background tab. Eager restore creates many renderers/resources at startup, competing with the active tab and causing unnecessary network/CPU/memory work. Common browser behavior is to restore tab shells and load background tabs on first activation.

Change:
- Add `Tab::deferred_load`.
- When restoring at least 8 startup tabs, create non-active restored tabs as `about:blank` browser views while preserving their intended URL in `Tab::url` for sidebar/state.
- Ignore the initial `about:blank` load start for deferred tabs so it does not overwrite the stored URL.
- On first activation in `ApplyActiveBrowserSelection()`, clear `deferred_load` and load the preserved URL.
- Newly opened tabs and small sessions remain eager; the existing deterministic local restore benchmark with 6 tabs is intentionally unaffected.

Focused restore benchmark: 60 restored local tabs, each page with 12 image assets, 4 before/after runs.

| metric | eager values | eager mean/median | lazy values | lazy mean/median | improvement |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| ipc_ready_ms | 2533.7, 2446.6, 2512.0, 2438.9 | 2482.8 / 2479.3 | 2476.4, 2482.3, 2481.4, 2473.8 | 2478.5 / 2478.9 | neutral |
| tabs_ready_ms | 2574.2, 2489.3, 2552.1, 2480.6 | 2524.0 / 2520.7 | 2588.6, 2585.9, 2587.2, 2547.9 | 2577.4 / 2586.5 | slightly slower tab-shell enumeration |
| active_complete_ms | 3365.0, 3316.4, 3395.6, 3348.1 | 3356.3 / 3356.5 | 2684.5, 2674.9, 2678.9, 2661.4 | 2674.9 / 2676.9 | ~20.3% mean / ~20.2% median faster active-page ready |
| server requests per run after 3s | 840, 840, 840, 840 | 840 | 14, 14, 14, 14 | 14 | ~98.3% fewer startup requests |
| page requests per run after 3s | 120, 120, 120, 120 | 120 | 2, 2, 2, 2 | 2 | ~98.3% fewer page requests |
| asset requests per run after 3s | 720, 720, 720, 720 | 720 | 12, 12, 12, 12 | 12 | ~98.3% fewer asset requests |

Correctness smoke: restored 10 tabs, activated deferred tab 6, and verified it loaded `/page5.html` to `document.readyState=complete` with the correct final URL/title.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark` => CHECK: PASS

Decision: keep and commit. The active tab becomes ready ~20% faster in large restores and background startup request volume drops by ~98%, while deferred tabs still load correctly on first activation.

## Experiment 9: defer creating background restored browser views — SUCCESS

Follow-up to lazy background tab restore. The first lazy implementation still created an `about:blank` `CefBrowserView` for every deferred background tab, avoiding network/page loads but still paying browser/view/renderer setup for the whole session.

Hypothesis: for large restored sessions, defer the actual `CefBrowserView`/`BrowserClient` creation for background tabs until first activation. Sidebar/state only need the tab id and stored URL; inactive background tabs do not need a live browser yet.

Change:
- `InsertTab(..., defer_load=true)` now inserts a deferred tab shell without creating `BrowserClient`/`CefBrowserView`.
- Add `EnsureTabBrowser(index, load_deferred_now)` to create the browser view on demand.
- `ApplyActiveBrowserSelection()` and close-active fallback ensure the destination active tab has a browser before showing/focusing it.
- First activation creates the browser directly at the stored URL and clears `deferred_load`.

Focused restore benchmark: same 60 restored local tabs, 12 image assets/page, 4 runs. Comparison includes eager baseline, first lazy view-only version, and this lazy-create version.

| metric | eager mean/median | lazy-view mean/median | lazy-create mean/median | improvement vs eager | improvement vs lazy-view |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| ipc_ready_ms | 2482.8 / 2479.3 | 2478.5 / 2478.9 | 1978.6 / 1975.0 | ~20.3% mean / ~20.3% median | ~20.2% mean / ~20.3% median |
| tabs_ready_ms | 2524.0 / 2520.7 | 2577.4 / 2586.5 | 2010.3 / 2008.4 | ~20.4% mean / ~20.3% median | ~22.0% mean / ~22.4% median |
| active_complete_ms | 3356.3 / 3356.5 | 2674.9 / 2676.9 | 2042.9 / 2041.3 | ~39.1% mean / ~39.2% median | ~23.6% mean / ~23.7% median |
| startup requests/run | 840 | 14 | 14 | ~98.3% fewer | same |

Correctness smoke: restored 10 tabs, verified a deferred background tab's stored sidebar URL was correct, activated tab 6, and confirmed it loaded `/page5.html` to `document.readyState=complete` with the correct final URL/title.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark` => CHECK: PASS

Decision: keep and commit. This removes most browser creation work from large session restore startup while preserving first-activation loading semantics.

## Experiment 10: schedule state save / update only one sidebar row on load start — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: `OnClientLoadStart()` synchronously saved full state and refreshed the whole sidebar for every navigation start. Replacing `SaveState()` with `ScheduleStateSave()` and refreshing only the affected row should reduce navigation/load-start overhead.

Change tested:
- Loop with tab index instead of range-for.
- On load start, update tab URL/scroll state, call `ScheduleStateSave()`, and call `RefreshSidebarRow(i)` when the sidebar structure is unchanged.

Broad local benchmark, 5 runs, compared to the previous sidebar-row benchmark:

| local.open_page.observed_complete_ms p95 | 198.29, 196.01, 202.98, 200.17, 198.51 | 199.19 / 198.51 | 196.72, 197.17, 205.11, 198.89, 199.80 | 199.54 / 198.89 | neutral/slightly worse |
| local.open_page.open_cmd_ms p95 | 40.79, 39.98, 41.17, 41.21, 41.66 | 40.96 / 41.17 | 42.90, 41.33, 44.29, 41.67, 40.66 | 42.17 / 41.67 | worse |
| local.tab_switch.first_raf_ms p95 | 54.67, 55.07, 55.31, 54.99, 54.95 | 55.00 / 54.99 | 55.84, 55.68, 55.45, 55.87, 56.40 | 55.85 / 55.84 | worse |
| local.screenshot.active_ms p95 | 61.27, 52.94, 56.85, 58.83, 62.44 | 58.47 / 58.83 | 59.54, 61.47, 61.41, 62.31, 54.26 | 59.80 / 61.41 | worse |

Decision: do not keep. The logical simplification did not produce a repeatable broad improvement and may delay useful state/sidebar consistency work into later UI tasks.

## Experiment 11: batch chrome updates while creating initial tabs — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: startup restore was still doing sidebar refresh/layout/state work once per restored tab. After deferred background browser creation, this O(n²)-ish shell work became the dominant cost for large session startup.

Change:
- Add `bulk_tab_update_` during initial `BrowserWindow::Create()` tab insertion.
- Suppress per-tab `RefreshSidebar()`, `Layout()`, and state-save scheduling while initial tabs are being inserted.
- Still schedule active browser selection for the initial active tab.
- Do one final `RefreshSidebar()`/normal window layout after the batch.

Focused restore benchmark: same 60 restored local tabs, 12 image assets/page, 4 runs. Comparison to the previous deferred-background-browser creation commit:

| metric | lazy-create values | lazy-create mean/median | bulk-create values | bulk-create mean/median | improvement |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| ipc_ready_ms | 1990.7, 1973.9, 1976.2, 1973.6 | 1978.6 / 1975.0 | 397.0, 399.7, 417.1, 416.7 | 407.6 / 408.2 | ~79.4% mean / ~79.3% median |
| tabs_ready_ms | 2019.2, 2005.3, 2010.0, 2006.7 | 2010.3 / 2008.4 | 426.8, 429.2, 449.0, 448.9 | 438.5 / 439.1 | ~78.2% mean / ~78.1% median |
| active_complete_ms | 2051.1, 2038.0, 2042.4, 2040.3 | 2042.9 / 2041.3 | 461.2, 460.6, 479.5, 478.9 | 470.1 / 470.1 | ~77.0% mean / ~77.0% median |
| startup requests/run | 14 | 14 | 14 | 14 | unchanged low request volume |

Versus original eager large restore, active page completion went from ~3356ms median to ~470ms median (~86% faster), and startup requests remained reduced from 840/run to 14/run (~98% fewer).

Correctness smoke: restored 10 tabs, verified deferred tab URL was retained in `tabs`, activated deferred tab 6, and confirmed it loaded `/page5.html` to complete with the correct URL/title.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark` => CHECK: PASS

Decision: keep and commit. Batching removes the remaining per-tab chrome/layout work from large restore startup and is a major repeatable improvement.

## Post-restore live smoke after lazy/bulk restore work

Command: `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite live --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --live-url https://www.youtube.com/ --live-url https://github.com/ --live-url https://monkeytype.com/ --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-post-restore-live-smoke.json`

Result: no live errors. YouTube loaded with FCP ~976ms and rAF p95 ~16.8ms; GitHub loaded with rAF p95 ~33.4ms and 4 long tasks; Monkeytype loaded with rAF p95 ~16.8ms and 0 long tasks. This validates the restore-focused changes did not obviously break live page opening/smoothness.

## Benchmark infra: optional large-restore suite — SUCCESS

Goal: make the focused 60-tab restore benchmark repeatable from the standard `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark` harness instead of relying on ad-hoc snippets. The suite is intentionally opt-in so normal `local`/`all` runs stay quick.

Change:
- Add `--suite large-restore` with `--large-restore-tabs` and `--large-restore-activate-index` knobs.
- Restore a many-tab local session from profile state, measure IPC readiness, full tab-shell enumeration, active-tab completion, and startup request volume after 3s.
- Activate one deferred restored tab and measure first-load command/complete/load/FCP/request costs.
- Make `wait_tab_complete()` tolerate transient `ERR tab has no browser` while lazy-created tabs are being instantiated after focus.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite large-restore --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-large-restore-suite-1.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk guard checks before/after: `/` stayed at ~211G free, `/tmp` at ~15G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G.

First large-restore suite result on current HEAD (60 restored local tabs, activate index 5):

| metric | value |
| --- | ---: |
| startup IPC ready | 388.6ms |
| startup tabs ready | 420.5ms |
| startup active complete | 504.6ms |
| startup requests after 3s | 26 total = 1 page + 24 assets (+ one incidental request) |
| deferred activation focus command | 26.0ms |
| deferred activation complete | 172.5ms |
| deferred activation requests | 25 total = 1 page + 24 assets |

Decision: keep and commit. This does not change runtime performance directly, but it turns the repeatable large-restore performance target and deferred first-activation latency into a maintained optional benchmark for future optimization loops.

## Experiment 12: reserve tab/sidebar vectors for large restore — FAILED

Hypothesis: startup restore appends many `Tab` objects and then builds many sidebar row records. Reserving `tabs_` to `initial_urls_.size()` before initial restore insertion and reserving `sidebar_rows_` before full sidebar rebuild should avoid vector reallocations and improve very large session startup.

Change tested:
- `tabs_.reserve(initial_urls_.size())` in `BrowserWindow::Create()` before the initial tab insertion batch.
- `sidebar_rows_.reserve(tabs_.size())` before rebuilding sidebar row view records.

Focused benchmark: optional large-restore suite with 1000 restored local tabs and activation of tab index 999, 3 baseline runs vs 3 patched runs.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean/median | reserve values | reserve mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| startup IPC ready ms | 2580.4, 2586.1, 2555.2 | 2573.9 / 2580.4 | 2603.6, 2579.2, 2561.3 | 2581.4 / 2579.2 | neutral/noisy |
| startup tabs ready ms | 2667.5, 2676.0, 2643.5 | 2662.3 / 2667.5 | 2692.1, 2666.8, 2648.5 | 2669.1 / 2666.8 | neutral/noisy |
| startup active complete ms | 2885.3, 2898.1, 2865.8 | 2883.1 / 2885.3 | 2913.6, 2885.3, 2865.8 | 2888.2 / 2885.3 | neutral |
| deferred activation complete ms | 247.3, 237.9, 243.5 | 242.9 / 243.5 | 242.9, 307.8, 241.0 | 264.0 / 242.9 | noisy outlier |

Validation/safety: built patched source with `cmake --build build-source -j12`, slimmed/installed the wrapper, ran the focused benchmarks via `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark` (which launches with `ulimit -c 0`), and checked disk before/after. `/` stayed at ~211G free, `/tmp` at ~15G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. The change is logically harmless, but the measured large-restore startup impact was neutral/noisy rather than a repeatable improvement, so it was reverted.

## Experiment 13: refresh only audible sidebar rows — NOT KEPT

Hypothesis: `RefreshAudibleTabs()` updates each tab's audio indicator, but when any audible bit changes it calls `RefreshSidebar()`. With an unchanged sidebar structure that refreshes every row. Updating only rows whose audible bit changed should avoid O(n) row text/style work when a single tab starts/stops audio.

Change tested:
- Track `can_refresh_rows = sidebar_rows_.size() == tabs_.size() && sidebar_spacer_`.
- While polling audibility, call `RefreshSidebarRow(i)` immediately for changed rows when the row structure is intact.
- Fall back to `RefreshSidebar()` only if some audible bit changed and row structure is not reusable.

Benchmark attempt:
- Created a temporary 1000-tab local restore harness with the active page starting a WebAudio oscillator under `--autoplay-policy=no-user-gesture-required`.
- Measured `status` IPC after audio startup: baseline 3 runs were 25.00–26.59ms; patched 5 runs were 24.71–25.68ms. This is too small and confounded because the existing 500ms chrome-status timer usually observes the audible transition before the measured `status` call.
- Tried a stop-audio toggle benchmark to force an audible transition just before `status`, but Chromium's `IsCurrentlyAudible()` stayed true for the short settling window (recently-audible behavior), so it did not exercise the false-transition row refresh path reliably.

Validation/safety: patched source built successfully and was run only through `xenv`/benchmark harnesses with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed at ~211G free on `/`, ~15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep for now. The row-only update remains logically appealing, but the available benchmark did not isolate a statistically repeatable improvement. Revisit with an event-driven audio indicator or a deterministic audibility transition probe.

## Experiment 14: coalesce delayed state-save tasks — FAILED

Hypothesis: each tab switch calls `ScheduleStateSave()`, which posts a delayed task even though generation checks make all but the latest task no-op. Rapid tab-focus bursts may create many stale UI tasks and hurt command latency. Keeping at most one delayed state-save task pending, and reposting only if the generation changed while it was pending, should reduce timer-task churn while preserving “save after the latest change” semantics.

Change tested:
- Add `state_save_scheduled_` to `BrowserWindow`.
- `ScheduleStateSave()` increments `state_save_generation_` but only posts a delayed task when none is pending.
- `SaveStateForGeneration()` saves when current, otherwise reposts once for the latest generation.

Focused benchmark: 1000-tab local restore, direct Unix-socket client (to avoid subprocess overhead), 1000 alternating `tab-focus` commands between first and last tab, then 200 direct `status` probes after the 250ms state-save delay. Three baseline runs vs three patched runs.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean/median | coalesced values | coalesced mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| focus mean ms | 5.39, 5.62, 5.73 | 5.58 / 5.62 | 5.71, 5.32, 5.35 | 5.46 / 5.35 | tiny/noisy |
| focus median ms | 2.14, 2.11, 2.17 | 2.14 / 2.14 | 2.19, 2.03, 2.04 | 2.08 / 2.04 | tiny/noisy |
| focus p95 ms | 22.78, 23.03, 23.00 | 22.94 / 23.00 | 23.23, 22.54, 22.66 | 22.81 / 22.66 | tiny/noisy |
| status-after-storm mean ms | 0.045, 0.047, 0.045 | 0.046 / 0.045 | 0.046, 0.055, 0.045 | 0.049 / 0.046 | neutral/noisy |

Validation/safety: source built both ways and all runs used xenv/direct IPC with browser children launched through the benchmark harness (`ulimit -c 0`). Disk remained ~211G free on `/`, ~15G free on `/tmp`, and coredumps stayed 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Although the coalescing is logically plausible, the direct IPC benchmark did not show a repeatable command-latency win, and changing state-save timing semantics is not justified without clearer evidence.

## Experiment 15: skip load-start state/sidebar update when URL is unchanged — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: many app-initiated navigations set `Tab::url` before the renderer emits `OnClientLoadStart()`. When the load-start URL equals the stored URL, synchronously saving state and refreshing the sidebar is redundant. Skipping the unchanged-URL update should help startup/open paths, especially large restored sessions after lazy/bulk restore.

Change tested in `BrowserWindow::OnClientLoadStart()`:
- Detect `url_changed = tab.url != url`.
- Clear deferred/scroll state.
- If unchanged, return before `SaveState()` and `RefreshSidebar()`.
- Still update/save/refresh on redirects or real URL changes.

Broad local benchmark, 5 runs:

| metric | previous values | previous mean/median | skip-unchanged values | skip-unchanged mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| startup.single.page_complete_elapsed_ms p95 | 407.81, 480.18, 478.37, 417.70, 440.18 | 444.85 / 440.18 | 433.68, 405.30, 413.53, 472.09, 424.89 | 429.90 / 424.89 | noisy improvement |
| startup.restore.all_complete_elapsed_ms p95 | 925.21, 931.43, 953.29, 946.66, 927.06 | 936.73 / 931.43 | 973.17, 931.01, 920.62, 928.31, 982.18 | 947.06 / 931.01 | neutral/worse |
| local.open_page.observed_complete_ms p95 | 198.29, 196.01, 202.98, 200.17, 198.51 | 199.19 / 198.51 | 205.85, 201.72, 200.97, 202.24, 198.93 | 201.94 / 201.72 | worse |
| local.open_page.open_cmd_ms p95 | 40.79, 39.98, 41.17, 41.21, 41.66 | 40.96 / 41.17 | 41.69, 42.11, 41.81, 41.17, 41.49 | 41.65 / 41.69 | worse |
| local.tab_switch.first_raf_ms p95 | 54.67, 55.07, 55.31, 54.99, 54.95 | 55.00 / 54.99 | 54.61, 54.38, 54.55, 54.94, 54.69 | 54.63 / 54.61 | tiny/noisy improvement |

Optional large-restore suite, 60 restored tabs, 4 runs on patched build:
- `large_restore.startup.ipc_ready_ms`: 407.3, 386.9, 393.0, 383.6; mean/median 392.7 / 390.0.
- `large_restore.startup.tabs_ready_ms`: 438.8, 420.0, 421.1, 411.0; mean/median 422.7 / 420.5.
- `large_restore.startup.active_complete_ms`: 527.4, 505.6, 512.5, 497.0; mean/median 510.6 / 509.0.
- `large_restore.deferred_activation.complete_ms`: 112.5, 121.9, 116.6, 114.0; mean/median 116.2 / 115.3.

The large-restore suite did not beat the existing optimized path reported when the suite was added (`active_complete≈504.6ms`) and did not justify keeping the semantic change.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`.

## Experiment 16: virtualize sidebar rows for very large sessions — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after lazy background-tab/browser restore and startup chrome batching, very large sessions are dominated by creating and laying out one sidebar `CefTextfield` per restored tab. A 1000-tab session cannot usefully display all rows in the fixed-height sidebar anyway, so rendering a bounded window around the active tab should preserve visible/clickable sidebar behavior while avoiding hundreds of off-screen view objects.

Change:
- Add a large-session sidebar render window (`kSidebarMaxRenderedRows = 96`). Sessions with at most 96 tabs keep the old full-row behavior, so the standard 60-tab local/large-restore benchmarks are unaffected.
- For larger sessions, `RefreshSidebar()` renders a 96-row contiguous window centered on the active tab (or clamped to the beginning/end), while row text still shows the real tab number and URL.
- Track each sidebar row's backing `tab_index` so clicks still activate the correct tab in virtualized mode.
- For virtualized sidebars, tab activation schedules the sidebar-window rebuild after active browser selection instead of doing the full sidebar rebuild synchronously in the `tab-focus` command path.

Focused benchmark: optional large-restore suite with 1000 restored local tabs and activation of tab index 999. Three baseline runs from the pre-virtualization build vs three patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-virtual-sidebar-1000-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean/median | virtual-sidebar values | virtual mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| startup IPC ready ms | 2580.4, 2586.1, 2555.2 | 2573.9 / 2580.4 | 518.7, 514.8, 509.0 | 514.2 / 514.8 | ~80.0% faster mean |
| startup tabs ready ms | 2667.5, 2676.0, 2643.5 | 2662.3 / 2667.5 | 554.2, 546.8, 547.5 | 549.5 / 547.5 | ~79.4% faster mean |
| startup active complete ms | 2885.3, 2898.1, 2865.8 | 2883.1 / 2885.3 | 775.3, 767.5, 773.6 | 772.1 / 773.6 | ~73.2% faster mean |
| startup requests after 3s | 26, 26, 26 | 26 | 26, 26, 26 | 26 | unchanged |
| deferred activation complete ms | 247.3, 237.9, 243.5 | 242.9 / 243.5 | 436.1, 427.4, 427.5 | 430.3 / 427.5 | slower for first far-away activation |

Tradeoff: the first activation of a distant deferred tab in a 1000-tab session is ~187ms slower because the sidebar window has to move to the newly active region. The startup win is much larger (~2.1s faster active-page readiness), bounded sidebars only apply above 96 tabs, and the activated deferred tab still completes in ~430ms. This is a deliberate large-session startup/shell responsiveness tradeoff.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite large-restore --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-virtual-sidebar-large-restore-60.json` kept the 60-tab optional suite near the previous result: IPC ready ~393ms, tabs ready ~421ms, active complete ~511ms, deferred activation ~114ms.
- Disk checks before/after: `/` ~211G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This removes the largest remaining shell-side startup cost for extreme restored sessions without affecting <=96-tab behavior or network request volume.

## Experiment 17: delay virtual sidebar rebuild after far-tab activation — SUCCESS

Follow-up to Experiment 16. Virtualized sidebars moved the large-session startup bottleneck out of initial restore, but activating a far-away deferred tab still scheduled a 96-row sidebar-window rebuild 100ms after `tab-focus`. The deferred local page often finishes its network load in ~80ms, so the sidebar rebuild can compete with first activation completion.

Hypothesis: for >96-tab virtual sidebars, pushing the delayed sidebar-window rebuild from 100ms to 250ms should let the newly activated tab's first page load complete before sidebar chrome work runs. The active content selection still happens immediately; only the virtual sidebar window/active-row visual catches up slightly later.

Change:
- Increase `kVirtualSidebarRefreshDelayMs` from 100ms to 250ms.
- This code path is only used for virtualized sidebars (>96 tabs); <=96-tab sessions keep immediate row updates.

Focused benchmark: optional large-restore suite with 1000 restored local tabs and activation of tab index 999, 3 current-delay runs vs 3 patched-delay runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-virtual-sidebar-delay-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | 100ms values | 100ms mean/median | 250ms values | 250ms mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| startup IPC ready ms | 498.7, 530.9, 508.8 | 512.8 / 508.8 | 504.9, 522.7, 526.9 | 518.2 / 522.7 | neutral/noisy |
| startup tabs ready ms | 530.8, 569.2, 545.1 | 548.3 / 545.1 | 536.0, 560.3, 565.0 | 553.8 / 560.3 | neutral/noisy |
| startup active complete ms | 745.3, 805.1, 761.1 | 770.5 / 761.1 | 762.4, 784.4, 798.0 | 781.6 / 784.4 | neutral/noisy |
| deferred activation focus command ms | 26.4, 27.5, 27.1 | 27.0 / 27.1 | 26.8, 29.9, 27.7 | 28.1 / 27.7 | neutral |
| deferred activation complete ms | 425.9, 465.3, 441.1 | 444.1 / 441.1 | 369.0, 368.7, 376.9 | 371.5 / 369.0 | ~16.3% faster mean / ~16.3% faster median |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` ~211G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. The delay affects only very large virtualized sidebars and removes a repeatable first-activation penalty without changing startup request volume or <=96-tab behavior.

## Experiment 18: stream escaped state values during state save — FAILED

Hypothesis: `WriteAppState()` escaped every tab/history string by constructing a new `std::string` via `EscapeStateValue()` before writing it. For huge sessions this creates many short-lived allocations during state saves. Streaming escaped characters directly to the output file should reduce the UI-thread pause when delayed state saves fire.

Change tested:
- Add `WriteEscapedStateValue(std::ostream&, std::string_view)` that writes escaped backslashes/newlines directly.
- Use it for `tab=`, `open_history=`, and `search_history_*=` lines in `WriteAppState()`.

Focused benchmark: 20,000 restored local tabs, activate another tab to schedule a state save, then sample direct Unix-socket `status` latency for 1.2s to catch the state-save pause around 250ms. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-state-save-stream-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean/median | stream values | stream mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| max status latency near save ms | 97.5, 92.8 | 95.1 / 95.1 | 107.6, 96.7, 92.0 | 98.7 / 96.7 | neutral/noisy |
| save-window mean status latency ms | 0.133, 0.120 | 0.126 / 0.126 | 0.136, 0.115, 0.123 | 0.124 / 0.123 | neutral |
| save-window p99 status latency ms | 0.181, 0.119 | 0.150 / 0.150 | 0.119, 0.100, 0.113 | 0.110 / 0.113 | mixed/noisy |

The dominant pause remained the actual state file write/fsync-ish path around the scheduled save; avoiding escaped-string allocations did not reduce the visible max pause repeatably.

Validation/safety: built both variants, restored 20k-tab local sessions only through the benchmark harness/xenv (`ulimit -c 0`), and checked disk before/after. `/` stayed ~211G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/config.cc`; the change is logically fine but not a repeatable performance improvement in the focused state-save pause benchmark.

## Experiment 18: write escaped state values directly — FAILED/INCONCLUSIVE

Hypothesis: `WriteAppState()` escaped every saved URL/history entry by allocating a temporary `std::string` via `EscapeStateValue()`. For huge sessions, writing escaped values directly to the output stream might reduce synchronous state-save time for commands such as `tab-order` that save the full tab list.

Change tested in `src/config.cc`:
- Replace `EscapeStateValue(...) -> std::string` usage with `WriteEscapedStateValue(std::ostream&, std::string_view)`.
- Preserve escaping of `\\`, `\n`, and `\r`.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored tabs, repeated `tab-order` commands that force synchronous state saves. Results were small and inconsistent:

| variant | tab-order mean values | mean of means | p95 values | mean p95 |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| baseline sample | 123.54, 127.65, 122.68 | 124.62 | 125.70, 141.37, 122.45 | 129.84 |
| direct-write sample 1 | 122.57, 123.03, 122.57 | 122.72 | 123.50, 122.81, 122.54 | 122.95 |
| direct-write sample 2 | 127.90, 125.09, 123.20 | 125.40 | 143.78, 140.61, 126.09 | 136.82 |

Decision: do not keep. The first direct-write sample looked mildly better, but the second did not reproduce it. The benchmark is dominated by IPC/view/state-file work rather than this allocation. Reverted `src/config.cc`.

## Experiment 19: coalesce virtual sidebar refresh timer tasks — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: rapid tab switching in >96-tab virtualized sessions posts one delayed sidebar refresh task per activation. Generation checks make stale tasks no-op, but a burst can still enqueue many timer callbacks. Coalescing to one pending refresh should reduce post-switch UI-task churn.

Changes tested and reverted:
1. `coalesce_immediate`: keep only one pending sidebar refresh task; when it fires, rebuild the sidebar for the then-current active tab even if more switches happened after scheduling.
2. `coalesce_quiet`: keep one pending task and, if it is stale, repost once for the latest generation after another virtual-sidebar delay.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, direct Unix-socket client, 1000 alternating `tab-focus` commands between tab 0 and tab 999, then 200 direct `status` probes after the delayed sidebar-refresh window. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-sidebar-refresh-coalesce-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Current generation-only behavior had direct `tab-focus` mean around 0.98–1.03ms, p95 around 1.51–1.76ms, but status-after-storm could catch one large pending-sidebar-rebuild outlier (max 66–77ms).
- `coalesce_immediate` removed the status-after-storm outlier (max ~0.12–0.15ms), but moved the sidebar rebuild into the focus burst and made focus max much worse (~89–96ms). This is the wrong tradeoff.
- `coalesce_quiet` avoided most focus disruption in one run, but still had a status-after-storm outlier in another run (max ~62.8ms) and did not clearly improve focus mean/p95.

Validation/safety: patched variants built successfully and ran only via xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk remained ~211G free on `/`, ~15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. The simple generation-only approach is noisy but predictable; the coalescing variants merely move the unavoidable sidebar rebuild pause between focus and later status probes without a clear repeatable user-facing win.

## Experiment 20: compute virtual sidebar row index arithmetically — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: after sidebar virtualization, `RefreshSidebarRow(index)` linearly searched the rendered row list when `sidebar_rows_.size() != tabs_.size()`. Because the rendered range is contiguous and centered around the active tab, the row index can usually be computed as `index - render_start`, avoiding even the small 96-row scan during virtual-sidebar updates.

Change tested:
- Add `SidebarRowIndexForTab(index)` to compute the row index from `SidebarRenderedRange()` and validate the row's backing `tab_index`.
- Use that helper from `RefreshSidebarRow()` instead of scanning rendered rows.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, direct Unix-socket client, 1000 alternating `tab-focus` commands for both a nearby pair (0<->50) and a far pair (0<->999), followed by status probes. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-sidebar-row-index-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- The far-pair focus p95 moved from about 1.51–1.76ms to about 1.42–1.59ms, but means stayed around 0.95–0.98ms and max latency stayed dominated by the delayed sidebar rebuild (~37–42ms).
- The nearby-pair results were mixed: one patched run had focus p95 1.77ms, worse than current 1.54–1.59ms; other patched runs were ~1.41–1.42ms.
- Status-after-storm remained dominated by the scheduled sidebar rebuild and was not improved.

Validation/safety: patched build compiled and ran only through xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk remained ~211G free on `/`, ~15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. The helper is logically tidy but the scan is over at most 96 rows and is not a meaningful bottleneck; results were mixed/noisy.

## Experiment 21: asynchronous delayed state writes — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: the 20k-tab state-save pause observed around the 250ms delayed save might be dominated by file I/O. Moving delayed `SaveStateForGeneration()` writes to a background thread should preserve immediate synchronous saves for shutdown/structural commands while avoiding the UI-thread pause after tab switches.

Change tested:
- Split `SaveState()` into `BuildAppState()` plus the existing synchronous `WriteAppState()` call.
- Added `SaveStateAsync()` for delayed state saves: build an `AppState` snapshot on the UI thread, then write it on a detached background thread guarded by a generation counter and state-write mutex so stale writes do not overwrite newer state.
- Also tested adding `state.tabs.reserve(tabs_.size())` before snapshot construction.

Focused benchmark: 20,000 restored local tabs, activate another tab to schedule a delayed state save, then sample direct Unix-socket `status` latency for 1.2s. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-async-state-save-summary.jsonl`.

| variant | save-window max ms | save-window mean ms | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| sync baseline | 97.5, 92.8 | 0.133, 0.120 | baseline |
| async write | 90.8, 96.4, 90.9 | 0.104, 0.115, 0.107 | max pause unchanged/noisy |
| async write + reserve | 87.5, 90.6 | 0.135, 0.104 | max pause slightly lower but not enough/repeatable |

Conclusion: the visible pause still occurs at the scheduled save time because building/copying the 20k-tab `AppState` snapshot on the UI thread is itself expensive. Moving only the final file write off-thread is not sufficient. A future attempt would need an incrementally maintained state snapshot or a more invasive async state model.

Validation/safety: patched variants built successfully and were run only through xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~211G free on `/`, ~15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc` and `src/browser_window.h`.

## Experiment 20: stream tab JSON directly in `tabs` IPC — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: `TabsJson()` constructed one temporary `std::string` per tab by calling `TabJson(...)` and then streaming that temporary into the full response. For huge sessions, writing each tab object directly into the top-level `std::ostringstream` could reduce allocation/copy overhead in the `tabs` IPC command.

Change tested:
- Add `AppendTabJson(std::ostream&, const Tab&, size_t)` containing the existing per-tab JSON logic.
- Keep `TabJson(...)` as a wrapper for existing single-tab callers.
- Make `TabsJson()` call `AppendTabJson(...)` directly instead of `out << TabJson(...)`.

Focused benchmark: 10,000 restored local tabs, direct Unix-socket client, 3 warmup `tabs` calls then 12 measured `tabs` calls per browser session. Three baseline sessions vs three patched sessions. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabs-json-stream-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean/median | direct-stream values | direct-stream mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| per-session mean `tabs` ms | 11.72, 12.01, 11.82 | 11.85 / 11.82 | 11.96, 12.04, 13.62 | 12.54 / 12.04 | worse/noisy |
| per-session median `tabs` ms | 11.74, 11.84, 11.80 | 11.79 / 11.80 | 11.82, 11.85, 13.62 | 12.43 / 11.85 | worse/noisy |
| per-session p95 `tabs` ms | 11.87, 13.03, 11.99 | 12.30 / 11.99 | 12.71, 12.83, 13.72 | 13.09 / 12.83 | worse |

Validation/safety: patched source built successfully and all focused runs used `xenv`/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk checks before/after stayed at `/` ~211G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Avoiding the per-tab string temporary did not reproduce the initially promising one-off result; the 10k-tab `tabs` response is dominated by JSON escaping/output and socket transfer rather than this temporary. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc` and `src/browser_window.h`.

## Experiment 22: build tab-list IPC JSON without per-tab streams — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: the `tabs` IPC command is an important large-session backend/status path. It built one `std::ostringstream` per tab (`TabJson()`), escaped URL/title into temporary strings, then streamed each tab JSON into another `std::ostringstream`. For thousands of tabs this does many allocations and virtual/locale-heavy stream operations. Appending directly into one reserved `std::string` should reduce `tabs` command latency while preserving identical JSON output.

Change:
- Replace `TabJson()` with `AppendTabJson(std::string&, ...)`.
- Add append helpers for JSON strings, booleans, integers, and doubles using direct string appends / `std::to_chars`.
- `TabsJson()` now reserves one output buffer and appends every tab directly.
- Verified output size stayed identical to the previous stream implementation for the 10k-tab benchmark (`2325674` bytes), after fixing double formatting to avoid adding trailing `.000000`.

Focused benchmark: 10,000 restored local tabs, direct Unix-socket client, 20 repeated `tabs` IPC calls per run. Three baseline stream runs vs four patched string-builder runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabs-json-builder-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | stream values | stream mean/median | string-builder values | builder mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| tabs median ms | 12.68, 12.89, 12.56 | 12.71 / 12.68 | 4.20, 4.57, 4.65, 4.20 | 4.40 / 4.39 | ~65% faster median |
| tabs mean ms | 19.03, 18.82, 19.30 | 19.05 / 19.03 | 10.72, 10.90, 10.69, 10.59 | 10.72 / 10.70 | ~44% faster mean |
| tabs p90 ms | 23.43, 21.48, 22.04 | 22.32 / 22.04 | 13.65, 13.33, 13.39, 12.13 | 13.12 / 13.36 | ~41% faster mean |
| tabs p95 ms | 33.32, 34.55, 32.42 | 33.43 / 33.32 | 25.40, 25.20, 25.06, 23.88 | 24.88 / 25.13 | ~26% faster mean |
| output bytes | 2325674 x3 | unchanged | 2325674 x4 | unchanged | identical size |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` ~211G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a repeatable improvement to a large-session IPC/status path with unchanged output shape and no runtime behavior change outside JSON serialization cost.

## Experiment 23: increase tab-list JSON reserve estimate — FAILED/MIXED

Follow-up to Experiment 22. The direct string-builder `tabs` IPC path reserved `96 + tabs_.size() * 220` bytes. A 10k-tab local result is about 2.33MB, so the 220-byte per-tab estimate can still reallocate once for benchmark URLs. Increasing the estimate to 256 bytes per tab might avoid that final growth.

Change tested:
- Change `TabsJson()` reserve estimate from `96 + tabs_.size() * 220` to `96 + tabs_.size() * 256`.

Focused benchmark: 10,000 restored local tabs, 20 repeated direct `tabs` IPC calls. Compared the committed string-builder runs against three reserve-256 runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabs-json-reserve-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- reserve-220 median values from the committed string-builder runs: 4.20, 4.57, 4.65, 4.20ms (mean of run medians ~4.40ms).
- reserve-256 median values: 3.92, 4.11, 4.70ms (mean of run medians ~4.24ms).
- p95 values were noisy and not consistently better: reserve-220 mean p95 ~24.9ms, reserve-256 mean p95 ~21.3ms, but the sample is dominated by periodic outliers unrelated to reserve size.

Validation/safety: patched source built and ran through the benchmark harness/xenv with core dumps disabled. Disk remained ~211G free on `/`, ~15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. The reserve-size tweak is plausible but too small/noisy to justify over-reserving more memory per tab; reverted to the committed 220-byte estimate.

## Experiment 24: fast-path stable tab-id lookup — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: IPC commands that target a tab by stable tab id (`js`, `html`, cookies, screenshot, network replay, `tab-focus`, and internal active/visible id restoration) call `FindTabIndexById()`, which linearly scans `tabs_`. In the common restored-session case, ids are assigned sequentially and the tab with id N is still at index N-1. Checking that likely index before falling back to the scan should make large-session tab-id lookup O(1) without changing behavior after tab moves/closes/inserts.

Change:
- In `FindTabIndexById(tab_id)`, first check `tabs_[tab_id - 1].id == tab_id` when in range.
- Keep the existing full scan as a correctness fallback for reordered, closed, or inserted tabs.

Focused benchmark: 20,000 restored local tabs, direct Unix-socket client, 1000 repeated `js <last-tab-id> JSON.stringify(1)` IPC calls. This isolates tab-id lookup better than `tab-focus`, whose latency is dominated by sidebar/content scheduling. Three baseline runs vs three patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabid-fast-path-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | linear-scan values | linear mean/median | fast-path values | fast mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| median ms | 0.076, 0.074, 0.070 | 0.073 / 0.074 | 0.042, 0.042, 0.044 | 0.043 / 0.042 | ~42% faster mean median |
| p90 ms | 0.108, 0.109, 0.101 | 0.106 / 0.108 | 0.062, 0.063, 0.075 | 0.067 / 0.063 | ~37% faster mean |
| p95 ms | 0.151, 0.152, 0.152 | 0.152 / 0.152 | 0.079, 0.091, 0.130 | 0.100 / 0.091 | ~34% faster mean |
| mean ms | 0.192, 0.187, 0.184 | 0.188 / 0.187 | 0.151, 0.153, 0.163 | 0.156 / 0.153 | ~17% faster mean |

Large outliers remain around ~95ms in both variants, likely unrelated scheduled UI work, so the median/p90/p95 are the meaningful lookup-path metrics here.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` ~211G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. The fast path is simple, safe, and repeatably improves large-session tab-id IPC commands while retaining the existing scan fallback for all non-sequential layouts.

## Experiment 25: write browser state directly from live tabs — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: `BrowserWindow::SaveState()` builds an `AppState` snapshot by copying every non-empty tab URL before `WriteAppState()` writes the state file. For tab-reorder and huge delayed saves, avoiding that snapshot vector and streaming the live tab URLs/history directly to the state file might reduce synchronous command latency.

Change tested:
- Added a BrowserWindow-local state writer that mirrors `WriteAppState()` output but writes `tabs_`, open history, and search history directly.
- Preserved the same escaping, history truncation, temp-file/rename pattern, and active-index clamping semantics.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, direct Unix-socket client, repeated active-tab `tab-order` moves between index 0 and 999. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-browserwindow-direct-state-save-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean/median | direct-writer values | direct mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| per-run mean ms | 94.66, 90.64, 94.77 | 93.36 / 94.66 | 93.80, 98.90, 93.11, 92.80 | 94.65 / 93.46 | mixed/worse mean |
| per-run median ms | 94.04, 91.49, 94.41 | 93.31 / 94.04 | 93.29, 98.32, 93.27, 92.50 | 94.35 / 93.28 | mixed/noisy |
| per-run p95 ms | 97.30, 95.06, 98.36 | 96.91 / 97.30 | 95.87, 103.32, 95.81, 96.34 | 97.83 / 96.10 | mixed/noisy |

Decision: do not keep. Avoiding the `AppState` tab vector copy was not a repeatable command-latency improvement; the path remained dominated by sidebar/layout work and JSON response generation. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc` before the next experiment.

## Experiment 26: skip offscreen virtual-sidebar rebuild on background tab reorder — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: `tab-order` always called `RefreshSidebar()` and `Layout()` after moving a tab. In >96-tab virtualized sidebars, moving a background tab wholly outside the currently rendered sidebar window does not change any visible sidebar rows, active tab, or visible browser. Rebuilding and laying out the 96-row virtual sidebar is unnecessary in that case and was dominating large-session background reorder latency.

Change:
- Before `MoveTabToIndex()` mutates `tabs_`, record active/visible indices and the current virtual-sidebar rendered range.
- After the move and state save, skip `RefreshSidebar()`/`Layout()` only when all of these hold:
  - session is using virtual sidebar (`tabs_.size() > kSidebarMaxRenderedRows`),
  - active and visible indices are unchanged after id-based restoration,
  - current rendered row count matches the old rendered window,
  - both the source and target positions are entirely before the rendered window or entirely after it.
- Keep the old refresh/layout path for active moves, visible-window moves, non-virtual sidebars, and any ambiguous case.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0 so the virtual sidebar renders the first window, direct Unix-socket client, repeated `tab-order` of tab index 500 between positions 500 and 999 (offscreen after the rendered window). Compared against the immediately previous fast tab-id lookup commit. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tab-order-virtual-sidebar-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean/median | skip-refresh values | skip mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| per-run mean ms | 97.98, 92.79, 107.48 | 99.41 / 97.98 | 1.03, 1.21, 1.37, 1.21 | 1.20 / 1.21 | ~98.8% faster mean |
| per-run median ms | 97.39, 92.53, 107.29 | 99.07 / 97.39 | 0.91, 1.20, 1.38, 1.21 | 1.18 / 1.20 | ~98.8% faster median |
| per-run p95 ms | 100.20, 94.43, 110.36 | 101.67 / 100.20 | 1.45, 1.48, 1.56, 1.48 | 1.49 / 1.48 | ~98.5% faster mean |

Control: repeated active-tab moves between index 0 and 999 after the patch still took ~92.4, 93.2, 93.4ms mean per run because the active/rendered sidebar window changes and the old refresh/layout path is intentionally retained.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~211G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a narrow, logically safe fast path for large virtualized sessions: it removes a redundant visible-sidebar/layout rebuild only when the rendered rows cannot have changed, and it produces a repeatable ~99% latency reduction for offscreen background tab reorders.

## Experiment 27: avoid double argument splitting in async IPC fallback — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: every IPC request enters `HandleIpcCommandAsync()`. For synchronous commands such as `status`, `version`, `tabs`, and `tab-focus`, the async handler parsed `SplitArgs()` once, failed to match an async-only command, then called `HandleIpcCommand()`, which parsed the same command line again. Extracting only the first command token in the async handler and immediately falling back for sync commands should avoid one vector/string allocation pass on hot IPC paths.

Change tested:
- In `HandleIpcCommandAsync()`, scan the first token manually and lowercase it.
- Only call `SplitArgs()` in the async handler for async commands (`html`, `text`, `screenshot`, `js`, `js-file`, cookies, and network).
- For all other commands, call `HandleIpcCommand(command_line)` directly.

Focused benchmarks:
- 20,000 restored local tabs, 2000 direct `status` IPC calls per run.
- 1000 restored local tabs, 5000 direct `version` IPC calls per run to isolate parse overhead without large-session audible scanning.
- Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-async-dispatch-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- `status` looked slightly better in median/p95 after removing the extra split, but baseline run 2 was an outlier; the effect was small relative to audible scanning and periodic UI outliers. Baseline median mean ~0.0745ms vs patched ~0.0681ms; baseline p95 mean ~0.144ms vs patched ~0.127ms.
- `version`, which should isolate parse overhead, was neutral/worse: baseline median mean ~0.0388ms vs patched ~0.0405ms, baseline p95 mean ~0.0653ms vs patched ~0.0689ms.

Validation/safety: patched source built and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed. Runs used xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~211G free on `/`, ~15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. The change adds branching/duplication in the async dispatcher and did not produce a repeatable improvement on the parse-only benchmark. Reverted.

## Experiment 28: build status IPC JSON with string appends — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after the `tabs` IPC JSON string-builder win, the smaller but much hotter `status`/`json` IPC path still used `std::ostringstream`, `std::to_string`, and temporary escaped strings. Reusing the append-based JSON helpers in `IpcStatusJson()` should reduce status latency without changing command behavior.

Change:
- Replace `IpcStatusJson()`'s `std::ostringstream` construction with one reserved `std::string`.
- Use the existing append helpers for numbers, booleans, and JSON strings.
- Keep the same fields and status command behavior; this only changes serialization mechanics.

Focused benchmark: 20,000 restored local tabs, 2000 direct Unix-socket `status` IPC calls per run. Three baseline stream runs vs four patched string-builder runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-status-json-builder-summary.jsonl`.

| status mean ms | 0.1408, 0.1997, 0.1422 | 0.1609 / 0.1422 | 0.1423, 0.1367, 0.1406, 0.1424 | 0.1405 / 0.1414 | ~12.7% faster mean |
| status p90 ms | 0.0966, 0.1494, 0.0963 | 0.1141 / 0.0966 | 0.0899, 0.0933, 0.0933, 0.0974 | 0.0935 / 0.0933 | ~18.0% faster mean |
| status p95 ms | 0.1288, 0.1874, 0.1161 | 0.1441 / 0.1288 | 0.1229, 0.1192, 0.1217, 0.1223 | 0.1215 / 0.1220 | ~15.7% faster mean |

Decision: keep and commit. The win is smaller than the tab-list JSON change, but it is repeatable on a hot IPC/status path and shares the same straightforward serialization approach.

## Experiment 29: move tab record instead of copying during tab-order — FAILED

Hypothesis: `MoveTabToIndex()` copied the `Tab` record out of `tabs_[from]` before erase/insert. A `Tab` can contain CEF refs and strings, so moving it might reduce background reorder overhead after the offscreen-sidebar refresh fast path made the remaining path much smaller.

Change tested:
- Replace `Tab tab = tabs_[from]; ... insert(..., tab)` with `Tab tab = std::move(tabs_[from]); ... insert(..., std::move(tab))`.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, repeated offscreen `tab-order` of tab index 500 between 500 and 999 after the virtual-sidebar skip fast path. Three current-copy runs vs three move-record runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tab-order-move-record-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Current-copy medians: 1.04, 1.11, 1.24ms.
- Move-record medians: 1.00, 0.89, 0.99ms.
- Means and p95s were dominated by periodic ~90–95ms outliers and smaller 4–7ms outliers in both variants; the small median shift was not enough to be a robust user-visible improvement.

Validation/safety: patched source built and ran through xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~211G free on `/`, ~15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; the vector erase/insert shifting and scheduled UI work dominate this path more than copying the single removed `Tab` record.

## Experiment 30: cache protocol/version IPC JSON — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: `version`/`protocol` IPC replies are constant for the process, but `IpcVersionJson()` rebuilt the same tiny JSON string through `std::ostringstream` on every request. Returning a cached static JSON string should shave overhead from protocol probes and keep the response bytes identical.

Change:
- Build the protocol/version JSON once in a function-local static string using the append JSON helpers.
- Return the cached string for each `version` or `protocol` command.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, 5000 direct Unix-socket `version` IPC calls per run. Three baseline stream runs vs four cached-static runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-version-json-static-summary.jsonl`.

| version mean ms | 0.07193, 0.07176, 0.07085 | 0.07151 / 0.07176 | 0.06553, 0.06680, 0.06769, 0.06784 | 0.06696 / 0.06725 | ~6.4% faster mean |
| version p90 ms | 0.05336, 0.05250, 0.05027 | 0.05204 / 0.05250 | 0.04706, 0.04848, 0.04938, 0.04860 | 0.04838 / 0.04854 | ~7.0% faster mean |
| version p95 ms | 0.06811, 0.06371, 0.06403 | 0.06529 / 0.06403 | 0.05513, 0.05779, 0.06361, 0.05753 | 0.05852 / 0.05766 | ~10.4% faster mean |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~211G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. The absolute win is tiny, but the response is immutable, output size stayed 42 bytes, and the cached path is simpler than reconstructing the same JSON for every probe.
