## Experiment 32: cache help IPC text — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: the `help` IPC reply is a fixed text literal, but returning the long string literal directly may still construct a new `std::string` response for every request. Storing the text in a function-local static string might reduce repeated help-query latency.

Change tested:
- Replace the direct concatenated string-literal return in the `help` command with a `static const std::string kHelp` and `return kHelp;`.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, 5000 direct Unix-socket `help` IPC calls per run. Three baseline literal runs vs four static-string runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-help-static-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Baseline median values: 0.0402, 0.0414, 0.0408ms.
- Static-string median values: 0.0412, 0.0439, 0.0409, 0.0374ms.
- Means and p95s were mixed/noisy; one static run had a worse mean due outliers. The tiny median movement is not a repeatable improvement.

Validation/safety: patched source built and ran through xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~211G free on `/`, ~15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted the source change; the existing help path is already cheap enough that a static string is not a meaningful performance win.

## Experiment 33: reuse virtual sidebar rows when rendered window changes — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: active tab reorders in very large sessions still rebuilt the virtual sidebar by removing and recreating all 96 rendered `CefTextfield` rows, then laying out the window. When the sidebar remains virtualized and the rendered row count is unchanged, the existing row widgets can be reused by updating their backing tab indices, text, and style in place. That should remove most of the remaining active `tab-order` latency without changing visible output.

Change:
- Make `RefreshSidebar()` return whether it changed the sidebar view hierarchy.
- For `tabs_.size() > kSidebarMaxRenderedRows` with an existing 96-row virtual sidebar, update the existing row widgets in place instead of removing/recreating them.
- Let `MoveActiveTab()` and active `MoveTabToIndex()` skip `Layout()` when `RefreshSidebar()` only refreshed existing rows.
- Keep full rebuild/layout behavior for first sidebar construction, row-count changes, non-virtual structural changes, and ambiguous cases.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, repeated direct Unix-socket `tab-order` of the active tab between index 0 and index 999. Three baseline rebuild runs vs four row-reuse runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-virtual-sidebar-row-reuse-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean/median | row-reuse values | row-reuse mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| active tab-order mean ms | 97.07, 97.81, 97.39 | 97.42 / 97.39 | 10.89, 10.94, 10.74, 10.61 | 10.80 / 10.81 | ~88.9% faster mean |
| active tab-order median ms | 91.62, 93.96, 92.81 | 92.80 / 92.81 | 10.30, 10.31, 10.37, 9.73 | 10.18 / 10.30 | ~89.0% faster mean median |
| active tab-order p95 ms | 102.48, 102.51, 97.82 | 100.94 / 102.48 | 16.27, 14.53, 14.79, 14.60 | 15.05 / 14.69 | ~85.1% faster mean |

Control: repeated offscreen background tab-order of tab id 501 between positions 500 and 999 still used the earlier skip-refresh path and stayed low-latency (medians 1.03ms and 1.16ms; small means 2.08ms and 1.86ms due 8-13ms outliers), so the new row-reuse path did not regress that already-optimized case.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after focused and broad runs: `/` stayed ~211G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G, results stayed ~2.2M.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a logically bounded virtual-sidebar fast path: it only reuses rows when the row count is unchanged and the row widgets already exist, while preserving full rebuilds for structural changes. It repeatably cuts active large-session reorder latency from ~97ms to ~11ms.

## Experiment 33: use std::rotate for tab-order vector moves — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: `MoveTabToIndex()` currently removes the source `Tab` with `erase()` and reinserts it at the target index. After virtual-sidebar row reuse reduced active reorder latency, the remaining vector movement might be measurable. `std::rotate()` can express the same order change through moves across the affected range and might be faster than erase/insert shifting.

Change tested:
- Replace the `Tab` copy + `tabs_.erase()` + `tabs_.insert()` sequence with `std::rotate()` for both forward and backward moves.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs after the virtual-sidebar row-reuse optimization. Repeated active tab-order between index 0 and 999, plus offscreen background reorder control between positions 500 and 999. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tab-order-rotate-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Active erase/insert row-reuse runs: means 10.89, 10.94, 10.74, 10.61ms; medians 10.30, 10.31, 10.37, 9.73ms.
- Active rotate runs: means 11.07, 10.83, 11.14ms; medians 9.99, 9.91, 10.30ms.
- Background control stayed low-latency in both variants and was dominated by small outliers rather than the move primitive.

Validation/safety: patched source built and ran through xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~211G free on `/`, ~15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. `std::rotate()` was roughly neutral: median shifted slightly down in some active runs, but means/p95s did not improve repeatably. Reverted to the simpler existing erase/insert code.

## Experiment 34: stop audible refresh after known client tabs — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: large restored sessions can have thousands of deferred tabs with no `BrowserClient`. `RefreshAudibleTabs()` still scanned every `Tab` on `status`/`tabs`/chrome tick, even though only tabs with clients can be audible. Tracking the number of tabs that currently have a client lets the scan stop once all client-backed tabs have been checked, preserving audible refresh semantics while avoiding a full deferred-tab walk.

Change:
- Add `tab_client_count_` to `BrowserWindow`.
- Increment it when `EnsureTabBrowser()` creates a client and when a popup tab is inserted with a client.
- Decrement it when `CloseTabBackend()` detaches a client.
- In `RefreshAudibleTabs()`, skip null-client tabs and break once `tab_client_count_` clients have been visited.

Focused benchmark: 20,000 restored local tabs, lazy background tabs (only active tab has a client), 2000 direct Unix-socket `status` IPC calls per run. Three baseline scans vs four patched scans. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-audible-client-count-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | scan-all values | scan-all mean/median | client-count values | client-count mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| status median ms | 0.0596, 0.0635, 0.0638 | 0.0623 / 0.0635 | 0.0431, 0.0443, 0.0425, 0.0455 | 0.0438 / 0.0437 | ~29.7% faster mean median |
| status mean ms | 0.0946, 0.0975, 0.0971 | 0.0964 / 0.0971 | 0.0785, 0.0788, 0.0729, 0.0803 | 0.0776 / 0.0787 | ~19.5% faster mean |
| status p90 ms | 0.0879, 0.0877, 0.0876 | 0.0878 / 0.0877 | 0.0738, 0.0761, 0.0726, 0.0743 | 0.0742 / 0.0741 | ~15.4% faster mean |
| status p95 ms | 0.1225, 0.1205, 0.1195 | 0.1208 / 0.1205 | 0.0987, 0.1050, 0.0972, 0.1006 | 0.1004 / 0.0997 | ~16.9% faster mean |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210-211G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is not the previously rejected audible IPC throttle: every refresh still checks all client-backed tabs immediately; it only avoids walking known clientless deferred tabs.

## Experiment 35: skip sidebar refresh after offscreen background tab close — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: in a large virtualized sidebar, closing a background tab after the rendered sidebar window does not change any visible sidebar rows, active tab, or visible browser. `CloseTabAtIndex()` still rebuilt/refreshed the sidebar and laid out the window after every close. The same offscreen-window reasoning used for background `tab-order` can skip that work for this narrow close case.

Change:
- Before closing, record the current active/visible indices and virtual sidebar rendered range.
- If the session is using virtual sidebars, the closed tab is not active, the closed index is after the rendered window, the rendered row count is unchanged, and active/visible indices remain unchanged after close, return after `SaveState()` without `RefreshSidebar()`/`Layout()`.
- Keep the original refresh/layout path for active closes, visible-window closes, closes before the rendered window, non-virtual sidebars, and all ambiguous cases.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, close the last offscreen tab 50 times through direct Unix-socket `tab-close <tabid>`. Two usable baseline runs (one launch failed before IPC ready and produced no data) vs four patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-offscreen-close-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | refresh values | refresh mean/median | skip values | skip mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| close median ms | 7.70, 7.58 | 7.64 / 7.64 | 0.358, 0.305, 0.351, 0.319 | 0.333 / 0.335 | ~95.6% faster mean median |
| close mean ms | 7.98, 8.01 | 7.99 / 7.99 | 0.595, 0.536, 0.595, 0.663 | 0.597 / 0.595 | ~92.5% faster mean |
| close p90 ms | 8.64, 8.51 | 8.58 / 8.58 | 0.799, 0.949, 0.927, 0.805 | 0.870 / 0.866 | ~89.9% faster mean |
| close p95 ms | 8.90, 8.95 | 8.93 / 8.93 | 0.850, 1.189, 1.066, 1.024 | 1.032 / 1.045 | ~88.4% faster mean |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210-211G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a narrow, logically safe virtual-sidebar fast path: it only skips chrome work when the closed tab is wholly after the rendered rows and cannot affect visible sidebar contents.

## Experiment 36: cache validated tab-id lookup result — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: Experiment 24 added an O(1) sequential tab-id fast path (`tabs_[tab_id - 1]`) but moved/reordered tabs still fall back to a full scan on every IPC command targeting that tab. Many automation flows repeatedly target the same tab id (`js`, `html`, `screenshot`, `tab-focus`, etc.). Caching the last successful id->index lookup, while validating that the cached index still contains the same id, should accelerate repeated lookups for moved tabs without weakening correctness after reorder/close/insert operations.

Change:
- Add `cached_tab_lookup_id_` and `cached_tab_lookup_index_` to `BrowserWindow`.
- `FindTabIndexById()` still tries the sequential id-to-index fast path first.
- If the sequential fast path misses, check the cached id/index and validate `tabs_[cached_index].id == tab_id`.
- After either sequential or full-scan success, update the cache.
- Keep the existing full scan fallback for cache misses/stale cache entries.

Focused benchmark: 20,000 restored local tabs, move tab id 1 from index 0 to index 19999, then run 1000 direct Unix-socket `js <moved-tab-id> JSON.stringify(1)` calls. Three baseline runs vs four cached-lookup runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabid-lookup-cache-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | moved linear-fallback values | linear mean/median | cached values | cached mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| median ms | 0.1908, 0.1771, 0.2005 | 0.1895 / 0.1908 | 0.1629, 0.1605, 0.1558, 0.1534 | 0.1582 / 0.1581 | ~16.5% faster mean median |
| mean ms | 0.2271, 0.2157, 0.2303 | 0.2244 / 0.2271 | 0.1987, 0.1986, 0.1958, 0.1900 | 0.1958 / 0.1972 | ~12.8% faster mean |
| p90 ms | 0.2561, 0.2342, 0.2367 | 0.2423 / 0.2367 | 0.2097, 0.2017, 0.2147, 0.2118 | 0.2095 / 0.2108 | ~13.5% faster mean |
| p95 ms | 0.3120, 0.2984, 0.2963 | 0.3022 / 0.2984 | 0.2512, 0.2412, 0.2759, 0.2543 | 0.2556 / 0.2527 | ~15.4% faster mean |

Sequential-id control: repeated lookup of the last sequential tab remained in the same range (cached run median mean ~0.0407ms vs prior sequential fast-path median mean ~0.0427ms), so the cache does not regress the common sequential case.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. The cache is tiny, validated before use, and improves repeated moved-tab IPC lookups while retaining both existing safe fallbacks.

## Experiment 37: skip layout after non-active virtual-sidebar row reuse close — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after the virtual-sidebar row-reuse work, closing a non-active tab inside the rendered virtual window updates existing sidebar row text/style in place and does not change the view hierarchy. `CloseTabAtIndex()` still called `Layout()` unconditionally after `RefreshSidebar()`, adding avoidable UI-thread work to non-active close/delete commands.

Change:
- Use the `RefreshSidebar()` hierarchy-changed boolean in `CloseTabAtIndex()`.
- After the existing offscreen-close fast path, call `Layout()` only when the closed tab was active (content visibility/backend changed) or the sidebar hierarchy actually changed.
- Keep the normal layout path for active closes and structural sidebar rebuilds; only non-active row-reuse closes skip layout.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, repeated direct-socket `tab-close` of rendered sidebar index 50 (50 closes per run). This forces row text/index updates within the visible virtual-sidebar window but does not close the active/content tab. Three baseline layout runs vs four skip-layout runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-rendered-close-skip-layout-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | layout values | layout mean/median | skip-layout values | skip mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| close mean ms | 9.74, 9.56, 9.72 | 9.68 / 9.72 | 7.23, 7.11, 7.21, 7.33 | 7.22 / 7.22 | ~25.4% faster mean |
| close median ms | 9.37, 9.35, 9.51 | 9.41 / 9.37 | 6.60, 6.74, 6.86, 6.80 | 6.75 / 6.77 | ~28.3% faster mean median |
| close p90 ms | 10.20, 10.21, 10.27 | 10.23 / 10.21 | 8.88, 8.59, 8.44, 8.87 | 8.70 / 8.73 | ~15.0% faster mean |
| close p95 ms | 10.90, 11.38, 10.48 | 10.92 / 10.90 | 9.26, 8.82, 8.58, 9.30 | 8.99 / 9.04 | ~17.7% faster mean |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after focused and broad runs: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. The change is logically bounded to non-active close paths where `RefreshSidebar()` reports no hierarchy change, and the focused rendered-close benchmark is repeatably faster.

## Experiment 38: move closing tab record before erase — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: `CloseTabAtIndex()` copies `tabs_[closing]` into a temporary `Tab` before detaching backend state and erasing it. A `Tab` contains strings and CEF refs, so moving the record could reduce close latency after sidebar/layout work had been reduced.

Change tested:
- Replace `Tab closing_tab = tabs_[closing];` with `Tab closing_tab = std::move(tabs_[closing]);` before `CloseTabBackend(closing_tab)`.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, repeated direct-socket `tab-close` of rendered sidebar index 50 after the skip-layout optimization. Four copy baseline runs vs three move-record runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-close-tab-move-record-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Copy baseline medians: 6.60, 6.74, 6.86, 6.80ms (mean median ~6.75ms).
- Move-record medians: 7.41, 6.70, 7.06ms (mean median ~7.06ms).
- Means were also mixed/slightly worse for move in two runs. The remaining cost is not dominated by copying the single removed `Tab` record.

Validation/safety: patched source built and ran through xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted to the existing copy before erase.

## Experiment 39: refresh active audible tab before client scan — FAILED

Hypothesis: after the client-count audible optimization, a large restored session with client-backed tabs far apart can still scan many clientless tabs before finding every client. Refreshing the active client first and returning immediately when it accounts for all clients (or scanning from the beginning with the active client already counted) might reduce `status`/chrome-tick audible refresh latency after activating a high-index deferred tab.

Change tested:
- Factor the audible update into a small `refresh_tab` lambda.
- Refresh the active tab first when it has a client.
- If the active tab is the only client-backed tab, refresh the sidebar if needed and return; otherwise scan remaining tabs, skipping the active index, until `tab_client_count_` clients have been checked.

Focused benchmark: 20,000 restored local tabs, activate the last tab to force a high-index client, then run 2000 direct Unix-socket `status` IPC calls. Three baseline runs vs four active-first runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-audible-active-first-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | in-order values | in-order mean/median | active-first values | active-first mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| status mean ms | 0.0479, 0.0467, 0.0407 | 0.0451 / 0.0467 | 0.0433, 0.0455, 0.0472, 0.0404 | 0.0441 / 0.0444 | neutral |
| status median ms | 0.0478, 0.0439, 0.0351 | 0.0422 / 0.0439 | 0.0422, 0.0425, 0.0430, 0.0373 | 0.0412 / 0.0423 | neutral/no repeatable win |
| status p95 ms | 0.0652, 0.0562, 0.0515 | 0.0576 / 0.0562 | 0.0557, 0.0585, 0.0624, 0.0515 | 0.0570 / 0.0571 | neutral |

Validation/safety:
- Patched source built with `cmake --build build-source -j12`, runtime was slimmed, and wrapper installed.
- Focused runs used xenv benchmark launch with `ulimit -c 0` inherited by vimbrowser.
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~15G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. The benchmark is essentially noise-level after the existing client-count optimization, and the active-first code is more complex than the current straight scan. Reverted the source change.

## Experiment 40: adjust active index directly after non-active close — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: when closing a non-active tab, `CloseTabAtIndex()` uses the active tab id and `FindTabIndexById()` to recover the active index after erasing the closed tab. For non-active closes, the new active index is derivable from the old index and closed index: decrement when closing before the active tab, otherwise leave it unchanged. Avoiding the id lookup could reduce close latency for high-index active tabs.

Change tested:
- Replace the non-active close `FindTabIndexById(active_id)` recovery with direct index adjustment: `--active_index_` if `closing < active_index_`, clamp if needed.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs with active index 999, repeatedly close rendered sidebar index 50 through direct Unix-socket `tab-close` (50 closes per run). Three baseline runs vs four direct-index runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-close-active-index-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Baseline medians: 8.78, 8.45, 8.65ms (mean median ~8.63ms).
- Direct-index medians: 8.68, 8.69, 8.61, 8.82ms (mean median ~8.70ms).
- Means/p90/p95 were neutral to slightly worse in the patched variant. The post-close sidebar row update/layout work dominates this path more than the active-id lookup.

Validation/safety: patched source built, `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed, and all focused runs used benchmark-launched/xenv browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted to the existing id-based recovery, which is more robust to future reorder semantics and not measurably slower here.

## Experiment 41: keep two validated tab-id lookup cache entries — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: Experiment 36 caches the last validated tab id -> index lookup, which helps repeated IPC commands targeting one moved tab. Automation can also alternate between two moved/reordered tabs; with a single-entry cache, every other lookup falls back to a full scan. Keeping a second validated cache entry should cover two-tab alternation while retaining correctness through the same `tabs_[cached_index].id == tab_id` validation.

Change:
- Add `second_cached_tab_lookup_id_` and `second_cached_tab_lookup_index_` to `BrowserWindow`.
- On sequential fast-path or scan success, shift the old primary cache entry into the secondary slot and store the new result as primary.
- On lookup, check the primary validated cache first, then the secondary validated cache, then fall back to the existing full scan.

Focused benchmark: 20,000 restored local tabs, move tab ids 1 and 2 to the end, create browsers for both, then alternate 1000 direct Unix-socket `js <tabid> JSON.stringify(1)` calls between those two moved tabs. Three one-entry-cache runs vs four two-entry-cache runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabid-two-entry-cache-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | one-entry values | one-entry mean/median | two-entry values | two-entry mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| median ms | 0.1902, 0.1943, 0.1944 | 0.1930 / 0.1943 | 0.1723, 0.1572, 0.1744, 0.1735 | 0.1694 / 0.1729 | ~12.2% faster mean median |
| mean ms | 0.2044, 0.2038, 0.2067 | 0.2050 / 0.2044 | 0.1853, 0.1719, 0.1872, 0.1845 | 0.1822 / 0.1849 | ~11.1% faster mean |
| p90 ms | 0.2199, 0.2167, 0.2216 | 0.2194 / 0.2199 | 0.1989, 0.1844, 0.1979, 0.1959 | 0.1943 / 0.1969 | ~11.4% faster mean |
| p95 ms | 0.2324, 0.2255, 0.2290 | 0.2290 / 0.2290 | 0.2087, 0.1972, 0.2061, 0.2047 | 0.2041 / 0.2054 | ~10.8% faster mean |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14-15G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. The extra cache entry is tiny, every cached hit is still validated against the live tab vector, and it repeatably improves a two-moved-tab IPC automation pattern without changing fallback semantics.

## Experiment 42: expand validated tab-id lookup cache to four entries — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: Experiment 41 kept two validated tab-id lookup cache entries, which helps automation alternating between two moved tabs. Automation can also cycle across several moved/reordered tabs. A tiny four-entry validated cache should cover a four-tab alternation pattern while preserving the same safety property: every cached hit is accepted only if the live tab vector still contains the requested id at the cached index.

Change:
- Replace the primary/secondary cached lookup fields with fixed four-entry `std::array`s.
- Check each cached id/index pair after the sequential `tabs_[tab_id - 1]` fast path and before the full scan.
- On sequential or full-scan success, shift existing entries and store the new validated result at the front.
- Keep the existing full scan fallback for misses or stale cache entries.

Focused benchmark: 20,000 restored local tabs, move tab ids 1–4 to the end, create browsers for them, then alternate 1000 direct Unix-socket `js <tabid> JSON.stringify(1)` calls across those four moved tabs. Three two-entry-cache runs vs four four-entry-cache runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabid-four-entry-cache-summary.jsonl`.

| four-tab mean ms | 0.2007, 0.1991, 0.2156 | 0.2051 / 0.2007 | 0.1832, 0.1865, 0.1902, 0.1888 | 0.1872 / 0.1876 | ~8.8% faster mean |
| four-tab p90 ms | 0.2124, 0.2148, 0.2349 | 0.2207 / 0.2148 | 0.1887, 0.2009, 0.2055, 0.1976 | 0.1982 / 0.1992 | ~10.2% faster mean |
| four-tab p95 ms | 0.2231, 0.2342, 0.2512 | 0.2362 / 0.2342 | 0.1976, 0.2138, 0.2420, 0.2058 | 0.2148 / 0.2098 | ~9.0% faster mean |

Two-tab control: the four-entry cache remained slightly faster/no worse on the earlier two-tab alternation pattern (median mean ~0.1683ms vs two-entry ~0.1694ms; mean ~0.1788ms vs ~0.1822ms).

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14-15G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. The cache remains tiny and fully validated, but now covers small multi-tab automation cycles without repeated large-session scans.

## Experiment 43: expand validated tab-id lookup cache to eight entries — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: after the four-entry validated tab-id lookup cache, expanding the fixed cache to eight entries might help automation cycling across more moved/reordered tabs. The cache still validates each hit against the live tab vector, so correctness risk remains low; the question is whether scanning eight cache entries adds too much overhead for smaller cycles.

Change tested:
- Increase `cached_tab_lookup_ids_` / `cached_tab_lookup_indexes_` from 4 entries to 8 entries.

Focused benchmark: 20,000 restored local tabs, move several low-id tabs to the end, create browsers for them, then alternate direct Unix-socket `js <tabid> JSON.stringify(1)` calls. Compared four-entry-cache runs to eight-entry-cache runs for both an eight-moved-tab cycle and a four-moved-tab control. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabid-eight-entry-cache-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Eight-tab cycle improved: four-entry median mean ~0.1903ms vs eight-entry ~0.1782ms; mean ~0.2109ms vs ~0.2005ms.
- Four-tab control regressed: four-entry median mean ~0.1727ms vs eight-entry ~0.1890ms; mean ~0.1872ms vs ~0.2105ms.
- Since the smaller 2–4 tab automation cycles are more likely than eight moved tabs, and four entries already cover the observed useful cases, the regression on the four-tab control is not worth the broader cache.

Validation/safety: patched source built, `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed, and all focused runs used xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14-15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted to the committed four-entry cache.

## Experiment 44: do not store sequential tab-id fast-path hits in lookup cache — FAILED/MARGINAL

Hypothesis: after the four-entry validated tab-id lookup cache, `FindTabIndexById()` still shifted/stored the cache on every sequential `tabs_[tab_id - 1]` fast-path hit. Sequential tab IDs do not need the moved-tab cache, so returning immediately could reduce common IPC lookup overhead and avoid polluting the moved-tab cache with ordinary sequential accesses.

Change tested:
- In the sequential fast path, return `likely_index` without calling `cache_store(likely_index)`.
- Keep validated cache lookup and cache population for non-sequential full-scan hits unchanged.

Focused benchmark: 20,000 restored local tabs, activate sequential tab id 20000, then run 1000 direct Unix-socket `js 20000 JSON.stringify(1)` calls per run. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-sequential-tabid-no-cache-store-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | four-entry baseline values | baseline mean/median | no-sequential-store values | no-store mean/median | result |

| sequential mean ms | 0.1753, 0.1779, 0.1741 | 0.1757 / 0.1753 | 0.1683, 0.1814, 0.1665, 0.1716 | 0.1719 / 0.1700 | tiny/noisy improvement |
| sequential p95 ms | 0.1944, 0.2038, 0.2019 | 0.2000 / 0.2019 | 0.1989, 0.2227, 0.1876, 0.1938 | 0.2008 / 0.1964 | neutral/noisy |

Four-moved-tab control with the patched build stayed in the same range as the committed four-entry-cache results (patched median mean ~0.1720ms, mean ~0.1850ms, p95 ~0.2009ms), so the change did not show an obvious moved-cache regression.

Validation/safety: patched source built and all focused runs used xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. The sequential-path improvement is too small and one patched run was worse than baseline; p95 was neutral. Reverted to the committed four-entry cache behavior.

## Experiment 45: avoid duplicate tab lookup in async HTML/JS IPC handlers — FAILED

Hypothesis: async `html`/`text`/`js` IPC dispatch first validated the tab id in `HandleIpcCommandAsync()`, then `HandleHtmlIpcCommand()` / `HandleJsIpcCommand()` called `BrowserForTabId()` and looked up the same tab id again. Passing the already-resolved `CefBrowser` into browser-specific helper functions could remove the second lookup from hot JS/HTML automation paths.

Change tested:
- Add `HandleHtmlIpcCommandForBrowser(...)` and `HandleJsIpcCommandForBrowser(...)` helpers containing the existing frame/source/eval logic.
- In async `html`/`text`, `js`, and `js-file` dispatch, resolve `BrowserForTabId()` once and call the browser-specific helper.
- Keep the public/internal tab-id entry points for other call sites.

Focused benchmark: 20,000 restored local tabs. For moved-tab coverage, move tab ids 1-4 to the end and cycle 1000 direct Unix-socket `js <tabid> JSON.stringify(1)` calls. For sequential control, activate sequential tab id 20000 and run 1000 direct Unix-socket `js 20000 JSON.stringify(1)` calls. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-async-single-browser-lookup-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Four-moved-tab cycle was essentially neutral/slightly noisy: committed four-entry cache mean/median references ~0.1872 / 0.1727ms; patched runs averaged ~0.1854 / 0.1717ms, with one median worse and p95 ~0.2083ms.
- Sequential control regressed: baseline sequential mean/median/p95 ~0.1757 / 0.1621 / 0.2000ms; patched sequential runs averaged ~0.1832 / 0.1694 / 0.2089ms.
- The extra helper dispatch / lambda path appears to offset the removed validated lookup, especially for the common sequential case where the second lookup is already O(1).

Validation/safety: patched source built, focused runs used xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled, and disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted the helper refactor; the moved-tab path was not repeatably faster and the sequential control was worse.

## Experiment 45: factor JS IPC handling through browser helper — FAILED

Hypothesis: `HandleIpcCommandAsync()` validates a `js` command's tab id before dispatch, then `HandleJsIpcCommand()` resolves the same tab id to a browser again. Factoring JS handling into a helper that accepts a `CefBrowser` might reduce repeated lookup overhead on hot automation paths.

Change tested:
- Add `HandleJsIpcCommandForBrowser(CefRefPtr<CefBrowser>, ...)` containing the frame/message-send work.
- Have `HandleJsIpcCommand()` resolve the browser and delegate to the helper.
- In the async `js`/`js-file` paths, resolve the browser before calling the helper.

Focused benchmark: 20,000 restored local tabs, active sequential tab id 20000, 1000 direct Unix-socket `js 20000 JSON.stringify(1)` commands per run. Three current runs vs three helper-path runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-js-browser-helper-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Current medians: 0.1525, 0.1462, 0.1476ms.
- Helper-path medians: 0.1483, 0.2102, 0.1581ms.
- Means/p95s were neutral to worse in the helper variant; one helper run was clearly worse.
- The tested patch did not remove enough real work to matter (and still needed async-path validation plus browser resolution for error handling), so this is not a useful direction as implemented.

Validation/safety: patched source built and focused runs used xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14-15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc` and `src/browser_window.h`.

## Experiment 46: add optional direct IPC benchmark suite — SUCCESS

Goal: recent work optimized backend IPC/status paths (`status`, `version`, `commands`, `tabs`, and tab-id lookup), but the maintained benchmark harness only had broad local/live suites and the optional large-restore suite. The ad-hoc IPC microbenchmarks used during experiments were useful because they bypassed `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` subprocess startup and measured the browser's Unix-socket command path directly. Add that focused coverage to `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark` so future IPC/backend changes have a repeatable maintained target.

Change:
- Add `BrowserSession.ipc_direct(command)` for raw Unix-socket IPC calls inside the benchmark harness.
- Add opt-in `--suite ipc` (not part of `local` or `all`) with knobs:
  - `--ipc-tabs` (default 1000)
  - `--ipc-iterations` (default 200)
  - `--ipc-moved-tabs` (default 4)
- The IPC suite restores a local multi-tab session, measures direct `version`, `commands`, `status`, and sequential `js` calls, then optionally moves several tabs and measures a moved-tab `js` cycle. It records response sizes as a guard against accidental output-shape changes.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 200 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-suite-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14-15G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G.

First maintained IPC-suite result on current HEAD (1000 restored local tabs, 200 iterations):

| metric | value |
| --- | ---: |
| `ipc.version_ms` median / p95 | 0.057ms / 0.443ms |
| `ipc.commands_ms` median / p95 | 0.058ms / 0.160ms |
| `ipc.status_ms` median / p95 | 0.047ms / 0.142ms |
| `ipc.js_sequential_ms` median / p95 | 0.179ms / 0.443ms |
| `ipc.js_moved_cycle_ms` median / p95 | 0.193ms / 0.330ms |
| response sizes | version 42B, commands 4089B, status 295B, js 41B |

Decision: keep and commit. This is benchmark infrastructure rather than a runtime speedup, but it turns the shell/backend IPC microbenchmarks used for recent successful changes into a repeatable, opt-in suite for future optimization loops.

## Experiment 47: activate inserted tabs before sidebar refresh — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: `InsertTab(..., activate=true)` inserted and created the new tab, then immediately refreshed/la[id] out the sidebar with the old active tab before calling `ActivateTab(insert_index)`, which updated active state and refreshed rows again. For active inserts (`open-tab`, clone, and active UI new-tab paths), setting the active index before the first sidebar refresh should avoid stale active-row chrome work and one extra activation row-update path.

Change:
- In `InsertTab()`, after inserting and creating the browser for an active, non-bulk tab insertion, set `active_index_ = insert_index` before refreshing the sidebar.
- Refresh/layout once with the new tab already active, then schedule state save and active browser sync directly.
- Keep existing behavior for bulk restore, non-active inserts, and deferred background inserts.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, 50 direct Unix-socket `open-tab <local-url>` commands per run. Three baseline runs vs four patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-active-insert-fast-path-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | old path values | old mean/median | active-before-refresh values | new mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| open-tab median ms | 29.70, 29.00, 31.03 | 29.91 / 29.70 | 27.54, 27.13, 27.18, 27.38 | 27.31 / 27.28 | ~8.7% faster mean median |
| open-tab mean ms | 30.01, 29.16, 31.37 | 30.18 / 30.01 | 27.99, 27.10, 27.42, 27.75 | 27.57 / 27.59 | ~8.7% faster mean |
| open-tab p90 ms | 33.11, 31.48, 34.68 | 33.09 / 33.11 | 31.70, 31.24, 30.26, 30.37 | 30.89 / 30.81 | ~6.6% faster mean |
| open-tab p95 ms | 33.38, 31.96, 37.08 | 34.14 / 33.38 | 32.48, 31.33, 30.93, 30.79 | 31.38 / 31.13 | ~8.1% faster mean |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14-15G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. The change is narrow to active non-bulk insertion and removes redundant stale-active sidebar work while preserving the scheduled active browser selection path.

## Experiment 48: gate close/undo lifecycle stderr logs — FAILED

Hypothesis: `CloseTabAtIndex()` and `UndoCloseTab()` wrote diagnostic lifecycle lines to `std::cerr` on every close/undo. In large-session automation loops this could add formatting and pipe/file I/O overhead, especially after previous close optimizations reduced the actual chrome work. Gating those logs behind an opt-in environment variable might reduce close latency while preserving diagnostics when explicitly enabled.

Change tested:
- Add `TabLifecycleLoggingEnabled()` reading `VIMBROWSER_TAB_LIFECYCLE_LOGS` once.
- Wrap the `close-tab` and `undo-close-tab` `std::cerr` diagnostics in that gate.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, close 80 descending offscreen tab ids through direct Unix-socket `tab-close` per run. Four baseline runs vs four gated-log runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-close-log-gate-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean/median | gated values | gated mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| close median ms | 0.329, 0.391, 0.315, 0.318 | 0.338 / 0.323 | 0.297, 0.337, 0.405, 0.327 | 0.341 / 0.332 | neutral/slightly worse |
| close mean ms | 0.808, 0.961, 0.821, 0.906 | 0.874 / 0.864 | 0.928, 0.889, 1.044, 0.923 | 0.946 / 0.925 | worse |
| close p90 ms | 1.006, 1.256, 0.904, 0.986 | 1.038 / 0.996 | 1.193, 0.899, 1.325, 0.902 | 1.080 / 1.048 | neutral/slightly worse |
| close p95 ms | 2.521, 2.615, 4.091, 2.253 | 2.870 / 2.568 | 2.548, 2.404, 2.931, 2.462 | 2.586 / 2.505 | noisy/tiny better |

Validation/safety: patched source built successfully and focused runs used benchmark-launched xenv browser processes with `ulimit -c 0`. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, and coredumps stayed at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. The focused close path was neutral to slightly worse on median/mean/p90; only p95 moved slightly in a noisy direction. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`.

## Experiment 49: include `tabs` in the maintained IPC benchmark suite — SUCCESS

Goal: Experiment 46 added a maintained direct IPC suite, but it covered `version`, `commands`, `status`, sequential `js`, and moved-tab `js` cycles while omitting the large `tabs` response path. Since several earlier backend wins targeted tab-list JSON construction and audible scanning triggered by `tabs`, the opt-in IPC suite should track `tabs` latency and response size alongside the other shell/backend commands.

Change:
- Add `("tabs", "tabs")` to the command list measured by `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc`.
- Keep it opt-in with the rest of the IPC suite so the broad local/all benchmarks remain unchanged.

Validation:
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 200 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-suite-tabs-metric-1000.json`
- Result file includes `ipc.tabs_ms` and `ipc.tabs_response_bytes`: median 0.437ms, p95 0.588ms, response size 228703B for the 1000-tab local session.
- Disk checks stayed at `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is benchmark coverage rather than a runtime speedup, but it makes the maintained direct IPC suite cover the important large tab-list path used by shell/status integrations.

## Experiment 48: skip layout after reusing sidebar rows on `open` — FAILED/MARGINAL

Hypothesis: the `open <tabid> <url>` IPC path changes a tab's URL and calls `RefreshSidebar(); Layout();` even when `RefreshSidebar()` only updates existing row text/style and reports no sidebar hierarchy change. Skipping `Layout()` when row widgets are reused might reduce existing-tab navigation command latency.

Change tested:
- Replace the unconditional `RefreshSidebar(); Layout();` in the `open` IPC handler with `if (RefreshSidebar()) Layout();`.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, 50 direct Unix-socket `open <active-tabid> <local-url>` commands per run. Three baseline runs vs four patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-open-existing-skip-layout-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Baseline medians: 10.78, 10.59, 9.95ms (mean median ~10.44ms).
- Patched medians: 10.63, 9.99, 9.92, 9.99ms (mean median ~10.13ms).
- Means and p95s moved only a few percent and were within run-to-run noise; the command returns a full `TabsJson()` payload (~229KB for 1000 tabs), which dominates this benchmark more than the layout call.

Validation/safety: patched source built, `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed, and focused runs used xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14-15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. The change is logically plausible, but the measured improvement was too small/noisy for a committed runtime change. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`.

## Experiment 49: move new tab record into tab vector — FAILED/MARGINAL

Hypothesis: `InsertTab()` creates a local `Tab` with id/url/deferred state and inserts it into `tabs_` by copy. Moving that local record into the vector should avoid copying the URL string on active `open-tab` and new-tab paths.

Change tested:
- Replace `tabs_.insert(..., tab)` with `tabs_.insert(..., std::move(tab))` in `InsertTab()`.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs after the active-insert fast path, 50 direct Unix-socket `open-tab <local-url>` commands per run. Four copy-baseline runs from Experiment 47 vs three move-insert runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-insert-tab-move-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Copy-baseline medians: 27.54, 27.13, 27.18, 27.38ms (mean median ~27.31ms).
- Move-insert medians: 27.08, 26.59, 26.70ms (mean median ~26.79ms).
- Means improved only ~1.5%; p90/p95 were effectively neutral/noisy. Browser creation, sidebar refresh, and page load scheduling dominate this path, not copying the small local `Tab` record.

Validation/safety: patched source built and focused runs used xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14-15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted to the existing copy insert because the measured improvement was too small/noisy to justify another runtime change.

## Experiment 50: buffer state-file contents before writing — FAILED/MARGINAL

Hypothesis: `WriteAppState()` streams many small `<<` writes to an `std::ofstream` when saving very large tab sessions. Building the state file in a reserved `std::string` and issuing one `file.write(...)` might reduce synchronous state-save overhead on commands that save state, such as large-session tab closes/reorders.

Change tested:
- In `src/config.cc`, replace direct streaming to `std::ofstream` with appending the same lines into a reserved `std::string`, then write that buffer to the temp state file once.
- Preserve the existing temp-file/rename pattern and escaping behavior.

Focused benchmark: restored local sessions, repeated direct Unix-socket background `tab-close` commands that force synchronous state saves. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-buffered-state-write-summary.jsonl`.

Observations on the 10,000-tab close benchmark:
- Current streaming medians: 2.016, 2.225, 2.348ms (mean median ~2.196ms).
- Buffered-write medians: 2.279, 2.069, 2.225, 2.180ms (mean median ~2.188ms).
- Means improved slightly/noisily (~3.645ms -> ~3.444ms), but p95 was mixed (~11.42ms -> ~11.18ms) and the median difference was negligible.
- A 20,000-tab buffered-only sample produced medians around 3.24-3.39ms, useful as a scale check but not a paired win.

Validation/safety: patched source built and focused runs used benchmark-launched xenv browser processes with `ulimit -c 0`. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, and coredumps stayed at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. The buffered writer is logically plausible but not a repeatable, statistically meaningful latency improvement. Reverted `src/config.cc` and the incidental `src/browser_window.cc` move-insert tweak before committing this log.

## Experiment 51: refresh only changed virtual sidebar rows on same-window activation — FAILED

Hypothesis: for >96-tab virtual sidebars, `ActivateTab()` schedules a delayed sidebar-window refresh even when switching between two tabs inside the currently rendered virtual row window. If the rendered range is unchanged, refreshing just the previous and new active rows inline might avoid the later 96-row refresh and improve near-tab switching.

Change tested:
- In `ActivateTab()`, when both the previous and new active tab are in the same virtual sidebar rendered range, call `RefreshSidebarRow(previous)` and `RefreshSidebarRow(active)` instead of scheduling `ScheduleSidebarRefresh()`.
- Similarly refresh the active row inline when activating the already-active tab and it is currently rendered.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, direct Unix-socket client, 1000 alternating `tab-focus` commands between nearby indices 10 and 20, followed by status probes. Three baseline runs vs three usable patched runs (one patched launch/run failed with a connection-refused IPC error and produced no result). Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-virtual-activate-row-refresh-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Baseline focus medians: 0.846, 0.842, 0.855ms (mean median ~0.848ms).
- Inline row-refresh medians: 1.227, 1.123, 1.076ms (mean median ~1.142ms).
- p95 also regressed substantially: baseline mean p95 ~1.49ms vs inline row-refresh ~3.39ms.
- The existing delayed virtual sidebar refresh intentionally keeps tab-focus command latency low; doing even two row text/style updates inline moves UI work back onto the hot command path.

Validation/safety: patched source built and the broad `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed, but focused performance regressed. Runs used xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14-15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; keep the scheduled virtual-sidebar refresh design for command responsiveness.

## Experiment 51: avoid copying tab URL in tab-list JSON builder — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: `AppendTabJson()` copied `tab.url` into a local `std::string` for every tab before appending the tab-list JSON. For large `tabs` IPC responses most restored tabs already have a non-empty stored URL and no live browser, so using a `std::string_view` for the stored URL and only allocating a `frame_url` fallback when needed might reduce `tabs` latency.

Change tested:
- Replace `std::string url = tab.url` with `std::string_view url = tab.url` plus a separate `std::string frame_url` used only for the existing browser-main-frame fallback.
- Output response sizes were unchanged at 2,325,709 bytes for the 10,000-tab local IPC benchmark.

Focused benchmark: `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 10000 --ipc-iterations 40 --ipc-moved-tabs 0`, measuring `ipc.tabs_ms`. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabs-url-view-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | copy-url baseline values | baseline mean/median | string_view sample A | sample A mean/median | string_view sample B | sample B mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| median ms | 3.613, 3.558, 3.943 | 3.705 / 3.613 | 3.739, 3.468, 3.516, 3.456 | 3.545 / 3.492 | 3.994, 4.193, 3.325 | 3.837 / 3.994 | mixed |
| mean ms | 3.893, 3.798, 4.226 | 3.972 / 3.893 | 4.069, 3.825, 3.826, 3.754 | 3.868 / 3.825 | 4.350, 4.370, 3.759 | 4.160 / 4.350 | mixed/worse on repeat |
| p95 ms | 4.975, 4.653, 5.353 | 4.993 / 4.975 | 5.559, 5.771, 5.474, 4.874 | 5.419 / 5.516 | 5.913, 5.607, 5.349 | 5.623 / 5.607 | worse |

Validation/safety: patched source built, IPC-suite runs used benchmark-launched xenv browser processes with core dumps disabled, and disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Avoiding the URL copy produced a small promising first sample but did not reproduce, and p95 consistently worsened. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`.

## Experiment 52: measure nearby tab-focus in the maintained IPC suite — SUCCESS

Goal: after Experiment 51 showed that moving virtual-sidebar row refresh work back into `tab-focus` hurts command latency, the maintained direct IPC suite should explicitly track tab activation latency. The IPC suite already covers status/tab-list/JS paths, but not the shell activation path optimized by earlier sidebar and tab-id work.

Change:
- In `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc`, when the restored session has at least two tabs, prewarm the last two tab ids with direct `tab-focus` calls.
- Measure `ipc.tab_focus_nearby_ms` by alternating direct Unix-socket `tab-focus` commands between those nearby tab ids for `--ipc-iterations` iterations.
- Record `ipc.tab_focus_nearby_response_bytes` to guard the status-response shape.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 200 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-suite-tab-focus-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

First result on current HEAD (1000 restored local tabs, 200 iterations): `ipc.tab_focus_nearby_ms` median 0.841ms, p95 1.493ms, response size 293B.

Decision: keep and commit. This is benchmark coverage rather than a runtime speedup, but it adds a maintained signal for the exact shell tab-activation command path that recent virtual-sidebar experiments affect.

## Benchmark infrastructure: measure nearby tab-focus in direct IPC suite

Added direct IPC coverage for the `tab-focus` command to the optional `--suite ipc` benchmark. The new `ipc.tab_focus_nearby_ms` metric alternates focus between the active tab and a nearby tab in a restored session, capturing the direct command cost after the large-session tab-id lookup, sidebar, and activation optimizations.

Validation command:

`scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 200 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-suite-tab-focus-1000.json`

Example validation result:
- `ipc.tab_focus_nearby_ms`: median ~0.84ms, p95 ~1.49ms, mean ~0.93ms over 200 iterations.
- Existing IPC metrics (`status`, `tabs`, `commands`, `version`, `js`) continued to emit normally.

Also ran:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS

Decision: keep as benchmark infrastructure. This gives future tab-focus/activation experiments a cheap repeatable direct-socket signal without replacing the broader local tab-switch benchmark.

## Experiment 52: cache virtual sidebar row contents — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after virtual-sidebar row reuse, `RefreshSidebar()` still called `SetText()`, `SelectRange()`, and `StyleTextfield()` for every rendered row whenever the virtual row window was reused. For rendered-background closes and active reorders, many rows either do not change or only change text, not style. Caching each row's last text and colors should avoid redundant CEF view updates while keeping the same visible sidebar contents.

Change:
- Extend `SidebarRowViews` with cached `text`, `text_color`, and `background_color`.
- When reusing virtual sidebar rows, only call `SetText()`/`SelectRange()` if the computed row text changed.
- Only call `StyleTextfield()` if text/background colors changed.
- Populate the cache when rows are first created and update it from `RefreshSidebarRow()` as well.

Focused benchmarks:
- Rendered background close: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, repeated direct-socket `tab-close` of rendered sidebar index 50.
- Active reorder: 1000 restored local tabs, repeated active `tab-order` between index 0 and 999.
- Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-sidebar-row-cache-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | row-reuse values | row-reuse mean/median | cached-row values | cached mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| close median ms | 6.595, 6.736, 6.861, 6.800 | 6.748 / 6.768 | 2.107, 2.108, 2.057 | 2.091 / 2.107 | ~69.0% faster mean median |
| close mean ms | 7.230, 7.107, 7.207, 7.328 | 7.218 / 7.219 | 2.363, 2.397, 2.423 | 2.394 / 2.397 | ~66.8% faster mean |
| close p95 ms | 9.258, 8.825, 8.581, 9.299 | 8.991 / 9.041 | 3.102, 3.000, 3.213 | 3.105 / 3.102 | ~65.5% faster mean |
| active reorder median ms | 10.296, 10.308, 10.372, 9.728 | 10.176 / 10.302 | 5.466, 6.498 | 5.982 / 5.982 | ~41.2% faster mean median |
| active reorder mean ms | 10.890, 10.941, 10.737, 10.614 | 10.796 / 10.813 | 6.253, 6.848 | 6.550 / 6.550 | ~39.3% faster mean |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14-15G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a repeatable shell/chrome improvement for virtual-sidebar row-reuse paths, and the cache is local to the row views with normal full-refresh fallback intact.

## Experiment 53: inline virtual activation row refresh after row-content cache — FAILED

Hypothesis: Experiment 51 found that refreshing just the previous/new active virtual sidebar rows inline regressed `tab-focus`, but that was before Experiment 52 cached sidebar row text/colors. With cached row contents, two inline `RefreshSidebarRow()` calls might now be cheap enough to avoid the delayed 96-row virtual sidebar refresh and improve nearby virtual-tab switching.

Change tested:
- In `ActivateTab()`, when switching within the same virtual sidebar rendered range, call `RefreshSidebarRow(previous_active_index)` and `RefreshSidebarRow(active_index_)` inline instead of scheduling `ScheduleSidebarRefresh()`.
- Also refresh the already-active row inline when it is inside the rendered virtual window.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, direct Unix-socket client, 1000 alternating `tab-focus` commands between nearby indices 10 and 20. Three scheduled-refresh baseline runs after row-content caching vs four inline-row-refresh runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-virtual-activate-row-refresh-after-cache-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Scheduled-refresh baseline medians: 0.848, 0.839, 0.834ms (mean median ~0.840ms).
- Inline row-refresh medians: 1.126, 1.144, 1.172, 1.219ms (mean median ~1.165ms).
- p95 regressed from baseline mean ~1.45ms to inline mean ~3.45ms.
- Even with cached row contents, moving row updates into the `tab-focus` command path is worse than the existing delayed virtual-sidebar refresh.

Validation/safety: patched source built and focused runs used benchmark-launched xenv browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14-15G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; keep virtual sidebar refresh asynchronous for tab-focus responsiveness.

## Experiment 53: reserve sidebar text buffer — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: `SidebarTextForTab()` builds a row label for each visible virtual-sidebar row and caps it at 160 bytes, but it starts with a tiny string literal and grows through tab number, audible marker, and display URL appends. After cached virtual-sidebar row contents reduced CEF view updates, this small string-building cost is a larger fraction of rendered-row refresh paths. Reserving the capped row size up front should avoid repeated small reallocations without changing output.

Change:
- Add `text.reserve(168)` immediately after initializing the active/inactive row prefix in `SidebarTextForTab()`.
- The existing 160-byte truncation and all visible row text semantics remain unchanged.

Focused benchmarks after the cached-sidebar-row commit:
- Rendered background close: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, 60 direct Unix-socket `tab-close` commands for rendered background tab ids 51..110.
- Active reorder: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab id 1, 60 direct Unix-socket active `tab-order` commands alternating index 999 and 0.
- Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-sidebar-text-reserve-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | cached-row baseline values | baseline mean/median | reserve values | reserve mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| close median ms | 2.555, 1.936, 1.980 | 2.157 / 1.980 | 1.917, 1.901, 1.925, 1.912 | 1.914 / 1.915 | lower and steadier |
| close mean ms | 2.554, 2.006, 2.065 | 2.208 / 2.065 | 1.971, 1.939, 1.991, 1.941 | 1.960 / 1.956 | ~11% faster mean |
| close p95 ms | 3.247, 2.304, 2.565 | 2.705 / 2.565 | 2.252, 2.099, 2.219, 2.072 | 2.160 / 2.159 | ~20% faster mean |
| active reorder median ms | 4.793, 7.086, 5.442 | 5.774 / 5.442 | 5.164, 5.057, 4.840, 5.859 | 5.230 / 5.111 | ~9% faster mean, less outlier-prone |
| active reorder mean ms | 5.037, 7.349, 5.530 | 5.972 / 5.530 | 5.079, 5.117, 4.926, 5.891 | 5.253 / 5.098 | ~12% faster mean |
| active reorder p95 ms | 6.700, 9.397, 6.933 | 7.677 / 6.933 | 6.259, 6.475, 5.762, 6.850 | 6.336 / 6.367 | ~17% faster mean |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. The change is tiny, output-preserving, and repeatably reduces row-text construction overhead on the rendered sidebar refresh paths that remain after row-content caching.

## Experiment 54: append sidebar display URL without `DisplayUrl()` copy — FAILED

Hypothesis: after reserving the sidebar row text buffer, `SidebarTextForTab()` still called `DisplayUrl(url)`, which takes the URL by value, strips the scheme by erasing, truncates, then returns a temporary string that is appended into the row text. Appending the display URL directly from a `std::string_view` could avoid that temporary copy in sidebar row refresh paths.

Change tested:
- Add a local `AppendDisplayUrl(std::string&, std::string_view)` helper in `browser_window.cc` using `string_view::remove_prefix()` and direct append/truncation.
- Replace `text += DisplayUrl(url)` in `SidebarTextForTab()` with `AppendDisplayUrl(text, url)`.

Focused benchmarks reused the rendered sidebar paths from Experiment 53. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-sidebar-display-url-append-summary.jsonl`.

Observations versus the committed reserve-only build:
- Rendered background close regressed: reserve-only mean median ~1.914ms, mean ~1.960ms, p95 ~2.160ms; append-display-url mean median ~2.019ms, mean ~2.120ms, p95 ~2.680ms.
- Active reorder was neutral/slightly worse: reserve-only mean median ~5.230ms, mean ~5.253ms, p95 ~6.336ms; append-display-url mean median ~5.221ms, mean ~5.269ms, p95 ~6.409ms.
- The branchy string-view helper did not beat the small existing `DisplayUrl()` temporary after the row text reserve change.

Validation/safety: patched source built after replacing an initially invalid `StartsWith()` call with `string_view::starts_with`; focused runs used benchmark-launched xenv browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc` to the committed reserve-only implementation and rebuilt/reinstalled that clean source.

## Experiment 55: append sidebar tab number with `std::to_chars` — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: after cached sidebar rows and text buffer reservation, `SidebarTextForTab()` still used `std::to_string(index + 1)` to append the tab number. Replacing that temporary string with stack-buffer `std::to_chars()` might reduce per-row text construction overhead.

Change tested:
- Replace `text += std::to_string(index + 1)` in `SidebarTextForTab()` with a local `char[32]`, `std::to_chars()`, and direct `text.append()`.

Focused benchmarks:
- Rendered background close: 1000 restored local tabs, 60 direct-socket `tab-close` commands for rendered sidebar index 50.
- Active reorder: 1000 restored local tabs, 60 direct-socket active `tab-order` commands alternating index 999 and 0.
- Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-sidebar-number-tochars-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Rendered close was mixed: baseline median mean ~2.420ms but included one noisy 2.812ms run; `to_chars` median mean ~2.137ms, while means/p95s overlapped with the better baseline runs.
- Active reorder regressed: baseline median mean ~5.316ms vs `to_chars` ~5.555ms; baseline mean ~5.797ms vs `to_chars` ~5.946ms.
- The branch/error-check path plus stack formatting did not beat the existing `std::to_string` path on the more important reorder benchmark.

Validation/safety: patched source built, `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed, and focused runs used benchmark-launched xenv browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; the close signal was not clean enough and active reorder got worse.

## Experiment 55: small hot-path micro-optimizations — FAILED/INCONCLUSIVE

Several tiny follow-ups were tested after the sidebar row cache and row-text reserve changes. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-small-hotpath-failures-summary.jsonl`.

### 55a: build sidebar tab number with `std::to_chars` — FAILED

Hypothesis: `SidebarTextForTab()` still used `std::to_string(index + 1)`, allocating a temporary string per rendered row. Replacing it with stack-buffer `std::to_chars()` and appending directly might reduce rendered-sidebar refresh cost.

Benchmarks:
- Rendered background close, 1000 restored tabs, 60 direct `tab-close` commands.
- Active reorder, 1000 restored tabs, 60 direct active `tab-order` commands alternating index 999 and 0.

Results:
- Close path was mixed: baseline mean median ~2.420ms, patched ~2.137ms, but the baseline had one slow outlier and p95 stayed mixed (~3.91ms baseline vs ~3.50ms patched).
- Active reorder regressed: baseline mean median ~5.316ms and mean ~5.797ms; patched mean median ~5.555ms and mean ~5.946ms, with p95 neutral/slightly worse.

Decision: do not keep. The change did not improve the broader sidebar row-text path and made active reorder worse.

### 55b: check active tab first in `OnClientLoadStart()` — FAILED/MARGINAL

Hypothesis: `OnClientLoadStart()` linearly scans `tabs_` for the client. For active-tab navigations in a large session, checking `ActiveTab()` first could avoid scanning 10k tabs.

Benchmark: 10,000 restored tabs, repeated direct `open <active-tabid> <url>` against active tab 9999.

Results:
- Baseline medians: 10.296, 9.875, 10.155ms (mean ~10.109ms).
- Patched medians: 10.078, 10.136, 10.052ms (mean ~10.089ms).
- Means/p90s were only slightly lower; p95 was effectively unchanged/noisy (~11.51ms both). The command remains dominated by state/sidebar/tab-list response work, not the load-start client scan.

Decision: do not keep. The result is too small/noisy for a runtime branch/refactor.

### 55c: use `std::string_view` for status URL — INCONCLUSIVE/NOT KEPT

Hypothesis: `IpcStatusJson()` copies `tab.url` into a local `std::string` before appending it as JSON. A `std::string_view` could avoid that copy on the hot `status` IPC path.

Benchmark: 1000 restored tabs, 2000 direct `status` commands per run. Baseline medians were 0.049, 0.046, 0.042ms. The patched run got only one usable sample (median 0.046ms, mean 0.051ms, p95 0.068ms) before a concurrent build/benchmark interfered and the second run failed with an IPC connection reset.

Decision: do not keep. The first patched sample was not clearly better and the experiment did not complete cleanly enough to justify a code change.

Validation/safety: all patched sources built before measurement, benchmark-launched xenv/browser processes used core dumps disabled, and disk stayed around `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`.

## Experiment 56: skip sidebar text construction with cached row source state — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: Experiment 52 caches each sidebar row's rendered text/colors, but still constructs `SidebarTextForTab()` for every reused row before comparing with the cached text. Storing the source fields that define row text (tab id, URL, active state, audible state) could skip text construction entirely for rows whose source state is unchanged.

Change tested:
- Extend `SidebarRowViews` with cached tab id, URL, active, and audible source state.
- In virtual-sidebar row reuse and `RefreshSidebarRow()`, return early before building row text when the cached source state matches the live tab row.
- Update source-state cache whenever a row is created or refreshed.

Focused benchmarks:
- Rendered background close: 1000 restored local tabs, 60 direct-socket `tab-close` commands for rendered sidebar index 50.
- Active reorder: 1000 restored local tabs, 60 direct-socket active `tab-order` commands alternating index 999 and 0.
- Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-sidebar-source-cache-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Rendered close improved relative to one noisy baseline sample (text-cache mean ~2.695ms vs source-cache ~2.385ms), but the better baseline runs overlapped the patched runs, so the close signal was not decisive.
- Active reorder regressed: text-cache median mean ~5.316ms vs source-cache ~5.591ms; mean ~5.797ms vs ~5.982ms; p95 ~8.172ms vs ~8.405ms.
- Duplicating URL/source state in each row and comparing it appears to cost more than it saves on the active reorder path, where many rows change anyway.

Validation/safety: patched source built, `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed, and focused runs used benchmark-launched xenv browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc` and `src/browser_window.h`; the optimization is mixed and worsens active reorder.

## Benchmark infrastructure: measure tab order and tab close in direct IPC suite

Added lifecycle command coverage to the optional `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc` direct Unix-socket suite. The maintained IPC suite now also measures:

- `ipc.tab_order_active_ms`: repeatedly moves the active tab between index 0 and the end of the restored session, capturing the active reorder path affected by virtual-sidebar row reuse/cache work.
- `ipc.tab_close_rendered_ms`: closes a run of rendered background tabs starting around sidebar index 50, capturing the rendered close path affected by row-content caching, text reservation, and layout-skip work.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 100 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-suite-lifecycle-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Example current result (1000 restored local tabs, 100 iterations):
- `ipc.tab_order_active_ms` median ~5.76ms, p95 ~8.08ms, response size ~228722B.
- `ipc.tab_close_rendered_ms` median ~3.98ms, p95 ~5.27ms, response size ~286B.

Decision: keep and commit. This is benchmark coverage, not a runtime speedup, but it adds maintained signals for two lifecycle command paths that have seen repeated successful and failed shell-performance experiments.

## Experiment 57: add lifecycle paths to maintained direct IPC suite — SUCCESS

Goal: recent runtime wins were concentrated in shell tab lifecycle paths: active tab reorder, rendered/virtual sidebar close, and tab focus. The maintained opt-in IPC suite already measured status/tabs/JS and nearby tab-focus, but it did not cover the close and active reorder paths that drove the sidebar row-cache and row-text reserve work.

Change:
- Extend `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc` with `ipc.tab_order_active_ms`, alternating the active tab between index 0 and the last index through direct Unix-socket `tab-order`.
- Extend it with `ipc.tab_close_rendered_ms`, closing non-active tab ids from the currently rendered 96-row virtual sidebar window. The close window is derived from the current `active_index` and clamps the same way as the virtual sidebar, so it exercises rendered-row update paths rather than offscreen skip paths.
- Record response sizes for both commands as lightweight response-shape guards.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 100 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-suite-lifecycle-1000-v2.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, `/var/lib/systemd/coredump` unchanged at 4.0G.

First validation result on current HEAD:
- `ipc.tab_order_active_ms`: median 5.95ms, p95 9.13ms, response size 228722B.
- `ipc.tab_close_rendered_ms`: n=95 (rendered window minus active tab), median 4.37ms, p95 6.00ms, response size 286B.

Decision: keep and commit. This is benchmark infrastructure rather than a runtime speedup, but it makes the repeatable IPC suite cover the lifecycle paths where the latest successful performance changes landed.

## Benchmark infrastructure: close tabs from the rendered IPC-suite sidebar window

Refined the optional `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc` lifecycle close metric added earlier. The first `ipc.tab_close_rendered_ms` implementation selected close ids starting at index 50, but after the suite's focus/order/moved-tab setup the active tab can be near the end of a large virtualized sidebar. Index 50 is then no longer guaranteed to be in the rendered virtual sidebar window, so the metric could accidentally measure offscreen-close behavior instead of rendered-row close behavior.

Change:
- Before measuring `ipc.tab_close_rendered_ms`, fetch the current `tabs` state.
- Compute the virtual sidebar rendered window around the current active index (96 rows for large sessions, full range for small sessions).
- Close non-active tab ids from that rendered window, excluding the current active tab id.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 100 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-suite-rendered-close-window-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Example current result: `ipc.tab_close_rendered_ms` closed 95 non-active tabs from rendered window start index 904, median ~4.32ms, p95 ~5.95ms, response size 286B.

Decision: keep and commit. This keeps the maintained IPC lifecycle benchmark aligned with the shell path it is intended to measure: rendered virtual-sidebar close updates, not the faster offscreen-close fast path.


## Experiment 57: tune tab-list JSON reserve estimate to 232 bytes/tab — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: `TabsJson()` reserves `96 + tabs_.size() * 220` bytes, but the 10,000-tab local IPC response is about 2,325,674 bytes (~232.6 bytes/tab). Earlier Experiment 18 tried a larger 256 bytes/tab reserve and was not convincing; a tighter 232 bytes/tab estimate might avoid one growth step without excessive over-reservation.

Change tested:
- Change `TabsJson()` reserve from `96 + tabs_.size() * 220` to `96 + tabs_.size() * 232`.

Focused benchmark: 10,000 restored local tabs, 40 direct Unix-socket `tabs` IPC calls per run. Three baseline 220-byte reserve runs vs four 232-byte reserve runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabs-json-reserve-232-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Baseline median values: 4.090, 3.762, 3.673ms (mean median ~3.842ms).
- Reserve-232 median values: 3.664, 3.652, 4.060, 3.768ms (mean median ~3.786ms).
- Means were essentially neutral (~4.886ms baseline vs ~4.866ms patched), and p95 was mixed/noisy (~8.41ms baseline vs ~8.52ms patched).
- The tiny median shift did not reproduce as a clear overall win and p95 did not improve.

Validation/safety: patched source built and focused runs used xenv/benchmark-launched browser processes with core dumps disabled. A broad `make benchmark` attempt on the patched build hit a connection-refused IPC failure in the local suite; after reverting the patch and rebuilding/reinstalling, `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted to the existing 220 bytes/tab reserve; the narrower reserve change is still not a repeatable improvement over the current string-builder path.

## Experiment 59: avoid copying close URL before logging — FAILED

Hypothesis: `CloseTabAtIndex()` copied `tabs_[closing].url` into a local `std::string closing_url`, then copied it again into the closed-tab stack. After row-cache and row-text optimizations made rendered close faster, replacing the local copy with `const std::string&` might remove one small allocation/copy from the close path while preserving logging and undo behavior.

Change tested:
- Replace `const std::string closing_url = tabs_[closing].url;` with `const std::string& closing_url = tabs_[closing].url;` in `CloseTabAtIndex()`.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, repeated direct-socket `tab-close` of rendered sidebar index 50 (60 closes per run). Four clean baseline runs vs four reference-url runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-close-url-reference-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Baseline close medians: 2.219, 2.183, 1.853, 2.122ms (mean median ~2.094ms).
- Reference-url medians: 2.202, 2.349, 2.240, 2.151ms (mean median ~2.235ms).
- Means and p95 were neutral to slightly worse with the reference variant; the single avoided URL copy is not a meaningful contributor to rendered close latency.

Validation/safety: both source variants built and focused runs used benchmark-launched xenv browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; keep the existing local copy, which is simple and safe around later tab mutation.

## Experiment 58: defer virtual sidebar refresh after tab-order — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: `MoveTabToIndex()` synchronously refreshed the virtual sidebar whenever a tab move touched the rendered sidebar window or moved the active tab across virtual-sidebar windows. That keeps the sidebar immediately current, but it puts 96-row sidebar update work directly on the `tab-order` IPC command path. `ActivateTab()` already defers virtual-sidebar window refreshes to preserve focus command responsiveness; applying the same delayed-refresh approach to large-session tab reorders should greatly reduce `tab-order` latency while still updating visible content/order state immediately and refreshing sidebar chrome shortly after.

Change:
- Keep the existing fast path that skips sidebar work entirely when a background move is wholly outside the rendered virtual sidebar window.
- For other >96-tab virtual-sidebar moves, call `ScheduleSidebarRefresh()` instead of synchronously `RefreshSidebar()`/`Layout()`.
- Retain synchronous refresh/layout for non-virtual sidebars.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab id 1, 60 direct Unix-socket active `tab-order` commands alternating between index 999 and 0. Three synchronous-refresh baseline runs vs four deferred-refresh runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-deferred-taborder-refresh-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | sync values | sync mean/median | deferred values | deferred mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| active tab-order median ms | 5.320, 5.282, 5.346 | 5.316 / 5.320 | 0.833, 0.737, 0.751, 0.815 | 0.784 / 0.783 | ~85.3% faster mean median |
| active tab-order mean ms | 5.659, 5.749, 5.982 | 5.797 / 5.749 | 1.149, 1.141, 1.186, 1.229 | 1.176 / 1.167 | ~79.7% faster mean |
| active tab-order p95 ms | 7.740, 8.232, 8.544 | 8.172 / 8.232 | 2.860, 2.892, 2.879, 3.616 | 3.062 / 2.886 | ~62.5% faster mean |

Maintained IPC-suite validation: `ipc.tab_order_active_ms` on the 1000-tab direct IPC suite moved from median ~5.25ms / p95 ~7.61ms to median ~0.65ms / p95 ~0.84ms. Full suite output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-deferred-taborder-refresh-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
