- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 100 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-deferred-taborder-refresh-ipc-1000.json`
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. For large virtualized sidebars, tab order/content state and IPC responses update immediately while sidebar chrome catches up through the same delayed refresh mechanism already used for virtual tab activation.

## Experiment 59: defer virtual sidebar refresh after rendered background close — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after Experiment 58 moved large-session `tab-order` sidebar updates off the command path, rendered background closes still synchronously refreshed virtual-sidebar rows. Closing a non-active tab in a >96-tab virtual sidebar updates tab state immediately and can refresh sidebar chrome shortly after, like virtual activation/reorder. Deferring the rendered-sidebar refresh should make `tab-close` much more responsive while preserving the existing immediate no-refresh fast path for wholly offscreen closes.

Change:
- Keep the existing offscreen close fast path that returns without any sidebar work when the closed tab is wholly after the rendered window.
- For other non-active closes in >96-tab virtual sidebars, call `ScheduleSidebarRefresh()` and return instead of synchronously `RefreshSidebar()`/`Layout()`.
- Keep synchronous refresh/layout for active closes, single-tab replacement, and non-virtual sidebars.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, 60 direct Unix-socket `tab-close` commands for rendered sidebar index 50. Three synchronous-refresh baseline runs vs four deferred-refresh runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-deferred-close-refresh-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | sync values | sync mean/median | deferred values | deferred mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| rendered close median ms | 2.812, 2.145, 2.302 | 2.420 / 2.302 | 0.332, 0.311, 0.318, 0.338 | 0.325 / 0.325 | ~86.6% faster mean median |
| rendered close mean ms | 3.091, 2.432, 2.562 | 2.695 / 2.562 | 0.462, 0.568, 0.475, 0.499 | 0.501 / 0.487 | ~81.4% faster mean |
| rendered close p95 ms | 4.926, 3.329, 3.476 | 3.910 / 3.476 | 0.656, 0.884, 0.932, 0.883 | 0.839 / 0.884 | ~78.5% faster mean |

Maintained IPC-suite validation: `ipc.tab_close_rendered_ms` on the 1000-tab direct IPC suite moved from median ~4.32ms / p95 ~5.95ms to median ~0.40ms / p95 ~4.66ms. Full suite output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-deferred-close-refresh-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`. The suite's p95 still includes occasional delayed UI-work outliers, but median/mean are much lower.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 100 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-deferred-close-refresh-ipc-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. For virtualized sidebars, non-active close state and IPC responses update immediately while sidebar chrome catches up through the same delayed-refresh mechanism used for activation and tab-order.

## Experiment 60: skip/defer virtual sidebar refresh after background `open` — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: the `open <tabid> <url>` IPC path always called `RefreshSidebar(); Layout();` after changing a tab URL. For a large virtualized sidebar, changing a non-active tab outside the currently rendered sidebar window cannot affect visible sidebar rows or layout, and changing a rendered non-active row can use the same delayed virtual-sidebar refresh strategy now used for tab-focus/tab-order responsiveness.

Change:
- In the `open` IPC handler, remember the resolved tab index before mutating the tab.
- For non-active tabs in a >96-tab virtual sidebar:
  - if the target tab is inside the current rendered virtual sidebar window, call `ScheduleSidebarRefresh()` and return the normal `TabsJson()` response;
  - if the target tab is outside the rendered window, skip sidebar refresh/layout entirely and return `TabsJson()`.
- Keep existing synchronous refresh/layout behavior for active tabs and non-virtual sidebars.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, repeated direct Unix-socket `open <tabid> <local-url>` against offscreen background tab index 500 (60 opens per run). Three synchronous-refresh baseline runs vs four skip/defer runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-background-open-refresh-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | sync values | sync mean/median | skip/defer values | skip/defer mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| open median ms | 2.459, 2.886, 2.730 | 2.692 / 2.730 | 0.793, 0.807, 0.845, 0.820 | 0.816 / 0.814 | ~69.7% faster mean median |
| open mean ms | 3.004, 3.275, 3.147 | 3.142 / 3.147 | 1.086, 1.055, 1.096, 1.024 | 1.065 / 1.071 | ~66.1% faster mean |
| open p95 ms | 5.261, 5.061, 4.730 | 5.018 / 5.061 | 2.965, 2.321, 2.360, 2.684 | 2.583 / 2.522 | ~48.5% faster mean |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a bounded large-session shell fast path: visible tab/content state and `TabsJson()` response update immediately, while irrelevant/offscreen sidebar work is skipped and rendered-row updates are deferred through the existing virtual-sidebar refresh queue.

## Experiment 61: defer virtual sidebar refresh after active tab insert — SUCCESS/MODEST

Hypothesis: Experiment 47 made active tab insertion faster by setting `active_index_` before the first sidebar refresh, but `InsertTab(..., activate=true)` still synchronously refreshed the 96-row virtual sidebar window for large sessions. Virtual tab activation, tab-order, tab-close, and background `open` paths now defer large-session sidebar chrome updates; active insertion can do the same after updating active tab state and scheduling active browser sync.

Change:
- In the active, non-bulk branch of `InsertTab()`, for >96-tab virtual sidebars call `ScheduleSidebarRefresh()` instead of synchronous `RefreshSidebar()`/`Layout()`.
- Keep synchronous refresh/layout for non-virtual sidebars.
- Keep state save and active-browser sync scheduling unchanged.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, 60 direct Unix-socket `open-tab <local-url>` commands per run. Three synchronous-refresh baseline runs vs four deferred-refresh runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-deferred-active-insert-refresh-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | sync values | sync mean/median | deferred values | deferred mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| open-tab median ms | 18.530, 19.578, 18.668 | 18.925 / 18.668 | 17.937, 19.465, 18.120, 17.467 | 18.247 / 18.028 | ~3.6% faster mean median |
| open-tab mean ms | 18.937, 19.841, 18.761 | 19.179 / 18.937 | 17.931, 19.440, 18.339, 17.784 | 18.374 / 18.135 | ~4.2% faster mean |
| open-tab p95 ms | 21.740, 24.708, 22.068 | 22.839 / 22.068 | 20.652, 23.332, 22.725, 21.121 | 21.958 / 21.923 | ~3.9% faster mean |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. The improvement is modest but consistent, and it aligns active insertion with the existing virtual-sidebar delayed-refresh strategy used for focus/reorder/close paths.

## Experiment 62: defer virtual sidebar refresh after active close — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after the virtual-sidebar tab-order/close/open deferrals, active tab close still synchronously refreshed the 96-row virtual sidebar and laid out the whole window after selecting the next active tab. The visible browser selection is already updated directly in `CloseTabAtIndex()`, so in large virtualized sidebars the sidebar chrome can be refreshed on the delayed virtual-sidebar queue instead of blocking the `tab-close` command.

Change:
- After active close state/visible-browser updates and `SaveState()`, if the session is using a virtual sidebar, call `ScheduleSidebarRefresh()` and return.
- Keep existing synchronous behavior for non-virtual sidebars and the single-tab placeholder case.
- Run a content-inner layout only if available, avoiding the full window `Layout()` previously coupled to active close.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, repeated direct Unix-socket active `tab-close` of index 0 (40 closes per run). Three synchronous active-close baseline runs vs four deferred-sidebar runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-active-close-defer-refresh-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | sync values | sync mean/median | deferred values | deferred mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| active close median ms | 14.170, 14.086, 13.659 | 13.972 / 14.086 | 11.692, 12.801, 11.155, 12.240 | 11.972 / 11.966 | ~14.3% faster mean median |
| active close mean ms | 14.643, 14.663, 14.469 | 14.592 / 14.643 | 12.571, 13.751, 11.764, 12.729 | 12.704 / 12.650 | ~12.9% faster mean |
| active close p95 ms | 16.254, 16.741, 17.077 | 16.691 / 16.741 | 16.546, 17.833, 15.923, 15.286 | 16.397 / 16.235 | neutral/slightly faster mean p95 |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This extends the same bounded delayed-virtual-sidebar-refresh design to active close while preserving immediate active tab/content state updates and broad benchmark correctness; the strongest signal is lower median/mean command latency, while p95 is roughly neutral.

## Experiment 63: defer virtual sidebar refresh after active `open` — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after background `open` can skip/defer virtual sidebar refreshes, active `open <tabid> <url>` still synchronously calls `RefreshSidebar(); Layout();` before returning the large `TabsJson()` response. For >96-tab virtual sidebars, the active tab URL/content state is updated immediately and the sidebar row can be refreshed on the delayed virtual-sidebar queue, matching the recent tab-focus/tab-order/close deferrals.

Change:
- Extend the `open` IPC handler's virtual-sidebar fast path to include the active tab.
- For large virtual sidebars, schedule a sidebar refresh when the changed tab is active or currently rendered, skip full `Layout()`, and return the unchanged `TabsJson()` response.
- Preserve the existing synchronous path for non-virtual sidebars.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, repeated direct Unix-socket `open <active-tabid> <local-url>` against the active tab (60 opens per run). Three synchronous active-open baseline runs vs four deferred-refresh runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-active-open-defer-refresh-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | sync values | sync mean/median | deferred values | deferred mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| active open median ms | 5.268, 4.985, 4.958 | 5.070 / 4.985 | 2.139, 2.224, 2.242, 2.150 | 2.189 / 2.181 | ~56.8% faster mean median |
| active open mean ms | 5.321, 5.028, 5.012 | 5.120 / 5.028 | 2.419, 2.505, 2.477, 2.422 | 2.456 / 2.449 | ~52.0% faster mean |
| active open p95 ms | 6.654, 6.152, 5.852 | 6.219 / 6.152 | 3.583, 3.299, 3.273, 3.301 | 3.364 / 3.300 | ~45.9% faster mean |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This completes the bounded virtual-sidebar deferral pattern for active existing-tab navigation while keeping state/response semantics immediate and reusing the existing delayed sidebar refresh mechanism for chrome catch-up.

## Benchmark infrastructure: measure active `open` in direct IPC suite

Goal: after Experiment 63 optimized active existing-tab navigation by deferring virtual-sidebar refresh/layout work, the maintained direct IPC suite should track that shell path instead of relying only on one-off focused scripts.

Change:
- Extend `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc` with `ipc.open_active_ms`.
- In a restored session, repeatedly send direct Unix-socket `open <active-tabid> <local-url>` commands to the active tab, capped at 40 iterations so the optional IPC suite remains reasonably quick and low-data.
- Record `ipc.open_active_response_bytes` to guard the unchanged `TabsJson()` response shape.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 100 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-suite-active-open-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G, results stayed ~2.5M.

First validation result on current HEAD:
- `ipc.open_active_ms`: n=40, median ~2.14ms, mean ~2.20ms, p95 ~2.34ms, response size 228705B.
- Existing lifecycle signals remained healthy: `ipc.tab_order_active_ms` median ~0.65ms, `ipc.tab_close_rendered_ms` median ~0.30ms, `ipc.tab_focus_nearby_ms` median ~0.81ms.

Decision: keep and commit as benchmark infrastructure. This gives future active-navigation/sidebar experiments a maintained repeatable direct-socket metric without replacing the broader local suite.

## Benchmark infrastructure: measure the current active tab in IPC active-open metric

The optional direct IPC suite had an `ipc.open_active_ms` metric, but after the suite's moved-tab setup it still opened the originally active tab id. The suite focuses moved tabs before this point, so the originally active tab can become a background tab and the metric may measure background `open` instead of active `open`.

Change:
- Before measuring `ipc.open_active_ms`, fetch the current `tabs` state.
- Use the current `active_tabid` and record the current `active_index` in the metric record.
- Keep the response-size guard and bounded 40-iteration limit.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 80 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-suite-actual-active-open-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Example current result: `ipc.open_active_ms` measured current active tab id 4 at active index 999, median ~2.20ms, p95 ~2.77ms, response size ~228810B.

Decision: keep and commit. This is benchmark-correctness infrastructure for the active-open path optimized in Experiment 63.

## Experiment 64: defer sidebar refresh on browser creation callback — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: `OnClientBrowserCreated()` calls `RefreshSidebar()` whenever a CEF browser view finishes creation. In large virtual-sidebar sessions, lazy first activation of a deferred restored tab creates a browser view on the active path, so using `ScheduleSidebarRefresh()` for >96-tab virtual sidebars might reduce first-activation latency while keeping the sidebar chrome eventually correct.

Change tested:
- In `OnClientBrowserCreated()`, if the tab list is virtualized and the sidebar spacer exists, call `ScheduleSidebarRefresh()` and return instead of immediately refreshing the sidebar.
- Keep the existing synchronous refresh for non-virtual sidebars.

Focused benchmark: `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite large-restore --large-restore-tabs 1000 --large-restore-activate-index 500`, comparing three baseline runs to four patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-onclient-created-sidebar-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Baseline startup active-complete values were 644.2, 540.0, 550.4ms; patched values were 548.7, 539.9, 538.4, 551.1ms. The patched mean is lower only because the first baseline run was a startup outlier; medians are close (550.4ms baseline vs 544.3ms patched).
- Deferred activation complete was neutral/slightly worse by median: 117.5ms baseline vs 119.7ms patched, with both variants showing one ~170ms outlier.
- Deferred focus command latency was also neutral/slightly worse: 26.2ms baseline median vs 26.8ms patched median.
- The targeted callback is not a repeatable bottleneck after the existing lazy restore and virtual-sidebar deferrals.

Validation/safety: both variants built with `cmake --build build-source -j12`; runs used `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark` so browser children inherited `ulimit -c 0`; disk stayed at `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, and coredumps unchanged at 4.0G. After reverting the patch, the clean source was rebuilt.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; commit only this failure log and summary so the callback deferral is not retried without new evidence.

## Benchmark infrastructure: measure active tab close in direct IPC suite

Goal: after the recent virtual-sidebar close deferrals, the maintained direct IPC suite still measured rendered non-active close but did not track the slower active-close path. Add a bounded metric so future close/state/sidebar experiments can see active-close regressions and wins without relying only on ad-hoc scripts.

Change:
- Extend `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc` with `ipc.tab_close_active_ms` and `ipc.tab_close_active_response_bytes`.
- Measure up to 40 direct Unix-socket `tab-close` commands with no explicit tab id, so the current active tab is closed and focus advances using normal active-close behavior.
- Record the initial active tab id/index and closed count in the detailed records.
- Run this destructive metric near the end of the IPC suite, before the existing rendered non-active close cleanup.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 80 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-suite-active-close-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

First validation result on current HEAD:
- `ipc.tab_close_active_ms`: n=40, median ~14.93ms, mean ~14.13ms, p95 ~18.70ms, response size 286B.
- Existing active/open/reorder/close signals remained present in the same run: `ipc.open_active_ms` median ~2.32ms, `ipc.tab_order_active_ms` median ~0.67ms, `ipc.tab_close_rendered_ms` median ~0.33ms.

Decision: keep and commit as benchmark infrastructure. Active close is now an explicit maintained metric and reveals that remaining active-close latency is dominated by work other than the already-deferred virtual-sidebar refresh.

## Experiment 65: skip resource handler for unblocked resource types — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: after native network capture was disabled by default, `BrowserClient::GetResourceRequestHandler()` still returned `this` for every request so `OnBeforeResourceLoad()`, response, filter, and completion callbacks could run content-blocking/capture checks. Main-frame, stylesheet, and font resources are never blocked by the native content blocker, so returning `nullptr` for those resource types when capture is disabled might avoid unnecessary CEF resource-handler callbacks on CSS/font-heavy pages.

Change tested in `src/browser_client.cc`:
- When `NativeNetworkCaptureEnabled()` is false and native content blocking is also disabled, return `nullptr` from `GetResourceRequestHandler()`.
- When capture is false but content blocking is enabled, return `nullptr` for `RT_MAIN_FRAME`, `RT_STYLESHEET`, and `RT_FONT_RESOURCE`; keep returning `this` for potentially blockable subresources.

Focused benchmark: temporary xenv/benchmark-harness script loading local pages with 300 or 600 distinct no-store stylesheet resources, so browser children still inherited `ulimit -c 0`. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-resource-handler-skip-failure-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- 300-CSS default runs looked mildly promising in page timing, but the effect was small and noisy: baseline load-event run means 283.65, 324.23, 293.55ms vs patched 283.48, 296.10, 286.63, 270.30ms; observed elapsed similarly shifted only modestly.
- 600-CSS default runs did not reproduce a clear win: baseline load-event means 575.43, 642.80, 522.77ms vs patched 586.23, 577.20, 532.33, 541.97ms, while command latency was neutral/mixed.
- With `--disable-vimbrowser-content-blocking`, where the patch can skip the handler entirely, page timing was still neutral/worse (baseline load-event means 561.50, 575.30ms vs patched 593.03, 583.03, 553.53ms), with command latency mixed.

Validation/safety: both variants built with `cmake --build build-source -j12` and were installed through `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`; all focused runs used xenv/benchmark-launched browsers with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed safely under the budget.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_client.cc`; the callback avoidance is logically plausible but not a statistically repeatable improvement in the focused CSS-resource benchmark.

## Experiment 66: skip content layout after virtual active close — FAILED

Hypothesis: after active close in a >96-tab virtual-sidebar session, `CloseTabAtIndex()` already schedules active-browser selection and virtual-sidebar refresh, but still runs `content_inner_panel_->Layout()` before returning. Since `ApplyActiveBrowserSelection()` lays out the content panel when the next active browser is shown, removing this immediate content layout might reduce active `tab-close` command latency.

Change tested:
- In the virtual-sidebar active-close fast path, remove the immediate `content_inner_panel_->Layout()` call while preserving `ScheduleActiveBrowserSync()` and `ScheduleSidebarRefresh()`.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, repeated direct Unix-socket `tab-close` of the active tab (40 closes per run). Three baseline runs vs four no-content-layout runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-active-close-content-layout-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Baseline medians: 12.935, 12.810, 13.026ms (mean median ~12.924ms).
- No-layout medians: 16.653, 16.408, 15.988, 15.551ms (mean median ~16.150ms).
- Means and p95 also regressed substantially (baseline mean latency ~13.18ms vs no-layout ~16.62ms; baseline mean p95 ~15.85ms vs no-layout ~18.49ms).
- The explicit content layout likely helps drain/remap content state predictably before the next close; removing it leaves more delayed work to interfere with subsequent active-close commands.

Validation/safety: both variants built with `cmake --build build-source -j12`; focused runs used xenv through the benchmark harness with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed at `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; retain the current active-close content layout despite the apparent redundancy.

## Experiment 67: defer next-browser selection after active close — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after Experiment 62 moved virtual-sidebar refresh/layout off the active `tab-close` path, active close still synchronously instantiated/showed the next active browser view before returning. Large restored sessions already use asynchronous active-browser selection for tab focus, so active close can update tab state immediately, invalidate the closed view, and schedule the next active browser selection on the same zero-delay generation path instead of doing that work inline.

Change:
- In `CloseTabAtIndex()`, after erasing an active tab in a >96-tab virtual-sidebar session, set `visible_tab_index_` to `kNoTabIndex` and call `ScheduleActiveBrowserSync()`.
- Keep the existing immediate `EnsureTabBrowser()` / `SetVisible(true)` path for non-virtual sidebars and the existing single-tab placeholder path.
- Keep state saving and delayed virtual-sidebar refresh behavior unchanged; `ApplyActiveBrowserSelection()` still loads deferred tabs, lays out the content inner panel, and restores webview focus shortly after the command returns.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, optional direct IPC suite with the maintained `ipc.tab_close_active_ms` metric (`--ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 80 --ipc-moved-tabs 4`). Three baseline runs vs four patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-active-close-defer-browser-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean | patched values | patched mean | improvement |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| active close median ms | 15.726, 16.438, 18.857 | 17.007 | 12.901, 12.895, 14.391, 13.968 | 13.539 | ~20.4% faster |
| active close mean ms | 14.863, 15.760, 17.597 | 16.073 | 12.239, 12.088, 13.470, 13.522 | 12.830 | ~20.2% faster |
| active close p95 ms | 18.031, 19.857, 22.510 | 20.133 | 15.108, 15.193, 17.676, 17.762 | 16.435 | ~18.4% faster |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. For large virtualized sessions, active tab/content state changes immediately while the next browser view catches up through the same coalesced active-selection mechanism used by tab focus, reducing active-close command latency without changing non-virtual behavior.

## Experiment 68: remove close lifecycle stderr logging after active-close deferrals — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: after the active close path was made faster and explicitly measured in the IPC suite, the remaining unconditional `std::cerr` diagnostic in `CloseTabAtIndex()` might now be a visible part of close latency. Removing the close lifecycle log entirely could improve active and rendered close metrics.

Change tested:
- Remove the `vimbrowser: close-tab ...` `std::cerr` line and avoid the temporary `closing_url` string used for that log.
- Preserve the closed-tab stack by pushing `tabs_[closing].url` directly.

Focused benchmark: current direct IPC suite (`--ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 80 --ipc-moved-tabs 4`) comparing three logged baseline runs to four no-log runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-close-log-removal-v2-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Active-close median mean moved from ~13.51ms with logging to ~12.93ms without logging; active-close mean moved from ~12.72ms to ~12.34ms.
- Active-close p95 was modestly better on average (~16.65ms to ~15.74ms), but this is a small improvement relative to the noisy browser-selection/load work in active close.
- Rendered close was neutral/small: median mean ~0.341ms with logging vs ~0.298ms without logging, but p95 stayed around 0.78-0.86ms and previous Experiment 48 already showed close-log gating was not a clear enough win.
- Removing diagnostics entirely is less conservative than the previously rejected opt-in gate and does not have a large enough signal to justify losing the default lifecycle log.

Validation/safety: both variants built and focused runs used benchmark-launched xenv browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed at `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; keep the summary/log only to avoid re-testing close-log removal without a stronger reason.

## Experiment 69: remove browser-ready stdout log on client creation — SUCCESS/MODEST

Hypothesis: `BrowserClient::OnAfterCreated()` printed `vimbrowser: browser ready` with `std::endl` for every CEF browser creation. After active-close work, closing active tabs can still create/select replacement browsers, so this diagnostic line can add synchronous log-file I/O and flushing to repeated lifecycle paths. The benchmark harness does not consume this line; IPC readiness is detected through the socket/status path.

Change:
- Remove the unconditional `std::cout << "vimbrowser: browser ready" << std::endl;` from `BrowserClient::OnAfterCreated()`.
- Keep the owner callback (`OnClientBrowserCreated`) unchanged.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, 40 direct Unix-socket active `tab-close` commands per run. Three baseline runs vs four patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-browser-ready-log-removal-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | with browser-ready log | without log | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| active-close median mean | 3.718ms | 3.387ms | ~8.9% faster |
| active-close mean | 4.371ms | 3.975ms | ~9.1% faster |
| active-close p90 mean | 6.430ms | 5.504ms | ~14.4% faster |
| active-close p95 mean | 7.298ms | 6.122ms | ~16.1% faster |

Maintained IPC-suite spot check after the change: `ipc.tab_close_active_ms` median ~12.14ms, mean ~11.58ms, p95 ~14.49ms. Full suite output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-browser-ready-log-removed-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 80 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-browser-ready-log-removed-ipc-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This removes unconditional synchronous diagnostic output from browser creation and gives a modest repeatable improvement on the active-close path.

## Experiment 70: defer state save after virtual active close — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: after active-close browser selection and virtual-sidebar work were moved off the command path, `CloseTabAtIndex()` still calls `SaveState()` synchronously before returning. In >96-tab virtual-sidebar sessions, using the existing coalesced `ScheduleStateSave()` for active closes might remove another O(tab-count) save from repeated close latency while still preserving state shortly after the command.

Change tested:
- For active closes in virtual-sidebar sessions, call `ScheduleStateSave()` instead of immediate `SaveState()`.
- Keep synchronous `SaveState()` for non-virtual sidebars and non-active closes.

Focused benchmark: maintained direct IPC suite with 1000 restored local tabs and `--ipc-iterations 80 --ipc-moved-tabs 4`. Three baseline runs vs four deferred-state-save runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-active-close-state-save-deferral-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Active-close median means were identical within noise: 12.732ms baseline vs 12.732ms patched; medians were 12.623ms baseline vs 12.373ms patched.
- Active-close mean latency was only slightly lower (12.251ms baseline vs 12.092ms patched), while p95 was effectively unchanged/slightly worse by mean (15.346ms baseline vs 15.376ms patched) and the first patched run had a 29ms max outlier.
- Adjacent IPC metrics (`open_active`, `tab_order_active`, rendered close) remained neutral, so there is no clear evidence that synchronous state save dominates after the existing active-close deferrals.
- Deferring persistence after tab close is a semantic risk unless it buys a clear latency win; this run does not justify that tradeoff.

Validation/safety: both variants built with `cmake --build build-source -j12`; focused runs used `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`/xenv with browser core dumps disabled. Disk stayed at `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; retain immediate `SaveState()` on active close.

## Experiment 71: keep close log but replace `std::endl` flush — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: Experiment 68 found that removing the close-tab lifecycle log entirely was not worth losing diagnostics, but part of the cost may be the `std::endl` flush rather than formatting. Replacing `std::endl` with `\n` would preserve the log line while avoiding a forced flush on every tab close.

Change tested:
- In `CloseTabAtIndex()`, change the close-tab diagnostic from `<< std::endl` to `<< '\n'`.

Focused benchmark: 1000 restored local tabs with direct Unix-socket active close loops. Three baseline runs with `std::endl` vs three newline runs; a fourth newline run was interrupted before writing data, so it was discarded. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-close-log-newline-failure-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Active-close medians moved the wrong way on average: baseline mean median ~3.293ms vs newline mean median ~3.541ms.
- Active-close mean latency was only slightly better (baseline ~4.098ms vs newline ~3.937ms), while baseline had one large p95 outlier that made p95 look better for newline. The per-run medians and means do not show a stable enough improvement.
- Rendered-close baseline spot checks stayed sub-ms, but patched rendered-close coverage did not complete before interruption, so there is not enough broad evidence to keep this diagnostic formatting tweak.

Validation/safety: tested builds used `cmake --build build-source -j12`; benchmark runs used xenv/harnessed browser children with core dumps disabled. Disk remained safe (`/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps ~4.0G).

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; retain the existing flushed close diagnostic unless a future experiment provides stronger end-to-end evidence.

## Experiment 72: detach closing tab in-place before vector erase — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: Experiment 38 found that moving the closing `Tab` record before backend detach was not a win. After later close-path deferrals, the remaining `Tab closing_tab = tabs_[closing]` copy might still be avoidable by detaching the live vector element in-place before erasing it, preserving the existing vector erase semantics without creating a temporary `Tab` copy.

Change tested:
- Replace `Tab closing_tab = tabs_[closing]; CloseTabBackend(closing_tab);` in `CloseTabAtIndex()` with `CloseTabBackend(tabs_[closing]);`, leaving the subsequent `tabs_.erase(...)` unchanged.

Focused benchmarks:
- 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, 40 direct Unix-socket active `tab-close` commands per run.
- 1000 restored local tabs, active tab 0, 60 direct Unix-socket rendered background closes at index 50 per run.
- Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-close-inplace-detach-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Active close regressed: copy-before-detach median mean ~3.293ms vs in-place detach ~3.613ms; mean ~4.098ms vs ~4.209ms.
- Rendered close was roughly neutral/slightly lower in mean (~0.579ms vs ~0.536ms) but sub-ms and noisy; the active-close regression dominates.
- Avoiding the temporary copy did not improve the hot active-close path, consistent with earlier move-record results that copying one `Tab` is not the dominant cost.

Validation/safety: patched source built and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed. After reverting, source rebuilt/reinstalled and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed again. All runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; keep the copy-before-detach code.

## Experiment 73: exact-command fast path before IPC argument splitting — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: `HandleIpcCommand()` always split the command string and lowercased the command token before handling even exact no-argument commands such as `version`, `commands`, `status`, `tabs`, and active `tab-close`. Adding exact lowercase fast paths before `SplitArgs()` might reduce direct IPC overhead and active close latency.

Change tested:
- Add early `command_line == ...` checks for `version`/`protocol`, `commands`, `status`/`json`, `tabs`, and no-arg `tab-close`, returning the same responses as the parsed path.

Focused benchmark: maintained direct IPC suite with 1000 restored local tabs, `--ipc-iterations 80 --ipc-moved-tabs 4`. One recent baseline suite run vs four exact-fast-path runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-exact-command-fastpath-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Tiny exact-command metrics moved only within noise: version median ~0.098ms baseline vs exact-fast-path run medians 0.079–0.098ms; commands/status similarly around ~0.05–0.08ms.
- Large-output `tabs` median was neutral/slightly faster in some runs (~0.499ms baseline vs exact-fast-path mean median ~0.487ms), but still dominated by JSON building and response transfer.
- Active close got worse in the direct suite: baseline median ~12.14ms vs exact-fast-path mean median ~12.56ms.
- A broad `make benchmark` attempt on the patched build hit a local-suite JS timeout. After reverting/rebuilding/reinstalling, `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed.

Validation/safety: patched source built and focused runs used benchmark-launched xenv browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; the micro-fast-path does not produce a meaningful stable win and may add branch overhead before normal parsed commands.

## Experiment 74: trim IPC command newline in-place — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: `IpcServer::HandleClient()` built a command string and then called `command = TrimNewline(command)`, where `TrimNewline()` accepted the string by value. That copies every IPC command before dispatch. Changing it to trim the existing string in-place could reduce direct IPC overhead, especially for longer commands, without changing protocol behavior.

Change tested:
- Change `TrimNewline(std::string value) -> std::string` to `TrimNewline(std::string& value) -> void` and call `TrimNewline(command)`.

Focused benchmark: maintained direct IPC suite with 1000 restored local tabs, `--ipc-iterations 80 --ipc-moved-tabs 4`. Three baseline runs vs four in-place-trim runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-trim-inplace-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Exact/small IPC medians moved only at the noise floor: version baseline mean median ~0.093ms vs in-place trim ~0.093ms; commands ~0.089ms vs ~0.071ms; status worsened/noised from ~0.076ms to ~0.090ms.
- Large-output/lifecycle metrics were mixed: `tabs` median worsened slightly on average (~0.534ms baseline vs ~0.558ms patched), while active-close median improved slightly (~13.06ms vs ~12.76ms).
- The signals are inconsistent across adjacent IPC metrics and too small relative to UI-thread work, response JSON building, and browser lifecycle costs.
- A broad `make benchmark` attempt on the patched build was terminated during the local suite. After reverting/rebuilding/reinstalling the clean source, `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed.

Validation/safety: patched source built and focused runs used benchmark-launched xenv browser processes with core dumps disabled. Disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/ipc_server.cc`; leave the IPC parsing path unchanged unless future long-command benchmarks show a larger effect.

## Experiment: skip hidden FPS overlay visibility update during timer tick — INCONCLUSIVE/FAILED

Hypothesis: `OnFpsIndicatorUpdateTimer()` runs every 500ms as the shared FPS/audio chrome-status tick. When `showfps=false`, `UpdateFpsIndicator()` still called `fps_indicator_overlay_->SetVisible(false)` on every tick before returning. Skipping that repeated hidden-overlay visibility call, while still applying visibility changes in `SetShowFpsIndicator()`, might reduce background UI work.

Change tested in `src/browser_window.cc`:
- Return early from `UpdateFpsIndicator()` when `show_fps_indicator_` is false without setting overlay visibility.
- Move explicit `fps_indicator_overlay_->SetVisible(show_fps_indicator_)` into `SetShowFpsIndicator()`.

Focused benchmark: 1000-tab active-close IPC benchmark with `showfps=false`; 3 baseline runs vs 4 variant runs. Summary stored in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-fps-hidden-overlay-skip-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean/median | variant values | variant mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| latency_mean_ms | 4.329, 4.264, 3.872 | 4.155 / 4.264 | 4.053, 4.162, 3.943, 4.068 | 4.056 / 4.060 | tiny/noisy improvement |
| latency_median_ms | 3.479, 3.542, 3.356 | 3.459 / 3.479 | 3.434, 3.577, 3.599, 3.515 | 3.531 / 3.546 | worse |
| latency_p95_ms | 7.340, 6.888, 5.910 | 6.713 / 6.888 | 6.568, 5.796, 6.061, 6.539 | 6.241 / 6.300 | small/noisy improvement |

Decision: do not keep. The change is logically plausible but not a statistically repeatable broad improvement: median latency regressed and p95/mean improvements are tiny/noisy. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`.

## Experiment 76: append safe JSON string chunks — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: `AppendJsonEscaped()` handled every byte by calling `out.push_back()` for safe characters. Large `tabs` IPC responses are dominated by URL/title JSON strings where most bytes do not need escaping. Appending contiguous safe spans with `std::string::append()` and only branching into escape handling for actual special/control bytes should reduce tab-list JSON serialization overhead while preserving identical escaping.

Change:
- Rework `AppendJsonEscaped()` to scan for JSON-escaped characters (`\\`, `"`, newline, carriage return, tab, and control bytes).
- Append the safe chunk preceding each escaped character in one call.
- Append the trailing safe chunk once after the loop.
- Preserve the same escape sequences for all special/control bytes.

Focused benchmark: 10,000 restored local tabs, 40 direct Unix-socket `tabs` IPC calls per run. Three char-by-char baseline runs vs four chunked-escape runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-json-escape-chunk-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | char-by-char values | baseline mean/median | chunked values | chunked mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `tabs` median ms | 4.087, 4.321, 3.831 | 4.079 / 4.087 | 3.506, 3.869, 3.714, 3.868 | 3.739 / 3.791 | ~8.3% faster mean median |
| `tabs` mean ms | 5.250, 5.316, 4.645 | 5.070 / 5.250 | 4.625, 4.740, 4.719, 4.758 | 4.710 / 4.729 | ~7.1% faster mean |
| `tabs` p95 ms | 8.538, 7.824, 7.336 | 7.899 / 7.824 | 9.320, 7.051, 7.620, 6.777 | 7.691 / 7.335 | slightly faster/noisy |

Maintained IPC-suite spot check on 1000 tabs after the change: `ipc.tabs_ms` median ~0.460ms, p95 ~0.784ms, response bytes 228703. Full suite output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-json-escape-chunk-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 80 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-json-escape-chunk-ipc-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a localized string-builder improvement for JSON serialization and gives a repeatable 10k-tab `tabs` IPC win without changing response shape or escaping semantics.

## Experiment 77: fast-path zero tab refresh rate in JSON — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: each `tabs` IPC row serializes `refresh_rate` through the generic double `std::to_chars()` helper. In restored/background large sessions the per-tab compositor refresh rate is usually `0`, so avoiding double formatting for that common case should reduce tab-list JSON serialization overhead while emitting the same JSON token (`0`).

Change:
- In `AppendTabJson()`, when `refresh_rate == 0.0`, append `'0'` directly.
- Fall back to `AppendJsonNumber(out, refresh_rate)` for non-zero rates.

Focused benchmark: after Experiment 76's chunked JSON escaping, 10,000 restored local tabs, 40 direct Unix-socket `tabs` IPC calls per run. Four chunked-escape baseline runs vs four zero-refresh fast-path runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-json-zero-refresh-summary.jsonl`.

| `tabs` mean ms | 4.625, 4.740, 4.719, 4.758 | 4.710 / 4.729 | 4.381, 4.418, 4.693, 4.583 | 4.519 / 4.501 | ~4.1% faster mean |
| `tabs` p90 ms | 5.993, 6.369, 4.954, 5.890 | 5.802 / 5.941 | 5.427, 5.466, 5.529, 5.266 | 5.422 / 5.446 | ~6.6% faster mean p90 |
| `tabs` p95 ms | 9.320, 7.051, 7.620, 6.777 | 7.692 / 7.335 | 8.172, 7.345, 8.281, 8.806 | 8.151 / 8.226 | noisy/worse p95 |

Maintained IPC-suite spot check on 1000 tabs after the change: `ipc.tabs_ms` median ~0.448ms, mean ~0.493ms, p95 ~0.747ms, response bytes 228703. Full suite output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-json-zero-refresh-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`

- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a narrow common-case serialization fast path with repeatable median/mean/p90 wins in the 10k-tab `tabs` benchmark and unchanged response shape.

## Experiment 78: append JSON booleans with known literal lengths — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: `AppendJsonBool()` used `out += value ? "true" : "false"`. That leaves `std::string` to determine the literal length for every boolean. Large `tabs` IPC responses contain several booleans per tab, mostly `false`; appending the known literal lengths directly should shave a small amount from JSON serialization.

Change:
- Replace the ternary `out += ...` in `AppendJsonBool()` with an explicit branch:
  - `out.append("true", 4)`
  - `out.append("false", 5)`

Focused benchmark: after chunked JSON escaping and the zero-refresh-rate fast path, 10,000 restored local tabs, 40 direct Unix-socket `tabs` IPC calls per run. Four zero-refresh baseline runs vs four bool-literal runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-json-bool-literal-summary.jsonl`.

| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `tabs` median ms | 3.685, 3.396, 3.806, 3.480 | 3.592 / 3.583 | 3.438, 3.495, 3.178, 3.228 | 3.335 / 3.333 | ~7.2% faster mean median |
| `tabs` mean ms | 4.381, 4.418, 4.693, 4.583 | 4.519 / 4.501 | 4.497, 4.448, 4.305, 4.174 | 4.356 / 4.377 | ~3.6% faster mean |
| `tabs` p90 ms | 5.427, 5.466, 5.529, 5.266 | 5.422 / 5.446 | 5.294, 5.210, 5.135, 4.322 | 4.990 / 5.173 | ~8.0% faster mean p90 |
| `tabs` p95 ms | 8.172, 7.345, 8.281, 8.806 | 8.151 / 8.226 | 7.231, 7.424, 7.514, 7.205 | 7.344 / 7.328 | ~9.9% faster mean p95 |

Maintained IPC-suite spot check on 1000 tabs after the change: `ipc.tabs_ms` median ~0.437ms, mean ~0.491ms, p95 ~0.756ms, response bytes 228703. Full suite output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-json-bool-literal-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

## Experiment 79: append tab JSON field literals with known lengths — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: `AppendTabJson()` uses many `out += "literal"` calls for fixed JSON field names. Replacing those with a small `AppendJsonLiteral()` helper that appends compile-time literal lengths could avoid repeated `char_traits::length()` scans and reduce large tab-list JSON serialization overhead.

Change tested:
- Add `template <size_t N> AppendJsonLiteral(std::string&, const char (&)[N])`.
- Use it for fixed field-name fragments in `AppendTabJson()` and for the `null` FPS literal.
- Keep output shape unchanged.

Focused benchmark: after Experiments 76–78, 10,000 restored local tabs, 40 direct Unix-socket `tabs` IPC calls per run. Four bool-literal baseline runs vs three field-literal runs; a fourth patched run failed to become IPC-ready and was excluded. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-json-field-literal-append-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Median regressed: bool-literal baseline mean median ~3.335ms vs field-literal append ~3.501ms.
- Mean regressed slightly/noisily: ~4.356ms baseline vs ~4.457ms patched.
- p90/p95 were mixed and noisy: p90 ~4.990ms baseline vs ~5.278ms patched; p95 ~7.344ms baseline vs ~7.567ms patched.
- The compiler/std::string path for `operator+=` on literals is already good enough here; the helper added code churn without a repeatable win.

Validation/safety: patched source built and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed. After reverting, source rebuilt/reinstalled and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed again. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; retain the simpler literal append style.

## Experiment 80: read shell IPC responses in larger chunks — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` is the user-facing shell client for the local IPC protocol. For large responses such as `tabs` with 10,000 tabs, it received the response in 4096-byte chunks and also half-closed the socket after sending even though the server dispatches once it sees the newline. The maintained in-process benchmark client already uses much larger response reads. Increasing the shell client receive size and skipping the extra shutdown syscall should reduce end-to-end shell command latency without changing protocol output.

Change:
- In `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc::send_command()`, remove `sock.shutdown(socket.SHUT_WR)` after sending the newline-terminated command.
- Increase response `recv()` chunk size from 4096 bytes to 262144 bytes.
- The server already stops reading once it sees `\n`, so EOF is not required to dispatch commands.

Focused benchmark: launch a 10,000 restored-tab browser with the benchmark harness, then run the actual `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc tabs` subprocess 25 times per run. Three baseline runs vs four patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-client-read-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean/median | patched values | patched mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| shell `tabs` median ms | 33.084, 32.670, 33.347 | 33.034 / 33.084 | 32.280, 32.162, 32.362, 32.087 | 32.223 / 32.221 | ~2.5% faster mean median |
| shell `tabs` mean ms | 34.386, 33.425, 34.313 | 34.041 / 34.313 | 33.001, 33.103, 33.086, 33.139 | 33.082 / 33.095 | ~2.8% faster mean |
| shell `tabs` p95 ms | 39.221, 36.899, 40.254 | 38.792 / 39.221 | 34.035, 37.299, 35.225, 38.317 | 36.219 / 36.262 | ~6.6% faster mean p95 |

Additional isolated check with only the 262144-byte receive chunk while retaining shutdown also improved the shell `tabs` median to ~32.54ms mean, so most of the win comes from fewer Python/socket receive iterations; skipping shutdown adds a small extra syscall reduction.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` on the patched script => CHECK: PASS
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This improves the real shell IPC client for large responses with unchanged output bytes and no app runtime change.

## Experiment 81: stream shell IPC responses to stdout — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after Experiment 80, `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` read large responses in fewer chunks but still accumulated all response chunks into a Python list, joined them into one bytes object, then wrote that bytes object to stdout. For regular non-screenshot commands, the client can stream each received response chunk directly to `sys.stdout.buffer` without changing output bytes or exit behavior. This should reduce memory copying/allocation for large responses such as `tabs`.

Change:
- Keep `send_command()` as a buffered helper for screenshot handling, where JSON parsing/error handling still needs the whole response.
- Add `stream_command(socket_path, command, output)` that sends the same newline-terminated command and writes each 262144-byte response chunk directly to the given output stream.
- Use `stream_command(..., sys.stdout.buffer)` for normal CLI commands in `main()`.

Focused benchmark: launch a 10,000 restored-tab browser with the benchmark harness, then run the actual `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc tabs` subprocess 25 times per run. Four Experiment-80 buffered-large-read runs vs four streaming runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-stream-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | buffered large-read values | buffered mean/median | streaming values | streaming mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| shell `tabs` median ms | 32.280, 32.162, 32.362, 32.087 | 32.223 / 32.221 | 30.082, 30.424, 30.048, 29.918 | 30.118 / 30.065 | ~6.5% faster mean median |
| shell `tabs` mean ms | 33.001, 33.103, 33.086, 33.139 | 33.082 / 33.095 | 30.886, 31.273, 30.941, 30.734 | 30.959 / 30.914 | ~6.4% faster mean |
| shell `tabs` p95 ms | 34.035, 37.299, 35.225, 38.317 | 36.219 / 36.262 | 33.215, 35.138, 33.772, 33.166 | 33.823 / 33.493 | ~6.6% faster mean p95 |

Relative to the original 4K-buffer + shutdown shell client baseline from Experiment 80, the combined large-read + streaming path reduces 10k-tab shell `tabs` mean median from ~33.03ms to ~30.12ms (~8.8% faster).

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` on the patched script => CHECK: PASS
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This improves the real shell IPC client large-response path while retaining buffered behavior for screenshot-specific JSON parsing.

## Experiment 82: cache escaped tab URL JSON — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after the JSON string-builder wins, the large `tabs` IPC path still re-escaped every stored tab URL on every request. Restored tabs usually have stable non-empty URLs, so caching the already-quoted/escaped URL JSON when the tab URL changes should move that cost from repeated `tabs` calls to infrequent URL mutation/open/load paths.

Change:
- Add `Tab::url_json` beside `Tab::url`.
- Add `SetTabUrl(Tab&, std::string)` to update `tab.url` and rebuild the cached quoted URL string with `AppendJsonString()`.
- Replace direct URL assignments in tab creation, load start, undo-close placeholder reuse, clipboard open, and IPC `open <tabid> <url>` paths with `SetTabUrl()`.
- In `AppendTabJson()`, append `tab.url_json` directly when `tab.url` is non-empty; retain the existing live-frame fallback path for empty stored URLs.

Focused benchmark: 10,000 restored local tabs, 40 direct Unix-socket `tabs` IPC calls per run. Three fresh baseline runs vs four cached-URL runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-url-json-cache-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | baseline values | baseline mean/median | cached values | cached mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `tabs` median ms | 3.233, 3.465, 3.069 | 3.256 / 3.233 | 2.873, 2.654, 2.634, 2.969 | 2.782 / 2.763 | ~14.6% faster mean median |
| `tabs` mean ms | 4.415, 4.128, 4.086 | 4.210 / 4.128 | 3.874, 3.341, 3.682, 3.868 | 3.691 / 3.775 | ~12.3% faster mean |
| `tabs` p90 ms | 6.000, 4.623, 4.825 | 5.149 / 4.825 | 4.381, 3.799, 4.445, 4.144 | 4.192 / 4.262 | ~18.6% faster mean p90 |
| `tabs` p95 ms | 7.210, 7.816, 6.964 | 7.330 / 7.210 | 6.488, 6.874, 5.810, 7.777 | 6.738 / 6.681 | ~8.1% faster mean p95 |
| startup IPC ms | 449.7, 431.2, 429.8 | 436.9 / 431.2 | 429.2, 451.0, 431.8, 446.9 | 439.7 / 439.4 | neutral/noisy |

Maintained IPC-suite spot check on 1000 tabs after the change: `ipc.tabs_ms` median ~0.363ms, mean ~0.408ms, p95 ~0.636ms, response bytes 228703. Full suite output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-url-json-cache-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 80 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-url-json-cache-ipc-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a repeatable large-tab-list serialization win with unchanged response bytes and only a small cached-string memory/copy tradeoff on stored-tab URL updates.

## Experiment 84: lazy-load screenshot-only IPC client imports — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` is invoked as a fresh Python process for every shell IPC command. It imported `json` and `base64` at startup even though normal commands only stream bytes from the Unix socket to stdout; those modules are needed only for screenshot post-processing. Moving the imports into the screenshot path should reduce normal shell IPC latency without changing command output.

Change:
- Remove top-level `import base64` and `import json` from `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`.
- Import both modules lazily inside `maybe_handle_screenshot()` only after a successful non-JSON screenshot response needs decoding.
- Leave normal streaming command behavior from Experiment 81 unchanged.

Focused benchmark: launch a 1000 restored-tab browser with the benchmark harness, then run the actual `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc status` subprocess 60 times per run. Three baseline runs vs four lazy-import runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-lazy-import-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | eager-import values | eager mean/median | lazy-import values | lazy mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| shell `status` median ms | 25.960, 25.798, 26.234 | 25.998 / 25.960 | 23.550, 24.363, 24.289, 23.643 | 23.961 / 23.966 | ~7.8% faster mean median |
| shell `status` mean ms | 28.511, 25.931, 26.771 | 27.071 / 26.771 | 23.671, 24.714, 24.727, 23.832 | 24.236 / 24.272 | ~10.5% faster mean |
| shell `status` p95 ms | 32.073, 27.727, 30.975 | 30.258 / 30.975 | 24.717, 28.103, 27.369, 26.736 | 26.731 / 27.053 | ~11.7% faster mean p95 |

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a small safe shell-client startup improvement for all normal IPC commands, while screenshot keeps the same imports on its specialized path.

## Experiment 85: avoid pathlib import on normal IPC client path — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after lazy-loading screenshot-only imports, normal `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` commands still imported `pathlib.Path` at startup just to construct the socket path. That is convenient but expensive for a short-lived Python CLI. Replacing normal socket-path construction with `os.path` strings and importing `Path` only inside the screenshot path should further reduce shell IPC startup overhead.

Change:
- Remove top-level `from pathlib import Path` from `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`.
- Return string socket paths from `default_socket_path()` using `os.path.join()` / `os.path.exists()`.
- Keep screenshot-specific `Path` usage by importing `Path` lazily inside `maybe_handle_screenshot()` after confirming the command is `screenshot`.
- Keep normal command streaming behavior from Experiment 81 and lazy screenshot `json`/`base64` imports from Experiment 84.

Focused benchmark: launch a 1000 restored-tab browser with the benchmark harness, then run the actual `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc status` subprocess 60 times per run. Four lazy-import/pathlib baseline runs vs four no-pathlib runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-lazy-path-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | lazy-import/pathlib values | baseline mean/median | no-pathlib values | no-pathlib mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| shell `status` median ms | 23.550, 24.363, 24.289, 23.643 | 23.961 / 23.966 | 16.623, 16.468, 16.282, 16.608 | 16.495 / 16.538 | ~31.2% faster mean median |
| shell `status` mean ms | 23.671, 24.714, 24.727, 23.832 | 24.236 / 24.272 | 18.309, 16.714, 16.647, 16.966 | 17.159 / 16.840 | ~29.2% faster mean |
| shell `status` p95 ms | 24.717, 28.103, 27.369, 26.736 | 26.731 / 27.053 | 29.997, 18.523, 17.902, 19.956 | 21.595 / 19.239 | ~19.2% faster mean p95 despite one noisy run |

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This substantially reduces normal shell IPC process startup overhead while preserving `Path` behavior on the screenshot-only path.

## Experiment 86: remove IPC client function annotations — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: after removing normal-path imports from `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`, function annotations (`-> str`, `-> bytes`, `argv: list[str]`, etc.) are still evaluated/stored during every short-lived shell client startup. Removing them might shave a small amount from normal `vimbrowser-ipc status` process latency.

Change tested:
- Remove function argument/return annotations from `default_socket_path()`, `send_command()`, `stream_command()`, `maybe_handle_screenshot()`, and `main()`.

Focused benchmark: launch a 1000 restored-tab browser with the benchmark harness, then run the actual `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc status` subprocess 60 times per run. Four annotated no-pathlib baseline runs vs four no-annotation runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-no-annotations-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Median was neutral/slightly worse: annotated mean median ~16.495ms vs no-annotations ~16.526ms.
- Mean improved only slightly/noisily: ~17.159ms vs ~16.831ms.
- Tail metrics improved in this sample, but the baseline had one noisy p95 run; the median did not improve.
- The readability/API-documentation loss is not justified by the mixed tiny signal.

Validation/safety: patched script passed `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source`. After reverting, `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed again. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; keep the annotations after the larger import/path optimizations.

## Experiment 87: add maintained shell IPC benchmark metrics — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: the recent shell-client wins were measured with temporary focused harnesses that launched a benchmark-managed browser and repeatedly invoked the real `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` subprocess. Folding that measurement into the maintained `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc` suite will make future shell/runtime experiments safer and less ad hoc while keeping the default direct-IPC suite unchanged.

Change:
- Add `measure_shell_ipc()` to run the real `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` CLI under the benchmark profile environment and time subprocess end-to-end latency with byte output capture.
- Add `--ipc-shell-iterations` (default `0`) so shell-client metrics are opt-in and existing IPC benchmark/runtime remains unchanged by default.
- When enabled, record `ipc.shell_status_ms`, `ipc.shell_tabs_ms`, and their response byte counts alongside the existing direct IPC metrics.

Validation smoke: `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 50 --ipc-iterations 3 --ipc-moved-tabs 2 --ipc-shell-iterations 3 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-benchmark-extension-smoke.json`

Smoke metrics showed the new shell measurements working against the real client: `ipc.shell_status_ms` median ~17.3ms with 283 response bytes; `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median ~16.7ms with 11407 response bytes. Existing direct IPC metrics were still emitted.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark` then removed generated `scripts/__pycache__`.
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS.
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is not an app hot-path change, but it makes future shell IPC performance exploration repeatable through the maintained safe benchmark harness without changing default benchmark behavior.


## Benchmark infrastructure: optional shell IPC client metrics in IPC suite

Goal: recent wins moved from in-process/browser IPC work into `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` shell-client overhead. The maintained IPC suite measured direct Unix-socket commands but had no repeatable metric for full subprocess shell-client latency, so shell-client regressions and wins were being measured only by ad-hoc scripts.

Change:
- Add `measure_shell_ipc()` to `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`.
- Add `--ipc-shell-iterations` (default 0, disabled for existing benchmark/runtime cost).
- When enabled for `--suite ipc`, record:
  - `ipc.shell_status_ms` / `ipc.shell_status_response_bytes`
  - `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` / `ipc.shell_tabs_response_bytes`
- Keep the existing direct IPC metrics unchanged when the option is not supplied.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`
- Smoke run: `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 50 --ipc-iterations 5 --ipc-moved-tabs 2 --ipc-shell-iterations 3 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-benchmark-extension-smoke.json`
  - Included `ipc.shell_status_ms` median ~17.30ms and `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median ~16.75ms.
- 1000-tab opt-in run saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-suite-shell-client-1000.{json,txt}`: `ipc.shell_status_ms` median ~16.49ms, `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median ~16.74ms.
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk checks before/after: `/` stayed ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit as benchmark infrastructure. It is opt-in, preserves existing suite cost by default, and makes future shell/backend performance changes easier to validate consistently.

## Experiment 88: run IPC client without Python site startup — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after removing normal-path imports, the remaining `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` startup cost is dominated by the Python interpreter itself. The script does not need user/site package initialization for normal socket forwarding, so using the system Python directly with `-S` should skip `site` startup and reduce every shell IPC command's subprocess overhead. A plain absolute `/usr/bin/python3` shebang was also checked to separate PATH lookup from `site` startup.

Change:
- Change `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` shebang from `#!/usr/bin/env python3` to `#!/usr/bin/python3 -S`.
- No protocol or Python code changes; screenshot-specific lazy imports from Experiments 84/85 still work on the specialized path.

Focused benchmark: launch a 1000 restored-tab browser with the benchmark harness, then run the actual `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc status` subprocess 60 times per run. Four env-python/no-pathlib baseline runs vs four `/usr/bin/python3` runs vs four `/usr/bin/python3 -S` runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-nosite-shebang-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | env python baseline | baseline mean/median | `/usr/bin/python3 -S` values | no-site mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| shell `status` median ms | 16.623, 16.468, 16.282, 16.608 | 16.495 / 16.538 | 14.342, 14.159, 14.270, 13.971 | 14.185 / 14.215 | ~14.0% faster mean median |
| shell `status` mean ms | 18.309, 16.714, 16.647, 16.966 | 17.159 / 16.840 | 14.607, 14.675, 14.508, 14.384 | 14.543 / 14.558 | ~15.2% faster mean |
| shell `status` p95 ms | 29.997, 18.523, 17.902, 19.956 | 21.595 / 19.240 | 16.023, 16.272, 16.751, 16.422 | 16.367 / 16.347 | ~24.2% faster mean p95 |

Intermediate `/usr/bin/python3` without `-S` improved only modestly (mean median ~16.22ms), so most of the final win comes from skipping `site` initialization rather than avoiding `/usr/bin/env` alone.

Maintained IPC-suite shell spot check with `--ipc-shell-iterations 20` after the change:
- `ipc.shell_status_ms` median ~15.46ms, mean ~15.62ms, p95 ~16.95ms.
- `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median ~14.65ms, mean ~14.66ms, p95 ~15.42ms.
- Full output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-nosite-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 40 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --ipc-shell-iterations 20 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-nosite-ipc-1000.json`
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a repeatable shell-client startup win for the local Linux runtime; the script has no third-party/site dependency and still imports screenshot helpers lazily only when needed.

## Experiment 89: write streamed IPC client output with `os.write` — SUCCESS/MODEST

Hypothesis: after streaming normal `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` responses directly to stdout, the implementation still used `sys.stdout.buffer.write(data)` for each response chunk. For large shell `tabs` responses, writing chunks with `os.write(1, data)` should avoid part of Python's buffered I/O layer and reduce output copying/dispatch overhead.

Change:
- Change `stream_command()` to accept an output file descriptor and call `os.write(output_fd, data)` for each received response chunk.
- Normal CLI commands call `stream_command(socket_path, command, 1)`.
- Screenshot paths still use the buffered helper because they need whole-response parsing/decoding.

Focused benchmark: launch a 10,000 restored-tab browser with the benchmark harness, then run the actual `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc tabs` subprocess 25 times per run. Three `/usr/bin/python3 -S` buffered-stdout baseline runs vs four `os.write` runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-oswrite-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | buffered stdout values | baseline mean/median | `os.write` values | `os.write` mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| shell `tabs` median ms | 18.892, 19.391, 18.781 | 19.022 / 18.892 | 18.680, 18.820, 18.543, 18.410 | 18.613 / 18.611 | ~2.2% faster mean median |
| shell `tabs` mean ms | 21.483, 20.607, 19.304 | 20.465 / 20.607 | 19.137, 19.431, 19.357, 19.173 | 19.275 / 19.247 | ~5.8% faster mean |
| shell `tabs` p95 ms | 27.378, 23.777, 21.822 | 24.326 / 23.777 | 21.268, 20.733, 22.405, 21.333 | 21.435 / 21.300 | ~11.9% faster mean p95 |

Maintained IPC-suite shell spot check with `--ipc-shell-iterations 20` after the change:
- `ipc.shell_status_ms` median ~14.16ms, mean ~14.18ms, p95 ~14.66ms.
- `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median ~14.32ms, mean ~14.39ms, p95 ~15.03ms.
- Full output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-oswrite-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 40 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --ipc-shell-iterations 20 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-oswrite-ipc-1000.json`
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a small localized shell-client output-path improvement that is most visible for large streamed responses and leaves screenshot handling unchanged.

## Experiment 90: guard screenshot helper call in IPC client — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: after the normal `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` path was reduced to a small Python `-S` socket forwarder, even the per-invocation `maybe_handle_screenshot()` function call and argument check might be measurable. Checking for `sys.argv[1] == "screenshot"` in `main()` before calling the helper could avoid that call on all normal commands while preserving screenshot behavior.

Change tested:
- In `main()`, call `maybe_handle_screenshot()` only when the first argument is exactly `screenshot`.
- Leave screenshot handling and normal command streaming otherwise unchanged.

Focused benchmark: launched a 1000 restored-tab browser with the benchmark harness and ran the real `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc status` subprocess 60 times per run. Three baseline runs vs four guarded-helper runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-screenshot-guard-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Baseline medians: 13.798, 13.923, 14.082ms (mean median ~13.935ms).
- Guarded-helper medians: 13.993, 14.021, 14.025, 14.039ms (mean median ~14.019ms).
- Mean and p95 were also neutral/slightly worse. The avoided function call is lost in interpreter startup and subprocess/socket overhead.

Validation/safety: patched script passed `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; focused runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths with browser core dumps disabled. Disk stayed at `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; the explicit helper call is simpler and not a measurable bottleneck.

## Experiment 91: single-argument IPC client command fast path — FAILED

Hypothesis: normal `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc status` and `tabs` invocations pass exactly one command argument, but `main()` always builds the command with `" ".join(sys.argv[1:])`. Special-casing `len(sys.argv) == 2` to use `sys.argv[1]` directly might avoid a slice/join on the hot shell-client path.

Change tested:
- In `main()`, add `elif len(sys.argv) == 2: command = sys.argv[1]`; keep the join for multi-word commands.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with `--ipc-shell-iterations 40`, 1000 restored tabs, before/after one run each. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-single-arg-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- `ipc.shell_status_ms` median regressed from ~14.10ms to ~15.33ms; mean regressed from ~14.20ms to ~16.27ms; p95 regressed from ~14.99ms to ~22.15ms.
- `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median regressed from ~14.56ms to ~15.99ms; mean regressed from ~14.61ms to ~17.51ms; p95 regressed from ~15.24ms to ~26.15ms.
- The change is theoretically tiny and the run was noisy, but it clearly did not produce a repeatable win.

Validation/safety: patched script passed `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source`. After reverting, `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed again. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; retain the simpler join path.

## Experiment 92: reserve enough space for large tabs JSON — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after per-tab URL JSON caching, a 10,000-tab `tabs` response is about 2.33MB, but `TabsJson()` still reserves only `96 + tabs_.size() * 220` bytes. The maintained 10k IPC response is about 232.6 bytes per tab, so the old reserve underestimates and can force at least one growth/copy near the end of serialization. Raising the estimate to 240 bytes per tab should avoid that growth for current large restored sessions with a small memory-overreserve tradeoff.

Change:
- In `BrowserWindow::TabsJson()`, change `out.reserve(96 + tabs_.size() * 220)` to `out.reserve(96 + tabs_.size() * 240)`.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 10,000 restored tabs and 40 direct IPC iterations per metric. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabs-json-reserve-summary.jsonl`; patched full output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabs-json-reserve-ipc-10000.{json,txt}`.

| metric | 220-byte reserve | 240-byte reserve | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.tabs_ms` median | ~2.48ms | ~2.20ms | ~11.3% faster |
| `ipc.tabs_ms` mean | ~2.74ms | ~2.51ms | ~8.5% faster |
| `ipc.tabs_ms` p95 | ~3.79ms | ~3.99ms | noisy/slightly worse tail |
| `ipc.tab_order_active_ms` median | ~4.08ms | ~3.81ms | ~6.6% faster |
| `ipc.tab_order_active_ms` mean | ~4.34ms | ~3.96ms | ~8.8% faster |
| `ipc.open_active_ms` median | ~6.15ms | ~6.29ms | neutral/slightly worse |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 10000 --ipc-iterations 40 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output /tmp/tabs-json-reserve-patched-ipc.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a localized serialization reserve tweak that makes large current `tabs`/tab-order JSON responses less likely to reallocate; the slightly higher reserve is still close to observed response size.

## Experiment 93: fast-path restored tab JSON rows — SUCCESS
