Hypothesis: in large restored sessions, most tabs are not backed by a live CEF browser yet but do have a stored URL and cached URL JSON. `AppendTabJson()` still executed the generic path: initialize per-row temporary strings/state, check browser/client fields, and append every default false/null/zero field separately. A restored-tab fast path can emit the same JSON row directly for `!tab.client && !tab.url.empty()` without generic browser-state work.

Change:
- In `BrowserWindow::AppendTabJson()`, add an early path for restored URL-only tabs (`!tab.client && !tab.url.empty()`).
- Emit the same fields/values as the generic path: id/index/tab/active/audible, cached URL JSON, empty title, `loading=false`, `can_go_back=false`, `can_go_forward=false`, `fps_has_sample=false`, `fps=null`, `refresh_rate=0`.
- Keep the generic path for live tabs, empty-URL fallback, frame URL lookup, titles, loading/back/forward state, and FPS state.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 10,000 restored tabs and 40 direct IPC iterations per metric. Baseline is the committed 240-byte reserve result from Experiment 92. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabjson-restored-fastpath-summary.jsonl`; patched full output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabjson-restored-fastpath-ipc-10000.{json,txt}`.

| metric | generic row path | restored fast path | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.tabs_ms` median | ~2.20ms | ~1.78ms | ~19.1% faster |
| `ipc.tabs_ms` mean | ~2.51ms | ~1.92ms | ~23.6% faster |
| `ipc.tabs_ms` p95 | ~3.99ms | ~2.63ms | ~34.1% faster |
| `ipc.tab_order_active_ms` median | ~3.81ms | ~3.31ms | ~13.1% faster |
| `ipc.tab_order_active_ms` mean | ~3.96ms | ~3.49ms | ~11.9% faster |
| `ipc.open_active_ms` median | ~6.29ms | ~5.20ms | ~17.3% faster |
| `ipc.tab_close_rendered_ms` median | ~1.84ms | ~1.79ms | ~2.6% faster/noisy |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 10000 --ipc-iterations 40 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output /tmp/tabjson-restored-fastpath-ipc.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a repeatable large-restored-session JSON serialization win with unchanged response bytes and unchanged live-tab behavior.

## Experiment 94: literal active/audible booleans in restored tab JSON — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: after the restored-tab JSON fast path, the `active` and `audible` fields still used `AppendJsonBool()` calls per row. Since restored large-session rows are almost always inactive and not audible, emitting the common `,"active":false,"audible":false` literal directly (with small branches for active/audible exceptions) might shave a little more from large `tabs` serialization.

Change tested:
- In the restored-tab fast path inside `AppendTabJson()`, replace the two separate `AppendJsonBool()` calls for `active` and `audible` with three literal cases:
  - active row: `,"active":true,"audible":...`
  - audible inactive row: `,"active":false,"audible":true`
  - common inactive/silent row: `,"active":false,"audible":false`

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 10,000 restored tabs and 40 direct IPC iterations per metric. One committed restored-fast-path baseline run vs two literal-bool patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tabjson-bool-literals-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- `ipc.tabs_ms` median improved in the patched samples (~1.64ms and ~1.73ms vs baseline ~1.78ms), but mean was essentially neutral (~1.91ms and ~1.85ms vs baseline ~1.92ms) and p95 was mixed (one worse ~3.77ms, one better ~2.50ms vs baseline ~2.63ms).
- `ipc.tab_order_active_ms` median/mean improved in both patched runs, but `open_active` and rendered-close metrics were mixed/noisy.
- The tiny median signal is not strong enough to justify extra branchy special-case code on top of the restored-row fast path.

Validation/safety: patched source built, slimmed/installed, and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed. After reverting, source rebuilt/slimmed/installed and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed again. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; retain the clearer restored-tab fast path with regular `AppendJsonBool()` calls.

## Experiment 95: move IPC command into UI task — FAILED

Hypothesis: `IpcServer::HandleClient()` builds a command string on the IPC server thread, then passes it by lvalue to `IpcCommandTask`, copying the command. Passing `std::move(command)` into the task should avoid one string copy per IPC request. The command is not used after `CefPostTask()`, so this should be semantically safe.

Change tested:
- In `IpcServer::HandleClient()`, call `new IpcCommandTask(owner_, std::move(command), pending)` instead of passing `command` by lvalue.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 80 direct IPC iterations and 20 shell iterations. One fresh baseline run vs one patched run. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-command-move-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Tiny direct metrics were mixed, with `commands` improving but `status` worsening relative to the fresh baseline.
- Larger lifecycle metrics regressed/noisy in the patched run: `open_active` median ~2.04ms baseline vs ~2.29ms patched; active close median ~12.14ms baseline vs ~13.20ms patched.
- Shell metrics also regressed: shell `status` median ~13.85ms baseline vs ~14.86ms patched; shell `tabs` median ~14.21ms baseline vs ~14.60ms patched.
- Avoiding this small command-string copy is not visible as a reliable win and may perturb scheduling/noise more than it helps.

Validation/safety: patched source built, slimmed/installed, and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed. After reverting, source rebuilt/slimmed/installed and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed again. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/ipc_server.cc`; retain the lvalue copy path.

## Experiment 96: cache tab id JSON — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after cached URL JSON and the restored-tab row fast path, 10,000-row `tabs` responses still format each stable tab id with `std::to_chars()` on every request. Tab ids are assigned once at tab creation and never change, so caching the JSON decimal string beside the raw id should move that formatting cost from repeated tab-list serialization to infrequent tab creation.

Change:
- Add `Tab::id_json` beside `Tab::id`.
- Add `SetTabId(Tab&, uint64_t)` to set both the raw id and cached JSON decimal string.
- Use `SetTabId()` in normal tab insertion and popup tab insertion.
- In `AppendTabJson()`, append `tab.id_json` for restored-tab fast-path rows, and use it in the generic path when present with a fallback to `AppendJsonNumber(out, tab.id)` for defensive compatibility.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 10,000 restored tabs and 40 direct IPC iterations per metric. Baseline is the committed restored-row fast-path result from Experiment 93. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tab-id-json-summary.jsonl`; patched full output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tab-id-json-ipc-10000.{json,txt}`.

| metric | format id each request | cached id JSON | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.tabs_ms` median | ~1.78ms | ~1.65ms | ~7.0% faster |
| `ipc.tabs_ms` mean | ~1.92ms | ~1.74ms | ~9.4% faster |
| `ipc.tabs_ms` p95 | ~2.63ms | ~2.27ms | ~13.6% faster |
| `ipc.tab_order_active_ms` median | ~3.31ms | ~3.26ms | ~1.4% faster |
| `ipc.tab_order_active_ms` mean | ~3.49ms | ~3.42ms | ~2.0% faster |
| `ipc.open_active_ms` median | ~5.20ms | ~5.14ms | ~1.1% faster |

Validation:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 10000 --ipc-iterations 40 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output /tmp/tab-id-json-ipc-10000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a small per-tab memory tradeoff for a repeated large-tab-list serialization win with unchanged response bytes.

## Experiment 97: connect IPC client with string socket path directly — SUCCESS/MODEST

Hypothesis: after the normal `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` path was converted from `Path` objects to plain strings, `send_command()` and `stream_command()` still called `sock.connect(str(socket_path))`. On the hot normal path, `socket_path` is already a string, so the extra `str()` call is unnecessary per subprocess invocation.

Change:
- Replace `sock.connect(str(socket_path))` with `sock.connect(socket_path)` in both buffered screenshot command sending and normal streamed command sending.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 20 direct IPC iterations and 40 shell iterations. One fresh `str()` baseline run vs two direct-string patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-shell-ipc-connect-path-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | `str(socket_path)` baseline | direct string patched | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` median | ~14.63ms | ~14.00ms, ~13.94ms | ~4.5% faster patched mean median |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` mean | ~14.79ms | ~14.13ms, ~14.06ms | ~4.7% faster patched mean |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median | ~16.42ms | ~14.28ms, ~14.24ms | baseline was noisy, patched consistently lower |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` mean | ~17.64ms | ~14.41ms, ~14.36ms | baseline was noisy, patched consistently lower |

Direct in-process IPC metrics were not the target and stayed noisy/mixed. The shell-client improvement is small and near subprocess noise, but the code change removes now-redundant work and preserves identical behavior for string socket paths.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a trivial normal-path cleanup with modest shell-client benchmark support after the earlier Path-to-string conversion.

## Follow-up: apply tab id JSON cache source change — CORRECTION/SUCCESS

Experiment 96 recorded the successful tab-id JSON cache benchmark, but the source change was left out of that commit during an interrupted run. This follow-up applies the exact intended runtime change:
- Add `Tab::id_json`.
- Add `SetTabId(Tab&, uint64_t)` to populate both the raw id and cached decimal JSON string.
- Use `SetTabId()` in normal and popup tab insertion.
- Append `tab.id_json` in the restored-row fast path and in the generic path when present, with a defensive fallback to `AppendJsonNumber(out, tab.id)`.

Validation after applying the source change:
- `cmake --build build-source -j12`
- `./scripts/slim-cef-runtime.sh build-source/Release`
- `make BUILD_DIR=build-source install-wrapper`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 10000 --ipc-iterations 20 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tab-id-json-source-verify-ipc-10000.json`
  - `ipc.tabs_ms` median ~1.89ms, response bytes unchanged at 2,325,709.
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Disk/coredump checks stayed safe (`/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps 4.0G).

Decision: keep. This makes the source tree match the already logged successful Experiment 96 result.

## Experiment 98: create IPC client sockets with SOCK_CLOEXEC — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: Python sockets are non-inheritable by default, but creating the Unix socket with `socket.SOCK_STREAM | socket.SOCK_CLOEXEC` might avoid an extra close-on-exec setup step and reduce short-lived `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` startup overhead.

Change tested:
- In both `send_command()` and `stream_command()`, create sockets with `socket.socket(socket.AF_UNIX, socket.SOCK_STREAM | socket.SOCK_CLOEXEC)` instead of `socket.SOCK_STREAM`.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 20 direct IPC iterations and 40 shell iterations. Two default-socket baseline runs vs two explicit-`SOCK_CLOEXEC` runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-sock-cloexec-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Shell `status` median was mixed/slightly worse: default medians ~13.89ms and ~14.42ms (mean ~14.16ms) vs explicit `SOCK_CLOEXEC` ~15.36ms and ~13.99ms (mean ~14.68ms).
- Shell `tabs` median was also mixed/slightly worse: default ~14.34ms and ~14.42ms (mean ~14.38ms) vs patched ~15.97ms and ~14.31ms (mean ~15.14ms).
- Direct IPC metrics were not the target and were noisy/mixed.
- Python already handles non-inheritable sockets efficiently enough here; explicit `SOCK_CLOEXEC` does not produce a repeatable shell-client win.

Validation/safety: patched script passed `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source`. After reverting, `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed again. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; retain plain `socket.SOCK_STREAM`.

## Experiment 99: reuse cached URL JSON in status response — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: `IpcStatusJson()` still copied the active tab URL into a local string, escaped it on every status request, and formatted zero refresh rate through the double `AppendJsonNumber()` path. Reusing `active_tab->url_json` and fast-pathing zero refresh rate might reduce `status`/shell `status` latency after the tab URL JSON cache work.

Change tested:
- Keep a `const Tab* active_tab` instead of copying `tab.url` into a local `std::string`.
- Reserve using `active_tab->url.size()`.
- Append `active_tab->url_json` directly when the active tab has a stored URL; otherwise append the empty JSON string.
- Emit `refresh_rate` as literal `0` when the sampled refresh rate is zero.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs. Two baseline runs vs two patched runs; summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-status-json-cache-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Direct `ipc.status_ms` median was mixed: baseline mean median ~0.087ms vs patched mean median ~0.072ms, but means and p95 were noisy and changed with iteration counts/run variance.
- Shell `status` did not improve clearly: baseline mean median ~14.16ms vs patched ~14.14ms, essentially neutral at subprocess scale.
- Other direct IPC metrics were noisy/mixed, and the code shape adds special cases to a non-bottleneck path.

Validation/safety: patched source built, slimmed/installed, and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed. After reverting, source rebuilt/slimmed/installed and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed again. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; keep the simpler status JSON builder.

## Experiment 99: use cached URL JSON in status response — FAILED

Hypothesis: `IpcStatusJson()` still copied the active tab URL to a local string and escaped it for every `status` response. Since tabs now maintain cached URL JSON, status could keep a pointer to the active tab, reserve from its URL size, append `active_tab->url_json`, and fast-path zero refresh-rate output.

Change tested:
- Replace the local copied `url` string with `const Tab* active_tab`.
- Append `active_tab->url_json` when present instead of calling `AppendJsonString(out, url)`.
- Emit literal `0` for the common zero `refresh_rate` case.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs and 200 direct IPC iterations per run. Three baseline runs vs three patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-status-url-json-cache-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | baseline median values | baseline mean median | patched median values | patched mean median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.status_ms` median | 0.0502, 0.0628, 0.0617 | 0.0582ms | 0.0547, 0.0612, 0.0630 | 0.0596ms | slightly worse |
| `ipc.tabs_ms` median | 0.2301, 0.2327, 0.2331 | 0.2320ms | 0.2355, 0.2476, 0.2457 | 0.2429ms | worse collateral/noise |
| `ipc.tab_focus_nearby_ms` median | 0.8410, 0.8650, 0.8243 | 0.8434ms | 0.8846, 0.8512, 0.8549 | 0.8636ms | worse/noisy |

Decision: do not keep. The status path is already too small for the cached-URL branch/pointer rewrite to help; it measured slightly worse. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`.

## Experiment 100: move resolved IPC open URLs into tab records — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: the IPC `open-tab` and `open` paths resolved the requested text into a `std::string url`, then copied that URL into the new/existing `Tab` even though the local variable was not needed afterward. Moving the resolved URL into the tab record and loading from `tab.url` should avoid one string allocation/copy per IPC open command, with identical tab state and browser navigation semantics.

Change tested:
- In `HandleIpcCommand()`, make the resolved `url` mutable for `open-tab` and `open`.
- Call `AddTab(std::move(url), true)` for `open-tab`.
- Call `SetTabUrl(tab, std::move(url))` for existing-tab `open`, then `LoadURL(tab.url)` so the browser receives the moved stored URL.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 80 direct IPC iterations and the built-in 40 active-open iterations. One fresh baseline run vs two patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-open-url-move-summary.jsonl`; full outputs saved as `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-open-url-move-{baseline,patched}-ipc-1000-*.{json,txt}`.

| metric | baseline | patched run 1 | patched run 2 | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.open_active_ms` median | ~1.99ms | ~1.93ms | ~2.26ms | mixed/noisy |
| `ipc.open_active_ms` mean | ~2.04ms | ~2.01ms | ~2.37ms | mixed/worse on repeat |
| `ipc.tabs_ms` median | ~0.273ms | ~0.258ms | ~0.406ms | mixed collateral noise |
| `ipc.tab_order_active_ms` median | ~0.450ms | ~0.446ms | ~0.574ms | mixed collateral noise |
| `ipc.tab_close_active_ms` median | ~12.75ms | ~12.37ms | ~14.60ms | mixed/noisy |

Validation/safety: patched source built, slimmed/installed, and both patched benchmark runs used the safe benchmark/xenv launch path with `ulimit -c 0`. After the mixed result, reverted `src/browser_window.cc`, rebuilt/slimmed/installed the baseline binary, and ran `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS. Kept only the log/results. Disk stayed safe (`/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G).

Decision: do not keep. The copy avoidance is logically harmless but the affected URLs are short and the open path is dominated by state/JSON/chrome work; the repeated benchmark did not show a repeatable improvement.

## Experiment 100: use cached active tab id JSON in `tabs` header — FAILED/TOO SMALL

Hypothesis: after adding cached per-tab id JSON, `TabsJson()` still formatted the top-level `active_tabid` with `AppendJsonNumber(ActiveTabId())`. Reusing `tabs_[active_index_].id_json` for that one header field could remove one integer format per `tabs` response.

Change tested:
- In `TabsJson()`, append `tabs_[active_index_].id_json` for `active_tabid` when present, with the existing `AppendJsonNumber(ActiveTabId())` as a fallback.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 10,000 restored tabs and 40 direct IPC iterations. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-active-tabid-json-header-summary.jsonl` alongside the recent tab-id-cache validation runs.

Observations:
- The patched run's `ipc.tabs_ms` median was ~1.84ms, between existing recent id-cache samples (~1.65ms and ~1.89ms). Mean/p95 were also in normal noise range.
- This changes only one integer field per multi-megabyte response, while the remaining work is dominated by per-row serialization and socket transfer.
- The added active-index/id-json branch in the header is not justified by a measurable repeatable win.

Validation/safety: patched source built, runtime was slimmed/installed, and the focused run used the benchmark harness/xenv with browser core dumps disabled. Disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; keep the existing direct `AppendJsonNumber(ActiveTabId())` for clarity.

## Experiment 101: measure active `open-tab` creation in maintained IPC suite — SUCCESS/BENCHMARK

Hypothesis: the maintained IPC suite covered existing-tab `open`, tab focus, tab order, and close paths, but it did not track the separate active new-tab creation path (`open-tab`). Recent large-session lifecycle work changes insertion, state-save, sidebar, and browser-creation behavior; keeping a direct metric for `open-tab` makes future regressions and improvements easier to validate.

Change:
- Extend `run_ipc_suite()` with an `open_tab_active` phase at the end of the suite, after close metrics so it does not perturb existing measurements.
- Run up to `min(iterations, 40)` direct `open-tab <local-url>` commands.
- Record `ipc.open_tab_active_ms` and `ipc.open_tab_active_response_bytes`, plus the raw record under `records.open_tab_active`.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` then removed `scripts/__pycache__`.
- Smoke: `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 100 --ipc-iterations 5 --ipc-moved-tabs 2 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-open-tab-metric-smoke.json` produced the new `ipc.open_tab_active_ms` metric (n=5, median ~95ms in the small rendered-tab smoke).
- Focused: `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 20 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-open-tab-metric-ipc-1000.json` produced `ipc.open_tab_active_ms` n=20 median ~16.8ms / mean ~16.9ms / p95 ~20.2ms.
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS after rerunning from a clean runtime state.
- All browser launches used the safe benchmark/xenv path with `ulimit -c 0`; disk stayed safe (`/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G).

Decision: keep and commit as benchmark infrastructure. This adds repeatable coverage for active new-tab lifecycle performance without changing vimbrowser runtime behavior or the existing IPC metrics.

## Experiment 102: defer active open-tab browser creation — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: active `open-tab` in large virtual-sidebar sessions creates the new tab's CEF browser before returning the IPC status. Since active inserts already defer sidebar refresh/state-save/active-browser sync, skipping immediate `EnsureTabBrowser()` and letting the scheduled active-browser sync create the browser might reduce `ipc.open_tab_active_ms`.

Change tested:
- In `InsertTab()`, when `activate && tabs_.size() > kSidebarMaxRenderedRows && sidebar_spacer_`, skip the immediate `EnsureTabBrowser(insert_index, false)` call.
- Rely on the existing `ScheduleActiveBrowserSync()` path to run `ApplyActiveBrowserSelection()` shortly afterward, create/show the active browser, and request focus.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 40 direct iterations, and 20 shell iterations. One patched run vs a fresh baseline after reverting the patch. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-defer-active-open-tab-browser-summary.jsonl`; full outputs saved as `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-defer-active-open-tab-browser-{baseline,patched}-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

| metric | baseline | patched | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.open_tab_active_ms` median | ~19.36ms | ~17.39ms | faster in this pair |
| `ipc.open_tab_active_ms` mean | ~19.29ms | ~17.23ms | faster in this pair |
| `ipc.open_tab_active_ms` p95 | ~21.97ms | ~20.76ms | faster in this pair |
| `ipc.open_active_ms` median | ~2.61ms | ~1.99ms | faster/noisy collateral |
| `ipc.tab_close_active_ms` median | ~14.94ms | ~12.65ms | faster/noisy collateral |

The pair looked favorable, but the fresh baseline was substantially slower than the just-committed open-tab benchmark sample (~17.6-18.0ms median), and the patch changes visible behavior timing: the new active tab's browser creation is deferred until the delayed active-browser sync rather than started immediately. That can make the IPC response return before active-page creation begins, improving the metric by moving work after the measured command rather than reducing total user-visible work. The behavioral/scheduling risk is not justified without a benchmark that measures time to active-tab content visibility after `open-tab`.

Validation/safety: patched source built/slimmed/installed and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed. After reverting, source rebuilt/slimmed/installed and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed again. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; treat the existing `ipc.open_tab_active_ms` metric as command-return latency, not proof that deferring browser creation improves the full active-open user path.

## Experiment 103: reserve spare tab vector capacity after restore — FAILED

Hypothesis: restored sessions build `tabs_` to exactly the restored tab count. The new maintained `ipc.open_tab_active_ms` metric creates additional active tabs after restore, so the first few `open-tab` commands may pay `std::vector<Tab>` growth/copy costs. Reserving a small amount of spare capacity after loading the restored URL list should reduce active new-tab insertion cost with negligible memory overhead.

Change tested:
- After `BuildChrome()` and before the bulk restore insertion loop, call `tabs_.reserve(initial_urls_.size() + 64)` when the restored URL list is non-empty.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs and 40 direct iterations. Three baseline runs vs three patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-open-tab-capacity-summary.jsonl`; full outputs saved as `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-open-tab-capacity-{baseline,patched}-ipc-1000-*.{json,txt}`.

Observations:
- `ipc.open_tab_active_ms` median mean regressed: baseline ~17.55ms vs patched ~17.85ms.
- `ipc.open_tab_active_ms` mean also regressed slightly: baseline ~17.74ms vs patched ~17.80ms.
- Collateral `ipc.tabs_ms` and `ipc.status_ms` medians were worse in the patched runs, likely noise but no positive signal.
- Any vector growth cost is either already amortized by the implementation's capacity behavior or too small relative to browser creation/sidebar/state work.

Validation/safety: patched source built/slimmed/installed and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed. After reverting, source rebuilt/slimmed/installed and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed again. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; do not add spare `tabs_` vector capacity after restore.

## Experiment 104: rewrite IPC client transport in Perl — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after minimizing the Python `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` startup path (`/usr/bin/python3 -S`, lazy screenshot imports, string socket paths, streamed `os.write()` output), full shell IPC commands were still dominated by Python interpreter/module startup (~14ms for `status`/`tabs`). A small Perl transport wrapper using core modules should preserve command semantics while substantially reducing subprocess startup overhead.

Change:
- Replace the Python `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` wrapper with a Perl implementation.
- Normal commands resolve the same socket path precedence, send the newline-terminated command over the Unix socket, and stream response bytes to stdout with `syswrite()`.
- Screenshot handling preserves existing user-facing modes: passthrough `--json`, `-o/--output` metadata, TTY temp-file output, raw PNG stdout, and `ERR ` handling. Screenshot-only `JSON::PP`, `MIME::Base64`, and file helpers are loaded lazily.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 20 direct iterations, and 40 full shell-client iterations. One fresh Python-client baseline vs one Perl-client run. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-perl-ipc-client-summary.jsonl`; full outputs saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-perl-ipc-client-{baseline-,}ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

| metric | Python client | Perl client | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` median | ~13.86ms | ~8.34ms | ~39.9% faster |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` mean | ~14.03ms | ~8.42ms | ~40.0% faster |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` p95 | ~14.49ms | ~9.28ms | ~36.0% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median | ~14.02ms | ~8.51ms | ~39.3% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` mean | ~14.13ms | ~8.56ms | ~39.4% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` p95 | ~14.61ms | ~9.02ms | ~38.3% faster |

Direct IPC/browser lifecycle metrics were not the target and stayed within normal run noise. `make benchmark` also exercised the screenshot `--json` path successfully; local screenshot active latency was ~44.5ms in that validation run.

Validation:
- `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 20 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --ipc-shell-iterations 40 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output /tmp/perl-client-clean-patched-ipc-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a large repeatable shell-client latency win while preserving the Unix-socket protocol and existing screenshot convenience behavior.

## Experiment 105: split screenshot handling out of the hot IPC client path — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: the Perl IPC client still parsed/compiled screenshot decoding helpers on every normal shell command, even though `status`, `tabs`, and most other commands only need the Unix-socket transport. Moving screenshot-specific JSON/base64/file handling into a separate helper should shrink the normal startup path while preserving `vimbrowser-ipc screenshot ...` behavior.

Change:
- Keep `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` as a small normal-command Perl transport wrapper.
- If the first argument is `screenshot`, `exec` a sibling helper, `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc-screenshot`, with the original arguments.
- Move screenshot modes (`--json`, `-o/--output`, TTY temp-file output, raw PNG stdout, and `ERR ` handling) to the helper.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 20 direct iterations, and 40 full shell-client iterations. One fresh inline-screenshot Perl baseline vs one split-helper run. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-split-ipc-screenshot-summary.jsonl`; full outputs saved as `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-split-ipc-screenshot-{baseline,patched}-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

| metric | inline screenshot client | split helper client | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` median | ~8.21ms | ~7.68ms | ~6.5% faster |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` mean | ~8.35ms | ~7.74ms | ~7.3% faster |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` p95 | ~9.27ms | ~8.20ms | ~11.6% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median | ~8.49ms | ~8.33ms | ~1.9% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` mean | ~8.52ms | ~8.36ms | ~1.9% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` p95 | ~8.83ms | ~8.78ms | ~0.6% faster |

The win is concentrated in short shell `status`, where removing cold screenshot parsing matters most. Large `tabs` output remains dominated by subprocess startup plus response transfer, so the improvement is smaller but not regressed.

Validation:
- `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc-screenshot`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 20 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --ipc-shell-iterations 40 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output /tmp/split-screenshot-ipc-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS; this exercised the screenshot path via the helper (`local.screenshot.active_ms` ~47.7ms)
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. Normal shell IPC gets a smaller hot-path wrapper without dropping the screenshot convenience modes.

## Experiment 106: remove Perl warnings from IPC client hot path — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: after splitting screenshot handling out of `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`, normal shell IPC still pays Perl startup/pragma costs. Removing `use warnings;` from the small hot-path Perl wrapper might shave a little startup overhead for frequent shell `status`/`tabs` commands.

Change tested:
- Remove `use warnings;` from `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` while leaving `use strict;` and command behavior unchanged.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 20 direct iterations, and 50 full shell-client iterations. Three baseline runs with warnings vs four no-warnings runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-no-warnings-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- `ipc.shell_status_ms` median mean was only slightly lower: ~7.90ms baseline vs ~7.82ms without warnings.
- One no-warnings status run had a large tail/outlier; mean worsened overall (~7.97ms -> ~8.20ms) and p95 mean worsened (~8.53ms -> ~10.87ms).
- `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` medians were slightly lower (~8.24ms -> ~8.10ms), but the absolute signal is tiny and not enough to justify dropping default Perl warning checks from the maintained script.

Validation/safety: both variants passed `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; focused runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths with browser core dumps disabled. Disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; keep `use warnings;` for maintainability.

## Experiment 107: avoid `Socket` module import in hot IPC client — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after the Perl transport rewrite and split screenshot helper, normal `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` shell commands were still paying to load Perl's `Socket` module just to get `AF_UNIX`, `SOCK_STREAM`, and `sockaddr_un()`. On this Linux target, the hot wrapper can use Linux's stable Unix-domain socket constants and pack `sockaddr_un` directly, avoiding the module import while preserving the same Unix-socket protocol.

Change:
- Replace `use Socket qw(AF_UNIX SOCK_STREAM sockaddr_un);` in the hot `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` wrapper with local Linux constants (`AF_UNIX=1`, `SOCK_STREAM=1`).
- Add `sockaddr_un_linux()` to length-check paths and pack `struct sockaddr_un` as native `sa_family_t` plus 108-byte NUL-terminated path.
- Use `sockaddr_un_linux($path)` for the normal command `connect()` call.
- Leave `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc-screenshot` on the regular `Socket` module path; screenshot is not the hot shell-client path and benefits from standard helper behavior.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 10 direct IPC iterations, and 80 full shell-client iterations per run. Three baseline runs vs three patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-manual-sockaddr-summary.jsonl`; full outputs saved as `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-manual-sockaddr-{baseline,patched}-*-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

| metric | `Socket` module baseline | manual sockaddr client | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` median (mean of runs) | ~8.15ms | ~3.61ms | ~55.7% faster |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` mean (mean of runs) | ~8.24ms | ~3.78ms | ~54.2% faster |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` p95 (mean of runs) | ~9.18ms | ~4.69ms | ~48.9% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median (mean of runs) | ~8.52ms | ~4.08ms | ~52.1% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` mean (mean of runs) | ~8.61ms | ~4.20ms | ~51.2% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` p95 (mean of runs) | ~9.59ms | ~4.94ms | ~48.5% faster |

The signal is large and repeated across all A/B runs. Direct in-process IPC metrics were not the target; the improvement comes from reducing short-lived Perl client startup/module-load overhead while keeping response bytes and request format unchanged.

Validation:
- `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc-screenshot`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS; this also exercised screenshot via the helper (`local.screenshot.active_ms` ~46.9ms)
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, `/tmp` stayed ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a large repeatable shell-client startup win for the maintained Linux target, with the non-hot screenshot helper retaining the standard `Socket` path.

## Experiment 108: drop warnings after manual sockaddr IPC client — FAILED

Hypothesis: after removing the hot `Socket` import and reducing shell IPC startup to ~4ms, Perl's `warnings` pragma might become a more visible fraction of the remaining startup path. Removing `use warnings;` from the manual-sockaddr hot wrapper might now produce a measurable improvement.

Change tested:
- Remove `use warnings;` from the normal `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` wrapper after the manual `sockaddr_un` change. Keep `use strict;` and leave the screenshot helper unchanged.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 10 direct iterations, and 80 shell iterations. Compared one no-warnings run to the committed manual-sockaddr patched runs; summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-manual-sockaddr-no-warnings-summary.jsonl`, full no-warnings output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-manual-sockaddr-no-warnings-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

Observations:
- No useful win after the `Socket` import removal. The no-warnings run had `ipc.shell_status_ms` median ~4.05ms and `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median ~4.46ms, essentially the same/worse than the kept manual-sockaddr runs (`status` median mean ~3.61ms, `tabs` median mean ~4.08ms in the committed A/B summary).
- As before, removing warnings is not worth reducing diagnostics/maintainability.

Validation/safety: no-warnings variant passed `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; after reverting, `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` passed again. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; keep `use warnings;` in the hot wrapper.

## Experiment 109: inline Linux socket constants in IPC client — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after avoiding Perl's `Socket` module in the hot IPC wrapper, the script still loads `constant.pm` for two Linux socket constants. Since this wrapper is intentionally Linux-specific after the manual `sockaddr_un` optimization, using literal `1` values for `AF_UNIX` and `SOCK_STREAM` should remove another small module load from each shell IPC subprocess.

Change:
- Remove `use constant AF_UNIX => 1;` and `use constant SOCK_STREAM => 1;` from `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`.
- Pack the Linux Unix-domain socket family as literal `1` in `sockaddr_un_linux()`.
- Create the socket with literal domain/type `socket(my $sock, 1, 1, 0)`.
- Leave the screenshot helper unchanged on the standard `Socket` module path.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 10 direct iterations, and 80 shell iterations. Compared one literal-constant run to the committed manual-sockaddr patched runs; summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-literal-socket-summary.jsonl`, full literal-constant output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-literal-socket-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

| metric | manual sockaddr with `constant.pm` | literal constants | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` median | ~3.61ms mean of prior runs | ~3.38ms | ~6.3% faster |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` mean | ~3.78ms mean of prior runs | ~3.45ms | ~8.7% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median | ~4.08ms mean of prior runs | ~3.75ms | ~8.1% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` mean | ~4.20ms mean of prior runs | ~3.80ms | ~9.4% faster |

The effect is small in absolute terms but repeatably moves the short-lived client closer to its lower bound by avoiding one more Perl module load. Request/response protocol and screenshot behavior are unchanged.

Validation:
- `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc-screenshot`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 10 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --ipc-shell-iterations 80 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output /tmp/ipc-client-literal-socket-confirm-ipc-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS; screenshot helper path still exercised (`local.screenshot.active_ms` ~43.0ms)
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is a straightforward hot-wrapper startup win on the maintained Linux target.

## Experiment 110: inline IPC client path helpers — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: after the hot wrapper dropped `Socket` and `constant.pm`, it still defined small helper subs for socket path selection and `sockaddr_un` packing. Inlining the default socket path logic and direct `pack("S Z108", 1, $path)` call might reduce Perl parse/compile overhead for every shell IPC command.

Change tested:
- Remove `sockaddr_un_linux()` and inline the socket path length check plus `pack("S Z108", 1, $path)` at the `connect()` call.
- Inline the default socket-path precedence instead of calling a helper sub.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 10 direct iterations, and 100 shell iterations. Compared the patched run with the current literal-socket committed reference. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-inline-path-summary.jsonl`; full patched output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-inline-path-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

Observations:
- No useful win: shell `status` median was essentially unchanged (~3.38ms reference vs ~3.36ms patched), and shell `tabs` median was slightly worse/noisy (~3.75ms reference vs ~3.77ms patched).
- The helper subs do not appear to be the remaining bottleneck; inlining makes the script less readable without repeatable benefit.

Validation/safety: patched script passed `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; after reverting, both `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` and `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc-screenshot` passed, and `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` passed. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; keep the current small helper shape.

## Experiment 111: avoid Perl join for single-argument IPC commands — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: normal shell `status` and `tabs` invoke `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` with exactly one command argument. After the Perl/socket startup reductions, even small hot-wrapper work matters, so avoiding `join(" ", @ARGV)` for the one-argument case might shave a little from shell IPC latency without changing multi-argument command behavior.

Change:
- Build the request command as `$ARGV[0]` when `@ARGV == 1`.
- Keep the existing `join(" ", @ARGV)` path for multi-argument commands and default to `status` with no arguments.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 10 direct iterations, and 100 shell iterations. Compared against the current literal-socket reference. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-perl-single-arg-summary.jsonl`; full patched output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-perl-single-arg-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

| metric | join-all-args client | single-arg fast path | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` median | ~3.38ms | ~3.37ms | neutral/slightly faster |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` mean | ~3.45ms | ~3.42ms | ~1.0% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median | ~3.75ms | ~3.79ms in confirm, ~3.68ms in first run | mixed but mean lower than reference |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` mean | ~3.80ms | ~3.81ms in confirm, ~3.68ms in first run | neutral to small win |

The absolute gain is small but the follow-up alternating A/B check supported the direction: two baseline runs had shell `status` median mean ~3.38ms and shell `tabs` median mean ~3.78ms, while two patched runs had shell `status` median mean ~3.35ms and shell `tabs` median mean ~3.75ms. The code change is minimal and preserves multi-argument semantics, so keep as a tiny hot-wrapper simplification.

Validation:
- `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc-screenshot`
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 10 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --ipc-shell-iterations 100 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output /tmp/ipc-client-single-arg-perl-confirm-ipc-1000.json`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS; screenshot helper path still exercised (`local.screenshot.active_ms` ~44.9ms)
- Follow-up alternating A/B runs: `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-perl-single-arg-ab-{baseline,patched}-{1,2}.{json,txt}`.
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit as a very small repeatable shell-wrapper cleanup. Larger remaining wins are likely outside argument joining.

## Experiment 112: use buffered print for IPC client stdout — FAILED

Hypothesis: the Perl IPC client currently writes each received response chunk to stdout with an explicit `syswrite()` loop. For normal shell clients where stdout is a pipe, using Perl's buffered `print STDOUT $buffer` might reduce write-loop overhead, especially for large `tabs` responses.

Change tested:
- Replace the stdout `syswrite(STDOUT, ...)` loop in `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` with `print STDOUT $buffer` after each socket `sysread()`.
- Leave socket writes and protocol framing unchanged.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 10 direct iterations, and 100 shell iterations. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-print-stdout-summary.jsonl`; full patched output saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-print-stdout-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

Observations:
- No win. Compared with the current `syswrite` client, shell `status` was slightly worse/noisy (~3.37ms -> ~3.40ms median), and shell `tabs` was also slightly worse/noisy (~3.79ms -> ~3.78ms median but worse mean/tails in the print run).
- The explicit `syswrite` loop remains the clearer low-level transport behavior and avoids Perl stdio buffering ambiguity.

Validation/safety: patched script passed `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; after reverting, `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` passed. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed ~210G free on `/`, ~14G free on `/tmp`, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; retain explicit stdout `syswrite()`.

## Experiment 113: increase IPC client read buffer to 1MiB — FAILED

Hypothesis: after shell IPC startup was reduced to ~3-4ms, large `tabs` shell responses might be limited by multiple `sysread`/`syswrite` loop iterations. Increasing the normal `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` response read buffer from 256KiB to 1MiB could reduce loop overhead for large tab-list responses.

Change tested:
- Change the hot wrapper response read size from `sysread($sock, $buffer, 262144)` to `sysread($sock, $buffer, 1048576)`.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs. Alternating repeated runs compared three 256KiB baseline runs to four 1MiB runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-read-buffer-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- Shell `tabs` got worse with the larger buffer: baseline 256KiB medians were ~6.03-6.07ms, while 1MiB medians were ~6.23-6.34ms.
- Shell `status` was neutral/worse: baseline medians were ~3.41-3.45ms, while 1MiB medians were ~3.45-3.55ms.
- Direct `tabs` was mixed/noisy and unaffected by the shell buffer size, as expected.

Validation/safety: patched script passed `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; after reverting, both `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` and `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc-screenshot` passed. Focused runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths with browser core dumps disabled. Disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; keep the 256KiB read buffer.

## Experiment 114: measure active `open-tab` page completion in IPC suite — SUCCESS/BENCHMARK

Hypothesis: the maintained IPC suite measured `ipc.open_tab_active_ms` as command-return latency, but Experiment 102 showed that this is not enough to evaluate open-tab scheduling changes: deferring browser creation can make the command return faster by moving user-visible work after the measured interval. Add a bounded content-completion metric so future active `open-tab` experiments can distinguish command latency from time until the newly active page actually loads.

Change:
- Extend `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc` with a bounded `open_tab_active_complete` phase after the existing command-return `open_tab_active` phase.
- Run up to `min(iterations, 5)` direct `open-tab` commands against local benchmark pages.
- For each opened active tab, parse the status response's `active_tabid`, wait for that tab to reach complete via the existing JS/navigation probe, and record:
  - `ipc.open_tab_active_complete_ms` from command start to observed completion,
  - `ipc.open_tab_active_complete_command_ms`,
  - `ipc.open_tab_active_complete_load_event_ms`,
  - `ipc.open_tab_active_complete_fcp_ms`.
- Keep the sample count small so the optional IPC suite remains safe/quick and low-data.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark` and remove `scripts/__pycache__`.
- Smoke: `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 50 --ipc-iterations 3 --ipc-moved-tabs 2 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-open-tab-complete-smoke.json`.
  - New metric appeared with n=3, median complete ~142ms, command ~52ms, load-event ~86ms, FCP ~60ms.
  - An initial smoke used a non-`/page...` URL and measured Chrome error pages/timeouts; the URL was corrected to `/page-open-tab-active-complete-*.html` before keeping the result.
- Focused 1000-tab validation: `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc --ipc-tabs 1000 --ipc-iterations 10 --ipc-moved-tabs 4 --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-open-tab-complete-1000.json`.
  - `ipc.open_tab_active_complete_ms`: n=5, median ~102.5ms, mean ~113.2ms, p95 ~150.0ms.
  - `ipc.open_tab_active_complete_command_ms`: median ~8.5ms for the small post-warm complete phase, while the existing `ipc.open_tab_active_ms` command-return metric still reported n=10 median ~16.5ms earlier in the suite.
  - Local pages loaded successfully with responseStatus 200 and `window.bench` present.
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS.

Safety: all runs used the safe benchmark/xenv launch path with browser core dumps disabled. Disk stayed safe (`/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps 4.0G); result data remained small (~4MB total).

Decision: keep and commit as benchmark infrastructure. This closes the measurement gap identified by the failed active open-tab browser-deferral experiment and gives future lifecycle/backend work a direct user-visible active-open-tab completion signal.

## Experiment 115: exact simple-command fast path before IPC arg splitting — FAILED

Hypothesis: hot single-word IPC commands (`status`, `tabs`, `commands`, `version`, etc.) pay for `SplitArgs()` and lowercasing even when no argument parsing is required. Checking exact lowercase command lines at the top of `BrowserWindow::HandleIpcCommand()` could reduce direct IPC latency and maybe shell tails without changing fallback behavior for quoted/upper-case/trailing-space forms.

Change tested:
- Add exact `command_line == ...` checks before `SplitArgs()` for `version`/`protocol`, `status`/`json`, `tabs`, `commands`, `fps`, `refresh`, and `url`.
- Preserve the existing split/lowercase fallback for all other forms and commands.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 20 direct iterations, and 80 shell iterations. One patched run vs one restored baseline run with the same current benchmark. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-exact-simple-fastpath-summary.jsonl`; full outputs saved as `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-exact-simple-{baseline,fastpath}-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

Observations:
- No useful win. Direct `status` median was unchanged (~0.09ms) but mean/tails were worse in the patched run; direct `tabs` regressed (~0.27ms -> ~0.32ms median).
- Shell `status` and `tabs` were effectively neutral/noisy, with no tail improvement.
- `commands` looked better in the patched run, but that command is not a dominant maintained hot path and did not justify the added duplicated dispatch logic when `status`/`tabs` did not improve.

Validation/safety: patched C++ built successfully with `cmake --build build-source -j12`; patched run also passed `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source`. After restoring the source, the baseline build completed and source diff was clean. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; keep the single split/lowercase command dispatch.

## Experiment 116: drop `strict` from hot Perl IPC wrapper — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after the normal `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` path was reduced to a tiny Linux-specific Perl wrapper (manual `sockaddr_un`, literal socket constants, split screenshot helper, one-argument command fast path), Perl pragma startup overhead is a visible fraction of shell IPC latency. Removing `use strict;` from this hot wrapper while keeping lexical `my` declarations and `use warnings;` might improve shell-client startup without changing protocol behavior.

Change:
- Remove `use strict;` from `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` only.
- Keep `use warnings;` in the hot wrapper.
- Leave `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc-screenshot` unchanged with normal stricter helper behavior.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 10 direct iterations, and 100 shell-client iterations. Three strict baseline runs vs four no-strict runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-no-strict-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | strict baseline values | strict baseline mean/median | no-strict values | no-strict mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` median | 3.366, 3.411, 3.359 | 3.379 / 3.366 | 3.139, 3.134, 3.156, 3.316 | 3.186 / 3.147 | ~5.7% faster mean median |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` mean | 3.440, 3.577, 3.511 | 3.509 / 3.511 | 3.156, 3.151, 3.198, 3.469 | 3.243 / 3.177 | ~7.6% faster mean |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median | 3.717, 3.808, 3.708 | 3.744 / 3.717 | 3.527, 3.522, 3.548, 3.646 | 3.561 / 3.538 | ~4.9% faster mean median |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` mean | 3.732, 3.965, 3.858 | 3.852 / 3.858 | 3.572, 3.561, 3.566, 3.753 | 3.613 / 3.569 | ~6.2% faster mean |

Validation:
- `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS; screenshot helper path still exercised (`local.screenshot.active_ms` ~46.4ms)
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths with browser core dumps disabled.
- Disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. The hot wrapper is now intentionally tiny/Linux-specific, continues to use lexical variables and warnings, and the stricter/less-hot screenshot helper remains separate.

## Experiment 117: fast path audible refresh for single active client — FAILED

Hypothesis: `RefreshAudibleTabs()` scans `tabs_` until it has seen `tab_client_count_` live clients. In a large lazy-restored session with only the active tab materialized, that can still scan across many restored tab records if the active tab is near the end. A single-active-client fast path might reduce `status`/`tabs` IPC overhead by checking `tabs_[active_index_]` directly when `tab_client_count_ == 1`.

Change tested:
- In `BrowserWindow::RefreshAudibleTabs()`, return immediately for zero clients.
- When there is exactly one client and the active tab owns a client, refresh only that tab's audible state and skip the full tab scan.
- Preserve the existing scan fallback for all other layouts.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 30 direct iterations, and 80 shell iterations. One patched run vs one restored baseline run. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-refresh-audible-single-client-summary.jsonl`; full outputs saved as `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-refresh-audible-single-client-{baseline,patched}-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

Observations:
- No useful win. Direct `status` was worse/noisier in the patched run (~0.085ms -> ~0.13ms median, mean ~0.10ms -> ~0.30ms), and direct `tabs` was neutral/slightly worse.
- Shell `status`/`tabs` were essentially neutral, with no repeatable tail improvement.
- The extra branchiness is not justified; the scan is not the current bottleneck in the maintained large-session IPC path.

Validation/safety: patched C++ built successfully with `cmake --build build-source -j12` and passed `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source`. After reverting, the baseline build completed and source diff was clean. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `src/browser_window.cc`; keep the existing `RefreshAudibleTabs()` scan.

## Experiment 118: per-site live benchmark metrics — SUCCESS/BENCHMARK

Hypothesis: the live real-site suite already collected useful readiness and smoothness rows, but only emitted aggregate `live.*` metrics across all requested URLs. That makes it awkward to compare repeatable per-site behavior (for example GitHub rAF/long-task smoothness vs Hacker News smoothness) across future backend/Chromium experiments. Emitting stable per-site metric keys should make real-site A/B analysis easier without changing browser behavior.

Change:
- Add a sanitized live-site label derived from URL host plus the first path segment, prefixed by request order for uniqueness.
- Store the label in each live page record as `site_label`.
- Emit per-site metrics under `live.site.<label>.*` for open command time, observed ready time, load event, FCP, rAF smoothness, slow-frame counts, and long-task totals, while keeping the existing aggregate `live.*` metrics.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark` and removed `scripts/__pycache__`.
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite live --live-url https://github.com/ --live-url https://news.ycombinator.com/ --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-live-per-site-metrics-smoke.json`.
  - Smoke output included per-site keys such as `live.site.01_github_com.smoothness.raf_p95_ms` and `live.site.02_news_ycombinator_com.smoothness.longtask_count`.
  - GitHub: ready ~1599ms, rAF p95 ~33.4ms, 4 long tasks totaling ~380ms.
  - Hacker News: ready ~475ms, rAF p95 ~16.8ms, 0 long tasks.
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS.

Safety: runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths with browser core dumps disabled. Disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G, and result data remained small (~4.3MB total).

Decision: keep and commit as benchmark infrastructure. This turns the live suite into a more useful repeatable real-site comparison harness for future backend/runtime experiments.

## Experiment 119: drop remaining warnings pragma from hot IPC wrapper — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after Experiment 116 removed `strict` from the tiny hot Perl IPC wrapper, the remaining `use warnings;` pragma may dominate shell-client startup. Removing it from `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` while keeping the stricter screenshot helper separate could materially reduce shell IPC latency. The normal wrapper is now intentionally small, lexical, Linux-specific, and covered by `perl -c` plus benchmark validation.

Change:
- Remove `use warnings;` from `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`.
- Leave `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc-screenshot` unchanged with strict/warnings and normal module-based screenshot handling.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 5 direct iterations, and 100 shell-client iterations. Three no-strict+warnings baseline runs vs three no-pragmas patched runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-no-pragmas-summary.jsonl`; full outputs saved as `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-no-pragmas-{baseline,patched}-{1,2,3}.{json,txt}`.

| metric | no-strict + warnings | no pragmas | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` median mean | ~3.145ms | ~1.681ms | ~46.5% faster |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` mean mean | ~3.169ms | ~1.712ms | ~46.0% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median mean | ~3.572ms | ~2.045ms | ~42.8% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` mean mean | ~3.609ms | ~2.056ms | ~43.0% faster |

Direct in-process IPC metrics were noisier/worse in these separate browser sessions, as expected for a client-wrapper-only change; the maintained shell metrics show the actual effect. The screenshot helper still carries normal diagnostics, and the hot wrapper remains simple enough for syntax/benchmark coverage.

Validation:
- `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc-screenshot`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS; screenshot helper path still exercised (`local.screenshot.active_ms` ~46.6ms)
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths with browser core dumps disabled.
- Disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. This is the largest remaining shell IPC startup win in the wrapper, reducing normal shell `status` to ~1.7ms median and shell `tabs` to ~2.0ms median on the maintained 1000-tab suite.

## Experiment 120: measure active `tab-focus` no-op in IPC suite — SUCCESS/BENCHMARK

Hypothesis: several activation experiments optimize real tab switches, but external controllers can also issue `tab-focus <active-tabid>` when the requested tab is already active. The maintained IPC suite should distinguish this lower-bound no-op focus path from nearby two-tab switching so future sidebar/activation changes do not accidentally make the already-active case expensive.

Change:
- Extend `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite ipc` with `ipc.tab_focus_active_noop_ms` and `ipc.tab_focus_active_noop_response_bytes`.
- Warm the active tab with one direct `tab-focus`, wait 300ms for any existing delayed virtual-sidebar refresh to drain, then measure repeated direct-socket `tab-focus <active_tabid>` calls.
- Keep the existing `ipc.tab_focus_nearby_ms` alternating-switch metric unchanged.

Validation/results:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark` and removed `scripts/__pycache__`.
- Three current-build IPC runs with 1000 restored tabs, 60 direct iterations, and 4 moved tabs: `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tab-focus-noop-baseline-{1,2,3}.{json,txt}`.
- New metric appeared and showed the current no-op path is already near direct-IPC lower bound: median values 0.046, 0.044, 0.048ms (mean/median of medians ~0.046/0.046ms), compared with nearby real switches around 0.800-0.947ms median.

Safety: all benchmark runs used `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark`, which launches vimbrowser under `ulimit -c 0` in xenv. Disk stayed safe (`/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps 4.0G; results grew only to ~5.0M).

Decision: keep as benchmark infrastructure. It documents that active focus no-op is already extremely cheap and gives future activation/sidebar experiments a maintained guardrail separate from real tab switches.

## Experiment 121: skip sidebar refresh for active `tab-focus` no-op — FAILED

Hypothesis: `ActivateTab()` still refreshed/scheduled the sidebar when asked to activate the already-active tab. Removing that work might reduce `tab-focus <active-tabid>` no-op latency and avoid unnecessary delayed virtual-sidebar refreshes in large sessions.

Change tested in `src/browser_window.cc`:
- In the `active_index_ == index` branch of `ActivateTab()`, skip the small-session row refresh and large-session delayed sidebar refresh.
- Continue scheduling active browser sync when the active view is not visible or has not been created.

Focused benchmark: using the new maintained no-op metric, 1000 restored tabs, 60 direct iterations, 4 moved tabs. Three current baseline runs vs three patched runs. Full patched outputs saved as `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tab-focus-noop-patched-{1,2,3}.{json,txt}`; combined summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-tab-focus-active-noop-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | baseline median values | baseline mean/median | patched median values | patched mean/median | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.tab_focus_active_noop_ms` | 0.046, 0.044, 0.048 | 0.046 / 0.046 | 0.044, 0.048, 0.063 | 0.052 / 0.048 | no win / slightly worse |
| `ipc.tab_focus_nearby_ms` | 0.862, 0.947, 0.800 | 0.870 / 0.862 | 0.835, 0.854, 0.844 | 0.844 / 0.844 | tiny/noisy |
| `ipc.open_tab_active_complete_ms` | 100.5, 96.1, 98.2 | 98.3 / 98.2 | 99.3, 98.9, 95.6 | 97.9 / 98.9 | neutral |

Observations:
- The no-op `tab-focus` path is already around 0.05ms, dominated by direct IPC dispatch/status JSON lower-bound rather than sidebar work.
- Removing the refresh was not repeatably faster and slightly worsened the new no-op median aggregate.
- The tiny nearby-switch movement is unrelated/noisy and does not justify removing a conservative sidebar consistency refresh.

Validation/safety: patched source built successfully (`cmake --build build-source -j12`, slim runtime, install wrapper) and benchmarked through the safe xenv harness. After the result, reverted `src/browser_window.cc` and rebuilt/restored the current committed browser binary. Disk stayed safe (`/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G; result data ~5.0M).

Decision: do not keep. Reverted the runtime change; retain the new benchmark metric as the useful outcome.

## Experiment 122: inline tiny hot IPC wrapper helpers — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after removing Perl pragmas from the normal shell IPC wrapper, the remaining subroutine calls for `sockaddr_un_linux()` and `default_socket_path()` are a measurable fraction of the ~2ms shell-client path. Inlining those tiny helpers in `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` could reduce startup/dispatch overhead without changing the socket protocol or screenshot behavior.

Change:
- Inline default socket-path selection into the hot wrapper body.
- Inline the Unix socket path length check and `pack("S Z108", 1, $path)` call at `connect()`.
- Leave screenshot handling delegated to `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc-screenshot`.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 5 direct iterations, and 100 shell-client iterations. Three current helper-subroutine baseline runs vs four inlined-helper runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-inline-subs-summary.jsonl`.

| metric | helper baseline | inlined helpers | result |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` median mean | ~1.694ms | ~1.624ms | ~4.1% faster |
| `ipc.shell_status_ms` mean mean | ~1.742ms | ~1.665ms | ~4.4% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` median mean | ~2.038ms | ~1.991ms | ~2.3% faster |
| `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` mean mean | ~2.100ms | ~2.039ms | ~2.9% faster |

The signal is smaller than the pragma/module-load wins but consistent in the targeted shell-client metrics. Direct in-process IPC is not expected to change.

Validation:
- `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS; screenshot helper path still exercised (`local.screenshot.active_ms` ~43.4ms)
- Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths with browser core dumps disabled.
- Disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: keep and commit. The hot wrapper remains a tiny, intentionally Linux-specific fast path, while the screenshot helper retains the fuller code path.

## Experiment 123: single syswrite for tiny IPC request — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: normal shell IPC requests are tiny (`status`, `tabs`, etc.), so the hot wrapper's robust request write loop may be unnecessary overhead. Replacing it with one `syswrite($sock, "$command\n")` could reduce client latency while keeping the response write loop intact for large replies.

Change tested:
- Replace the request write loop in `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` with a single `syswrite` of the command plus newline.
- Keep the robust stdout response write loop unchanged.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 5 direct iterations, and 100 shell-client iterations. Three current write-loop baseline runs vs four single-write runs. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-single-write-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- `ipc.shell_status_ms` mean improved slightly (~1.678ms -> ~1.662ms) and p95 improved (~1.893ms -> ~1.851ms), but median was slightly worse (~1.631ms -> ~1.643ms).
- `ipc.shell_tabs_ms` was neutral/slightly worse overall: median mean ~2.016ms -> ~2.022ms and mean ~2.028ms -> ~2.036ms.
- The effect is too small/mixed to justify weakening the fully robust request write path.

Validation/safety: patched script passed `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; focused runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths with browser core dumps disabled. Disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; keep the robust request write loop.

## Experiment 124: use bareword socket filehandle in hot IPC wrapper — FAILED

Hypothesis: after removing Perl pragmas and inlining tiny helpers, the hot wrapper still creates a lexical socket filehandle with `socket(my $sock, ...)`. With `strict` disabled, using a bareword filehandle (`S`) might avoid a little lexical/scalar setup and reduce shell IPC startup further.

Change tested:
- Replace `socket(my $sock, 1, 1, 0)`, `connect($sock, ...)`, `syswrite($sock, ...)`, and `sysread($sock, ...)` with the bareword socket filehandle `S`.
- Keep the protocol, request write, response buffer, stdout write loop, and screenshot helper unchanged.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 5 direct iterations, and 100 shell-client iterations. Compared four bareword-filehandle runs with the kept inline-helper/no-pragmas baseline rows. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-bareword-fh-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- No shell-client win. Baseline inline-helper shell `status` median mean was ~1.624ms vs ~1.634ms for the bareword variant; shell `tabs` median mean was ~1.991ms vs ~2.022ms for bareword.
- Direct in-process `tabs` was noisy/better in the bareword run, but this change only affects the shell client, and the maintained shell metrics are the relevant signal.
- The style is less maintainable and not faster on the hot shell path.

Validation/safety: bareword variant passed `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; after reverting, `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` passed again. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths with browser core dumps disabled. Disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; retain the lexical socket filehandle.

## Experiment 125: report live resource timing metrics — SUCCESS/BENCHMARK

Hypothesis: the live real-site suite had per-site readiness, rAF smoothness, and long-task metrics, but it still lacked the network/resource-count signal called out by the earlier live-benchmark notes. Adding Resource Timing summaries to the existing smoothness probe should make future backend/content-blocking experiments easier to compare per site without changing browser behavior.

Change:
- Extend the live smoothness readout to summarize `performance.getEntriesByType('resource')`.
- Record per-page resource count, transfer/encoded/decoded byte totals, total/max resource duration, slow-resource count over 500ms, and initiator-type counts in the page record.
- Emit aggregate `live.resources.*` metrics and per-site `live.site.<label>.resources.*` metrics, and include resource count in the human-readable live page line.

Validation:
- `python3 -m py_compile scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark` and removed `scripts/__pycache__`.
- `scripts/vimbrowser-benchmark --suite live --live-url https://github.com/ --live-url https://news.ycombinator.com/ --binary build-source/Release/vimbrowser --output performance-research/results/2026-05-22-live-resource-metrics-smoke.json`.
  - Smoke output included aggregate keys such as `live.resources.count` and per-site keys such as `live.site.01_github_com.resources.transfer_size_bytes`.
  - GitHub reported 148 resource entries, ~3.8MB transfer, 5 long tasks, and rAF p95 ~33.3ms.
  - Hacker News reported 6 resource entries, ~5.8KB transfer, 0 long tasks, and rAF p95 ~16.7ms.
- `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source` => CHECK: PASS.

Safety: runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths with browser core dumps disabled. Disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G, and result data stayed small (~5.1MB total).

Decision: keep and commit as benchmark infrastructure. This does not alter runtime behavior; it adds the missing real-site resource/request dimensions needed to evaluate future backend and content-blocking changes with per-site context.

## Experiment 126: single stdout write for small IPC responses — FAILED

Hypothesis: the hot IPC wrapper still uses the generic stdout `syswrite()` loop even for tiny responses such as `status`. Adding a small-response fast path that performs one `syswrite(STDOUT, $buffer)` for chunks under 8KiB might reduce shell `status` latency while preserving the loop for large `tabs` responses.

Change tested:
- After each socket `sysread()`, if the chunk is under 8192 bytes, write it to stdout with one `syswrite(STDOUT, $buffer)` and skip the offset loop.
- Keep the existing offset loop for larger chunks.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 5 direct iterations, and 100 shell-client iterations. Compared four small-response-fast-path runs with the kept inline-helper/no-pragmas baseline rows. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-ipc-client-small-stdout-summary.jsonl`.

Observations:
- No win. Shell `status` median mean worsened from ~1.624ms baseline to ~1.788ms with the small-response fast path, and tails were much noisier.
- Shell `tabs` also regressed slightly/noisily (~1.991ms -> ~2.087ms median mean) even though the large-response path still used the loop.
- The extra branch is not useful; the existing loop usually executes once for small responses and remains simple/consistent.

Validation/safety: patched script passed `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; after reverting, `perl -c scripts/vimbrowser-ipc` passed again. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths with browser core dumps disabled. Disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted `scripts/vimbrowser-ipc`; retain the existing stdout write loop.

## Experiment 127: cache tab title JSON from title-change notifications — FAILED/MIXED

Hypothesis: `IpcStatusJson()` and `AppendTabJson()` still query `CefNavigationEntry::GetTitle()` and JSON-escape titles on demand. Caching tab titles and their JSON-escaped form from CEF title-change notifications could reduce direct and shell `status`/`tabs` latency, especially once the shell wrapper is no longer dominant.

Change tested:
- Add `Tab::title` and `Tab::title_json`.
- Handle `BrowserClient::OnTitleChange()` and forward title updates to `BrowserWindow::OnClientTitleChange()`.
- Clear cached title on non-blank load start.
- Use cached title/title JSON in `IpcStatusJson()`, `AppendTabJson()`, and `ActiveTabTitle()` when available, falling back to `CefNavigationEntry` lookup otherwise.

Focused benchmark: maintained IPC suite with 1000 restored tabs, 30 direct iterations, and 80 shell iterations. One patched run vs one restored baseline run. Summary saved in `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-title-cache-summary.jsonl`; full outputs saved as `performance-research/results/2026-05-22-title-cache-{baseline,patched}-ipc-1000.{json,txt}`.

Observations:
- No repeatable hot-path win. Direct `status` stayed around ~0.07-0.08ms median with noise, direct `tabs` was roughly neutral (~0.29ms baseline vs ~0.30ms patched), and shell `status`/`tabs` moved only within noise.
- The patched run changed some status/tab response sizes because titles were populated earlier in a few active-tab cases; that is a behavioral/reporting change, not just a performance optimization.
- The extra fields and title-change plumbing are not justified by the measured maintained IPC path.

Validation/safety: patched C++ built successfully with `cmake --build build-source -j12` and passed `make benchmark BUILD_DIR=build-source`. After reverting, the baseline build completed and source diff was clean. Runs used benchmark/xenv-safe browser launch paths; disk stayed `/` ~210G free, `/tmp` ~14G free, coredumps unchanged at 4.0G.

Decision: do not keep. Reverted the title cache/title-change plumbing.

## Experiment 128: compiled C IPC client for shell commands — SUCCESS

Hypothesis: after squeezing the Perl wrapper down to ~1.6ms shell `status` and ~2.0ms shell `tabs`, process startup/interpreter overhead is the dominant remaining shell IPC cost.
